#+LATEX_HEADER: \author{Jan Snellman \orcidlink{0009-0002-6676-5068}\thanks{Department of Mathematics, Linköping University, 581 83 Linköping, Sweden. \texttt{jan.snellman@liu.se}.}}

#+bibliography: ../../references/exterior-convex-references.bib
#+cite_export: biblatex alphabetic

#+title: Hilbert functions over the exterior algebra and \(f\)-vectors in higher rank
#+subtitle: III: Lattice points of the Kozlov polytope
#+author: Jan Snellman
#+date: \today

#+begin_abstract
Let \(E\) be the exterior algebra on \(n\) generators and \(F=\bigoplus_{i=1}^rEg_i\) a graded free
\(E\)-module with \(\deg g_i=d_i\). Part I of this series identified the convex hull of the Hilbert
functions of the quotients \(F/M\), \(M\) a proper monomial submodule, as the Kozlov polytope
\(\Koz(n;\vek d)\), a Minkowski sum of shifted Kozlov simplices, and described its vertices at
rank two, conjecturing its facets there; Part II counted the Hilbert functions. Here we study the
lattice points of the polytope.

We show that
\(\Koz(n)\) is a lecture hall simplex and that every point of \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) has a unique
chained decomposition into points of \(\Koz(n)\). Hence the lattice points of \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) are
exactly the sums of shifted lattice points of \(\Koz(n)\), the polytope has the integer
decomposition property, every lattice point is a Hilbert function when \(n\le3\), and lattice-point
counts and Ehrhart polynomials are products of transfer matrices of the kind used in Part II. We
determine the codegree of every Kozlov polytope and show that none is Gorenstein for \(n\ge3\).
Finally we compare discrete and continuous invariants: the number of Hilbert functions against the
volume and the lattice-point count, where we prove Kupreyeva's observed limit for the fraction of
lattice points that are \(f\)-vectors and extend it to every rank. We also compare the centroid and
second moments of the Hilbert functions with those of the polytope.
#+end_abstract

#+begin_export latex
\medskip\noindent\textbf{Keywords.} exterior algebra; Hilbert function; \(f\)-vector; Kozlov
polytope; Minkowski sum; log-concavity; lecture hall
polytope; Ehrhart polynomial; Gorenstein polytope; Kruskal--Katona theorem.

\smallskip\noindent\textbf{2020 Mathematics Subject Classification.} Primary 05E40, 52B20;
Secondary 13F55, 05A15, 13D40.
\medskip
#+end_export

#+TOC: headlines 2

* Introduction
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: sec-intro
:END:

** Where Parts I and II leave things

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 1: "for F as above" in sec-setup stretched "above" to the introduction):
# F displayed and numbered, eq:F; sec-setup and the notation table cite it.
Let \(k\) be a field, \(E\) the exterior algebra on \(e_1,\dots,e_n\), and \(F\) the graded free
\(E\)-module
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:F}
  F=\bigoplus_{i=1}^rEg_i,\qquad \deg g_i=d_i,\qquad 0=d_1\le d_2\le\dots\le d_r .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
A monomial
submodule \(M\subseteq F\) is a direct sum \(I_1g_1\oplus\dots\oplus I_rg_r\) of monomial ideals,
and the Hilbert function of \(F/M\) is a sum of shifted \(f\)-vectors of simplicial complexes. If
\(S_n\) denotes the set of \(f\)-vectors \((1,n,f_2,\dots,f_n)\) of the complexes on \([n]\) that
use every vertex, the achievable Hilbert functions of quotients by proper monomial submodules form
the sumset
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ff}
  \FF(n;\vek d)\;=\;S_n+\shift^{d_2}(S_n)+\dots+\shift^{d_r}(S_n)\;\subseteq\;\mathbb Z^{N+1},
  \qquad N=n+d_r ,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
whose elements we call /ff/-vectors; here \(\shift^d\) prepends \(d\) zeros \eqref{eq:shift}. In Part II this set is written \(\FF_{\mathrm{pr}}(n;\vek d)\),
and \(\FF(n;\vek d)\) denotes there the larger sumset of \(f\)-vectors of all complexes on \([n]\),
the void complex included. In this paper \(\FF\) always means the proper set. Part I
[cite:@SnellmanArticle1] proved that its convex hull is the *Kozlov polytope*
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:koz}
  \Koz(n;\vek d)\;=\;\sum_{i=1}^r\shift^{d_i}\bigl(\Koz(n)\bigr),
\end{equation}
#+end_export
a Minkowski sum of shifted copies of the Kozlov simplex \(\Koz(n)=\conv(S_n)\)
[cite:@SnellmanArticle1 Theorem 39], and determined its vertices and, conjecturally, its facets
at rank two [cite:@SnellmanArticle1 Section 9]. Part II [cite:@SnellmanArticle2] counted
\(\FF(n;\vek d)\) by a product of transfer matrices, one per gap \(d_i-d_{i-1}\). Part I left the
vertices at every rank to this paper [cite:@SnellmanArticle1 Section 9.3]; they, and the facets at
every rank, are instead determined in a forthcoming manuscript [cite:@SnellmanArticle4], which also
proves Part I's two facet conjectures [cite:@SnellmanArticle1 Conjectures 52 and 55].

For example, for \(n=3\) and \(\vek d=(0,1)\) the ff-vectors are the \(22\) vectors
\((1,4,f_2,f_3,f_4)\) obtained by adding the \(f\)-vector of a graph on \([3]\), or of the full
triangle, to a shifted one; their convex hull \(\Koz(3;0,1)\) is a \(3\)-polytope with \(7\) vertices and
\(6\) facets (Figure \ref{fig:koz3-d01}), and every one of its lattice points is an ff-vector.

The question that remains, and the subject of this paper, is that of the lattice points.
A hull is coarse. Already for \(n=4\) the simplex \(\Koz(4)\) contains twenty lattice points of
which only sixteen are \(f\)-vectors, and the proportion falls quickly with \(n\). Which lattice
points are Hilbert functions, and how are they distributed?

** What this paper does

The answers, in the order of the paper. Section [[#sec-lattice]] is about the lattice points
themselves; Sections [[#sec-volume]] to [[#sec-moments]] compare discrete and continuous invariants,
one comparison to a section.

- *Lattice points* (Section [[#sec-lattice]]). \(\Koz(n)\) is, up to a unimodular map, the
  \(\vek s\)-lecture hall simplex with \(\vek s=(\binom n2,\dots,\binom nn)\)
  (Proposition [[prop-lecture-hall]]). Every point of \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) has a unique /chained/
  decomposition into points of \(\Koz(n)\), integral when the point is (Theorem [[thm-chained]]).
  Consequences: lattice points add, the polytope has the integer decomposition property, every
  lattice point is an ff-vector when \(n\le3\), and an effective membership test.
- *Volume and lattice-point count* (Section [[#sec-volume]]). The normalised volume of
  \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\), from a tiling by products of simplices (Theorem [[thm-staircase]]), and the
  number of Hilbert functions against the number of lattice points.
- *Kupreyeva's limit* (Section [[#sec-kupreyeva]]). As the number of vertices grows, the fraction of
  lattice points that are \(f\)-vectors tends to a limit that Kupreyeva observed; we prove it,
  explain it as a volume ratio (Theorem [[thm-kupreyeva-limit]]), and extend it to every rank
  (Theorem [[thm-kupreyeva-rank-r]]).
- *Ehrhart theory* (Section [[#sec-ehrhart]]). Ehrhart polynomials by transfer matrices; their
  linear and quadratic coefficients at every rank, read off ranks one and two; the codegree of
  every Kozlov polytope, and the fact that none with \(n\ge3\) is Gorenstein
  (Theorem [[thm-codegree]], Corollary [[cor-gorenstein]]).
- *Centroids* (Section [[#sec-centroid]]). The centroid of the Hilbert functions against that of
  the polytope: exact symmetries at rank one, and at equal shifts the convergence of the former to
  the latter after scaling (Theorem [[thm-centroid-dilation]]), with the whole uniform measure
  (Remark [[rem-weak-convergence]]).
- *Second moments* (Section [[#sec-moments]]). The Hilbert functions are more spread than the
  polytope for small \(n\), and concentrate for large \(n\) (Observation [[obs-concentration]]).

Which lattice points are Hilbert functions is answered here through the canonical decomposition
(Corollary [[cor-membership]]). There is also an intrinsic answer, a growth condition on the
sequence itself, valid for all graded submodules of \(F\) and not only for proper monomial ones:
the module version of the Kruskal--Katona theorem due to Amata and Crupi
[cite:@AmataCrupi2020KK Theorem 4.2]. A new proof of it, with a complete proof of the step in which
the free generators of largest degree are filled first, is the subject of a separate paper
[cite:@SnellmanArticle5].

** What is proved, and what is only observed

The distinction is kept explicit throughout, as in Parts I and II. *Proved* means proved in this
paper, or proved modulo a precise citation. *Verified* means checked by exact computation, along
a route independent of the formula being tested, over the range stated; such statements appear as
Observations or Conjectures and are not claimed to be proved. Section [[#sec-methods]] says what
was computed, how, and where the code is.

Vectors are set in bold, as in Part II [cite:@SnellmanArticle2] (Part I marks them with an arrow):

always fixed by the context. One exception is deliberate: a point of the polytope under study, of
\(\Koz(n;\vek d)\), \(\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\) or a dilate, is written \(x\), not in bold, with
coordinates \(x_k\), while the parts of its decompositions are bold, \(\vek p_i\).

# 2026-11-13 (Jan, sweep item 2: "the 17" inline definitions displayed and numbered): eq:gaps,
# eq:shift, eq:kk-cond, eq:Ftilde, eq:mu, eq:chained, eq:NV, eq:kup-sets, Delta_n
# (in eq:Rn), D_j and m_j (in eq:aj), eq:T0 (for the matrix N), eq:ar, eq:centroid, eq:Lambda, B_k (in
# eq:Sigmak), eq:gk and eq:classcounts. Inline restatements now cite them. The notation table points
# to them. Letter clashes renamed at the same time: kappa_n -> theta_n (proof of
# thm-centroid-dilation), K and Lambda_h -> Omega and Gamma_h (lem-anisotropic-count), D(t) ->
# omega(t) (lem-kup-dominant).
Throughout, \(n\) and the *shift vector* \(\vek d=(d_1,\dots,d_r)\), the degrees of the generators of
the free module \(F\) of \eqref{eq:F}, are fixed when nothing else is said. The shift vector is always sorted,
\(0=d_1\le d_2\le\dots\le d_r\); its entries are the *shifts*, \(r\) is its length, and its *gaps*
are
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gaps}
  \delta_i=d_i-d_{i-1},\qquad i=2,\dots,r .
\end{equation}
#+end_export

Two lattice polytopes are *unimodularly equivalent* if an affine bijection between their affine hulls
maps the one onto the other and the lattice points of the one affine hull onto those of the other.
Such a map preserves the normalised volume, the lattice points of every dilate, and so the Ehrhart
polynomial.

A few letters are reused locally with a different meaning (for instance \(u\) and \(T\) in
Appendix [[#sec-app-chained]]); each such use is introduced where it occurs and does not leave its
section.

** Hilbert functions and ff-vectors

We use the notation of Parts I and II. \(E=\bigwedge(k^n)\) is graded with
\(\dim_kE_j=\binom nj\). A simplicial complex \(\Delta\) on \([n]\) (an order ideal of the Boolean
lattice) has \(f\)-vector \((f_0,\dots,f_n)\), with \(f_k\) the number of faces of cardinality
\(k\). The /void/ complex, with no faces at all, has \(f_0=0\); every other complex is /non-void/
and has \(f_0=1\). The exterior face ideal
\(I_\Delta=(e_\sigma:\sigma\notin\Delta)\) satisfies \(\dim_k(E/I_\Delta)_k=f_k(\Delta)\). The
complex is /proper/ if it uses all \(n\) vertices, that is \(f_1=n\)
[cite:@SnellmanArticle1 Definition 10]. We write \(S_n\) for the set of \(f\)-vectors of proper
complexes, so \(\lvert S_n\rvert=1,2,5,16,70,457,4908\) for \(n=1,\dots,7\), and \(\mathcal L_n\)
for the set of lattice points of \(\Koz(n)\). For an integer \(d\ge0\) the *shift* prepends \(d\)
zeros,
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:shift}
  \shift^d(x_0,\dots,x_m)=(\underbrace{0,\dots,0}_{d},x_0,\dots,x_m),
\end{equation}
#+end_export
and for \(F\) as in \eqref{eq:F}, \(\FF(n;\vek d)\) is the sumset \eqref{eq:ff} of the introduction
[cite:@SnellmanArticle1 Proposition 35]. We call its elements ff-vectors, or achievable Hilbert
functions, or, where no confusion is possible, Hilbert functions; a lattice point of
\(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) that is not an ff-vector is /non-achievable/.

# 2026-11-09: the pseudo-power was defined in def-B, which moved to Part V with sec-module-kk; it is
# used in ex-koz3-koz4, in sec-kupreyeva and in Appendix sec-app-kupreyeva, so it is defined here.
# 2026-11-10 (Jan, round 5): the Macaulay representation displayed and numbered.
The set \(S_n\) is described by the Kruskal--Katona theorem [cite:@Kruskal1963;@Katona1968]. For
integers \(k\ge1\) and \(a\ge1\), the /\(k\)-th Macaulay representation/ of \(a\) is the unique
expression
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:macaulay}
  a=\binom{a_k}k+\binom{a_{k-1}}{k-1}+\dots+\binom{a_s}s,\qquad a_k>a_{k-1}>\dots>a_s\ge s\ge1,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
and the /\(k\)-th pseudo-power/ of \(a\) is
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kk}
  a^{(k)}=\binom{a_k}{k+1}+\binom{a_{k-1}}{k}+\dots+\binom{a_s}{s+1},\qquad 0^{(k)}=0 .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
A vector \((1,n,f_2,\dots,f_n)\) of non-negative integers lies in \(S_n\) if and only if
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kk-cond}
  f_{k+1}\le f_k^{(k)},\qquad 1\le k\le n-1 .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
That is, \(f_k\) faces of cardinality \(k\) have at most \(f_k^{(k)}\) faces of cardinality \(k+1\)
above them, and this bound is attained.

** Right-angle simplices and the two coordinate systems

# 2026-11-10 (Jan, round-3 report: "everything but C" of the Fable review; Part 1, Organisation 2 and
# Notation 1, 2): one coordinate system. def-ra now defines the angle, RA and F-tilde_j in the
# degree-0-inclusive space, with summand index i, which is how the rest of the paper uses them; Part I's
# degree-0-dropped objects are their images under pi. The opening of sec-lattice, which defined them a
# second time, is reduced to the integrality assumption.
# 2026-11-10 (Jan, round 5: number the displays in definitions that define their left side): eq:ra.
# The display in def-chained-points is a condition and stays unnumbered, as Jan asked.
# 2026-11-13 (Jan, sweep item 2): it is now numbered after all, eq:chained, being one of the 17.
#+NAME: def-ra
#+begin_definition
Let \(\vek c=(c_0,c_1,\dots,c_n)\) be a vector of positive reals with \(c_0=1\), the *leg vector*. The
*right-angle simplex* \(\rasimp(\vek c)\subseteq\mathbb R^{n+1}\) is the convex hull of the points
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Ftilde}
  \tilde F_j=(c_0,c_1,\dots,c_j,0,\dots,0),\qquad j=1,\dots,n .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
For a shift vector \(\vek d\) and
\(N=n+d_r\) the *right-angle polytope* is
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ra}
  \RA(\vek c;\vek d)=\sum_{i=1}^r\shift^{d_i}\bigl(\rasimp(\vek c)\bigr)\subseteq\mathbb R^{N+1} .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
The *window* of summand \(i\) is
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:window}
  W_i=[d_i,d_i+n] ,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
and every point of \(\shift^{d_i}(\rasimp(\vek c))\) is supported there.
#+end_definition

For \(\vek c=(\binom n0,\binom n1,\dots,\binom nn)\) these are \(\Koz(n)\) and \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\), and
\(\tilde F_j\) is the \(f\)-vector of the \((j-1)\)-skeleton of the full simplex on \([n]\).

# 2026-11-13 (Jan, sweep item 1: the sentence starting with "And", rephrase): "First", "Second".
This is [cite:@SnellmanArticle1 Definition 36], with two changes. First, the leg vector may be
real, which costs nothing. Second, the objects live in the space of the Kozlov polytope, the
/degree-\(0\)-inclusive/ space \(\mathbb R^{N+1}\) with coordinates \(x_0,\dots,x_N\). Part I works in
the /degree-\(0\)-dropped/ space \(\mathbb R^N\), with coordinates \(x_1,\dots,x_N\): the same names
with \(x_0\) forgotten. Let \(\pi\) be the projection forgetting \(x_0\). Part I's right-angle simplex
with leg vector \((c_1,\dots,c_n)\) is \(\pi(\rasimp(\vek c))\), and Part I's \(\tilde F_j\) is the
image of this one under \(\pi\). For the Kozlov polytope the two pictures are related by
[cite:@SnellmanArticle1 Section 8]
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:koz-as-ra}
  \pi\bigl(\Koz(n;\vek d)\bigr)=\RA^{\mathrm I}\bigl(\kozvec_n;\vek d\bigr)+\sum_{i\,:\,d_i\ge1}\vek e_{d_i},
  \qquad x_0=\#\lbrace i:d_i=0\rbrace\ \text{on }\Koz(n;\vek d),
\end{equation}
#+end_export
where \(\RA^{\mathrm I}(\kozvec_n;\vek d)\subseteq\mathbb R^N\) is Part I's right-angle polytope, whose
leg vector is the *Kozlov vector* \(\kozvec_n=(\binom n1,\dots,\binom nn)\), and \(\vek e_k\) is the
\(k\)-th standard basis vector of \(\mathbb R^N\), in the coordinates \(x_1,\dots,x_N\). Each summand
with \(d_i\ge1\) contributes its constant coordinate \(x_0=1\) in global coordinate \(d_i\), which
\(\pi\) keeps; the summands with \(d_i=0\) contribute it to \(x_0\), which \(\pi\) forgets. The vertex
and facet descriptions of Part I and of [cite:@SnellmanArticle4] are stated in Part I's coordinates.
In this paper \(\rasimp\), \(\RA\) and \(\tilde F_j\) always mean the degree-\(0\)-inclusive objects of
Definition [[def-ra]].

# 2026-11-13 (Jan, sweep items 2 and 3): eq:window in def-ra and eq:dim here, displayed and numbered.
The dimension is [cite:@SnellmanArticle1 Proposition 45]
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:dim}
  \dim\Koz(n;\vek d)=\Bigl\lvert\bigcup_{i=1}^r[d_i+2,d_i+n]\Bigr\rvert ,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
which is \(N-1\) when every gap is at most \(n-1\). When
every gap is at least \(n-1\) we say that \(\vek d\) is in the *product regime*: consecutive windows
then overlap only in coordinates where the later summand is constant, and \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) is
unimodularly equivalent to \(\Koz(n)^r\) (Proposition [[prop-product-regime]]).

The rank-one facet description, for a general leg vector, is the following. For
\(\Koz(n)\) it is [cite:@SnellmanArticle1 Lemma 13 and Corollary 16].

#+NAME: prop-rank-one-H
#+begin_proposition
\leanmark{\leanfull}Let \(\lambda>0\) and write
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:mu}
  \mu_k=x_k/c_k\quad(0\le k\le n),\qquad\mu_{n+1}=0 .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
Then
\(\lambda\rasimp(\vek c)=\lbrace x\in\mathbb R^{n+1}:\lambda=\mu_0=\mu_1\ge\mu_2\ge\dots\ge\mu_n\ge0\rbrace\),
and the barycentric coordinates of \(x\) with respect to \(\lambda\tilde F_1,\dots,\lambda\tilde F_n\)
are \((\mu_k-\mu_{k+1})/\lambda\), \(k=1,\dots,n\).
#+end_proposition

#+begin_proof
The vertex \(\lambda\tilde F_k\) has \(\mu=\lambda(1,\dots,1,0,\dots,0)\) with \(k+1\) ones, which
satisfies the chain; the chain set is convex, so it contains \(\lambda\rasimp(\vek c)\). Conversely
\(\mu=\sum_{k=1}^n(\mu_k-\mu_{k+1})(1^{k+1},0^{n-k})\) is a combination of the vertices'
\(\mu\)-images with non-negative coefficients summing to \(\mu_1=\lambda\).
#+end_proof

In the Kozlov case the \(\mu_k=x_k/\binom nk\) of \eqref{eq:mu} are the *LYM coordinates* of Part I, and
\(\Koz(n)=\lbrace1=\mu_0=\mu_1\ge\mu_2\ge\dots\ge\mu_n\ge0\rbrace\) is an order simplex. For a point
\(\vek p\in\rasimp(\vek c)\) we write
#+begin_export latex
\begin{align}
  \fl(\vek p)&=\max\lbrace k:\mu_k=1\rbrace,\label{eq:fl}\\
  \tp(\vek p)&=\max\lbrace k:\mu_k>0\rbrace,\label{eq:tp}\\
  \indeg(\vek p)&=\fl(\vek p)+1,\label{eq:indeg}
\end{align}
#+end_export
(\(\fl\) for "full up to"). For an \(f\)-vector \(\vek p\in S_n\), \(\tp(\vek p)\) is its top degree,
the largest \(k\) with \(p_k>0\), and \(\indeg(\vek p)\), the least \(k\) with \(p_k<\binom nk\), is
the initial degree of the corresponding ideal; these are Part II's two statistics
[cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Section 5]. By Proposition [[prop-rank-one-H]], \(\vek p\) is a convex
combination of the vertices \(\tilde F_k\) with \(\fl(\vek p)\le k\le\tp(\vek p)\), in which
\(\tilde F_{\fl(\vek p)}\) and \(\tilde F_{\tp(\vek p)}\) have positive weight; we call
\([\fl(\vek p),\tp(\vek p)]\) the *barycentric support* of \(\vek p\).

#+NAME: ex-koz3-koz4
#+begin_mexample
\(\Koz(3)\), of dimension \(2\), has vertices \((1,3,0,0)\), \((1,3,3,0)\), \((1,3,3,1)\); in \((x_2,x_3)\) it is the
triangle \(0\le3x_3\le x_2\le3\), whose five lattice points \((0,0),(1,0),(2,0),(3,0),(3,1)\) are
the \(f\)-vectors of the empty graph, one, two and three edges, and the full triangle. So
\(\mathcal L_3=S_3\).

\(\Koz(4)\), of dimension \(3\), is the tetrahedron \(1\ge x_2/6\ge x_3/4\ge x_4\ge0\) in \((x_2,x_3,x_4)\), with
\(20\) lattice points. Sixteen are \(f\)-vectors. The other four,
\[
  (x_2,x_3,x_4)=(2,1,0),\quad(3,2,0),\quad(4,2,0),\quad(5,3,0),
\]
violate the Kruskal--Katona bound \(x_3\le x_2^{(2)}\): one triangle needs three edges, two
triangles need five, three need six, and \(2^{(2)}=0\), \(3^{(2)}=1\), \(4^{(2)}=1\),
\(5^{(2)}=2\). Figure \ref{fig:koz4} shows them.
#+end_mexample

#+begin_export latex
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{img/fig-koz4.pdf}
\caption{The simplex $\Koz(4)$ (coordinates $x_2,x_3,x_4$; $x_0=1$, $x_1=4$) and its projection
to $(x_2,x_3)$. Filled dots: the $16$ $f$-vectors in $S_4$; hollow dots: the $4$ lattice points
$(2,1,0)$, $(3,2,0)$, $(4,2,0)$, $(5,3,0)$ of $\Koz(4)$ which are not $f$-vectors, all of them
violating the Kruskal--Katona bound $x_3\le x_2^{(2)}$. The only point with $x_4=1$ is the apex
$(6,4,1)$.}
\label{fig:koz4}
\end{figure}
#+end_export

* Lattice points: the canonical chained decomposition
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: sec-lattice
:END:

From here on the leg vector \(\vek c\) of Definition [[def-ra]] is integral: \(c_0=1\), and
\(c_1,\dots,c_n\) are positive integers. For \(\vek p\in\rasimp(\vek c)\) we use the LYM coordinates
\(\mu_k=p_k/c_k\) of \eqref{eq:mu} and the statistics \(\fl(\vek p)\) and \(\tp(\vek p)\) of Section [[#sec-setup]].

** The Kozlov simplex is a lecture hall simplex

For positive integers \(\vek s=(s_1,\dots,s_D)\) the *\(\vek s\)-lecture hall simplex* is
[cite:@SavageSchuster2012;@HibiOlsenTsuchiya2018]
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lhs}
  P^{(\vek s)}=\lbrace\lambda\in\mathbb R^D:0\le\lambda_1/s_1\le\dots\le\lambda_D/s_D\le1\rbrace .
\end{equation}
#+end_export

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 2: "describe the unimodular equivalence maps between angle(c) and
# P^(s) formally"): the maps phi, phi^{-1} and rev are now displayed (eq:lh-maps, eq:lh-reversal),
# with the affine hull A, and the proof checks each property. Same content as before, and the same as
# the Lean statement: phi is lhMap, phi^{-1} is liftC on planeC, rev is lhRev, and the converse is cOf
# (LEAN/ExteriorConvex/Lattice/LectureHall.lean), so \leanfull stands. The reversal is "rev", not rho,
# because rho(a,b) is eq:rho.
#+NAME: prop-lecture-hall
#+begin_proposition
\leanmark{\leanfull}Let \(D=n-1\) and \(\vek s=(c_n,c_{n-1},\dots,c_2)\), so that \(s_i=c_{n+1-i}\). The affine
hull of \(\rasimp(\vek c)\) is \(A=\lbrace p\in\mathbb R^{n+1}:p_0=1,\ p_1=c_1\rbrace\), and the maps
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lh-maps}
\begin{aligned}
  \varphi&\colon A\to\mathbb R^D, & (p_0,p_1,p_2,\dots,p_n)&\mapsto(p_n,p_{n-1},\dots,p_2),\\
  \varphi^{-1}&\colon\mathbb R^D\to A, & (\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_D)&\mapsto(1,c_1,\lambda_D,\lambda_{D-1},\dots,\lambda_1),
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
#+end_export
that is \(\lambda_i=p_{n+1-i}\), are mutually inverse affine bijections with
\(\varphi(A\cap\mathbb Z^{n+1})=\mathbb Z^D\) and \(\varphi(\rasimp(\vek c))=P^{(\vek s)}\). So
\(\rasimp(\vek c)\) and \(P^{(\vek s)}\) are unimodularly equivalent. Moreover, with
\(\bar{\vek s}=(s_D,\dots,s_1)\) the reversed sequence, the map
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lh-reversal}
  \operatorname{rev}\colon\mathbb R^D\to\mathbb R^D,\qquad \operatorname{rev}(\lambda)_i=s_{D+1-i}-\lambda_{D+1-i}\quad(1\le i\le D),
\end{equation}
#+end_export
is a unimodular affine bijection with \(\operatorname{rev}(P^{(\vek s)})=P^{(\bar{\vek s})}\); this is the
reversal equivalence of [cite:@HibiOlsenTsuchiya2018]. In particular \(\Koz(n)\) is unimodularly
equivalent to the \(\vek s\)-lecture hall simplex with
\(\vek s=(\binom nn,\dots,\binom n2)=(\binom n0,\dots,\binom n{n-2})\), and to the one with the
reversed sequence \((\binom n2,\dots,\binom nn)\). Conversely, for every \(\vek s\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^D\),
\(P^{(\vek s)}=\varphi(\rasimp(\vek c))\) for \(n=D+1\) and \(\vek c=(1,1,s_D,\dots,s_1)\).
#+end_proposition

#+begin_proof
The two maps in \eqref{eq:lh-maps} are inverse to each other by inspection, and both are affine.
The vertices \(\tilde F_j\) \eqref{eq:Ftilde} all have \(p_0=1\) and \(p_1=c_1\), so
\(\rasimp(\vek c)\subseteq A\); both have dimension \(D\), the vectors
\(\tilde F_j-\tilde F_1=(0,0,c_2,\dots,c_j,0,\dots,0)\) (\(2\le j\le n\)) being linearly independent, so
\(A\) is the affine hull. Since \(c_1\) is an integer, \(A\cap\mathbb Z^{n+1}\) is the set of integer
points with \(p_0=1\) and \(p_1=c_1\), which \(\varphi\) maps onto \(\mathbb Z^D\), and \(\varphi^{-1}\)
maps \(\mathbb Z^D\) back into it.

On \(A\), \(\mu_0=\mu_1=1\), so by Proposition [[prop-rank-one-H]] (at dilation factor \(1\))
\(\rasimp(\vek c)=\lbrace p\in A:1\ge p_2/c_2\ge\dots\ge p_n/c_n\ge0\rbrace\). In the coordinates
\(\lambda_i=p_{n+1-i}\), with \(s_i=c_{n+1-i}\), the chain read from the right is
\(0\le\lambda_1/s_1\le\dots\le\lambda_D/s_D\le1\), which is \eqref{eq:lhs}. So
\(\varphi(\rasimp(\vek c))=P^{(\vek s)}\).

The map \(\operatorname{rev}\) is a signed permutation of the coordinates followed by an integral translation,
so it maps \(\mathbb Z^D\) onto \(\mathbb Z^D\); it is an involution once \(\vek s\) and
\(\bar{\vek s}\) are exchanged. With \(\bar s_i=s_{D+1-i}\),
\(\operatorname{rev}(\lambda)_i/\bar s_i=1-\lambda_{D+1-i}/s_{D+1-i}\), so it carries the chain
\(0\le\lambda_1/s_1\le\dots\le\lambda_D/s_D\le1\) to
\(0\le\operatorname{rev}(\lambda)_1/\bar s_1\le\dots\le\operatorname{rev}(\lambda)_D/\bar s_D\le1\), and \(P^{(\vek s)}\) onto
\(P^{(\bar{\vek s})}\).

For the Kozlov polytope, \(c_k=\binom nk\) and \(\binom nk=\binom n{n-k}\). For the converse,
\(\vek c=(1,1,s_D,\dots,s_1)\) has \((c_n,\dots,c_2)=(s_1,\dots,s_D)=\vek s\).
#+end_proof

So the Ehrhart theory of \(\Koz(n)\) is that of a lecture hall simplex, and much of
Section [[#sec-ehrhart]] at rank one is a citation. The same holds for every integral leg vector. In
particular Kupreyeva's Ehrhart computations for the simplices of \(f\)-vectors with faces of at most
three or four elements [cite:@Kupreyeva2019], which are the simplices \(\rasimp(\vek c)\) with
\(c_k=\binom pk\) of Section [[#sec-kupreyeva]], are computations for lecture hall simplices.

** Chained tuples

#+NAME: def-chained-points
#+begin_definition
A tuple \((\vek p_1,\dots,\vek p_r)\in\bigl(\rasimp(\vek c)\bigr)^r\) is *chained* for
\(\vek d\) if
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:chained}
  \tp(\vek p_{i-1})+d_{i-1}\;\le\;\fl(\vek p_i)+d_i\qquad(i=2,\dots,r),
\end{equation}
#+end_export
equivalently \(\tp(\vek p_{i-1})<\indeg(\vek p_i)+\delta_i\), with the gap \(\delta_i\) of
\eqref{eq:gaps}. For
\(f\)-vectors this is the chaining condition of [cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Definition 19].
#+end_definition

For a summand \(i\) we distinguish its *own degree* \(m\), the index of a coordinate of
\(\vek p_i\), from the *global degree* \(k=m+d_i\), the coordinate of \(\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\) in which
it lands; the leg \(c_m\) is the *capacity* of own degree \(m\).
For a tuple \((\vek p_1,\dots,\vek p_r)\) and a global degree \(k\), the summands /present/ in
degree \(k\) are those \(i\) with \(k-d_i\in[0,n]\), and summand \(i\) contributes its own coordinate
\((\vek p_i)_{k-d_i}\) there. We call summand \(i\) *full* in degree \(k\) if that coordinate is the
whole capacity \(c_{k-d_i}\) (so \(\mu_{k-d_i}=1\)), *zero* if it is \(0\), and *partial* if it lies
strictly between.

In words, the definition says: in global degree \(\tp(\vek p_{i-1})+d_{i-1}\), the last degree in
which summand \(i-1\) is non-zero, summand \(i\) must still be full. So at every global degree \(k\)
the summands present there are, in the order of \(i\): zero, then at most one partial, then full.

# 2026-11-13 (Jan, sweep item 5: "state the log-concave condition where needed, rather than when not
# needed"). It was a hypothesis of the whole theorem, with "(no log-concavity needed)" in part 2; it is
# now the hypothesis of part 1 only, and cor-additivity and cor-idp, which said "under the hypotheses
# of Theorem thm-chained", now state it themselves. Part numbers unchanged, so the citations of
# parts (1)-(3) still hold. At d = 0 part 1 needs no log-concavity either (rem-chained-credit); not
# added to the statement, since the Lean statement (ChainedGeneral.lean) assumes it.
#+NAME: thm-chained
#+begin_theorem
\leanmark{\leanfull}(/Canonical chained decomposition./) Let \(\vek c\) be an integral leg vector and \(\vek d\) a
shift vector, and let \(x\in\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\).
1. (/Existence./) If \((c_2,\dots,c_n)\) is log-concave, that is \(c_k^2\ge c_{k-1}c_{k+1}\) for
   \(3\le k\le n-1\), then \(x=\sum_i\shift^{d_i}(\vek p_i)\) for a chained tuple of points
   \(\vek p_i\in\rasimp(\vek c)\).
2. (/Uniqueness./) There is at most one such chained tuple, and it is given by the
   *water-filling map*: with \(c_m=0\) for \(m\notin[0,n]\),
   # 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 1: "split"): the two formulas on two lines; the number had been
   # pushed below the display.
   #+begin_export latex
   \begin{equation}\label{eq:water}
   \begin{aligned}
     Q_i(k)&=\max\Bigl(0,\;x_k-\sum_{l>i}c_{k-d_l}\Bigr),\qquad Q_0\equiv0,\\
     (\vek p_i)_m&=Q_i(m+d_i)-Q_{i-1}(m+d_i).
   \end{aligned}
   \end{equation}
   #+end_export
3. (/Integrality./) If \(x\) is integral, so is every \(\vek p_i\) of that tuple.
The leg vector of the Kozlov polytope satisfies the hypothesis of (1), binomial coefficients being
log-concave.
#+end_theorem

In words: pour the amount \(x_k\) into the summands present in global degree \(k\), filling them
from the last one down, each to its capacity. Then \(Q_i(k)\) is what is left for summands
\(1,\dots,i\) once the summands \(l>i\) are full, and summand \(i\) receives the part of it that
does not go on to summands \(1,\dots,i-1\).

The proof is in Appendix [[#sec-app-chained]], followed by a comparison with the decomposition
theorem of Brändén and Solus for lecture hall polytopes, to which Theorem [[thm-chained]] reduces at
\(\vek d=\vek 0\) (Remark [[rem-chained-credit]]).

# 2026-11-09 (Jan): the proof of thm-chained and rem-chained-credit moved to Appendix sec-app-chained.

** Consequences

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 4): the first equation displayed and numbered (eq:lattice-add), the
# second numbered (eq:ff-in-lattice); the proof cites them instead of "the first equation" and "the
# display". (Comment moved out of the environment the same afternoon: code/leanmarks_article3.py skips
# only #+ lines, so a re-run would have put the Lean mark on it.)
#+NAME: cor-additivity
#+begin_corollary
\leanmark{\leanfull}(/Lattice points add./) If \((c_2,\dots,c_n)\) is log-concave, then
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lattice-add}
  \RA(\vek c;\vek d)\cap\mathbb Z^{N+1}=\sum_{i=1}^r\shift^{d_i}\bigl(\rasimp(\vek c)\cap\mathbb Z^{n+1}\bigr).
\end{equation}
#+end_export
In particular
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ff-in-lattice}
  \FF(n;\vek d)=\sum_{i=1}^r\shift^{d_i}(S_n)\;\subseteq\;\sum_{i=1}^r\shift^{d_i}(\mathcal L_n)=\Koz(n;\vek d)\cap\mathbb Z^{N+1},
\end{equation}
#+end_export
and the lattice points of \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) are in bijection with the chained tuples of
elements of \(\mathcal L_n\).
#+end_corollary

#+begin_proof
In \eqref{eq:lattice-add}, the right-hand side lies in the left-hand side because a sum of lattice
points is a lattice point and \(\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\) is the Minkowski sum of the
\(\shift^{d_i}\rasimp(\vek c)\). That every lattice point of \(\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\) is such a sum is
parts (1) and (3) of Theorem [[thm-chained]]. In \eqref{eq:ff-in-lattice}, the first equality is the
definition \eqref{eq:ff}, the inclusion holds because \(S_n\subseteq\mathcal L_n\), and the last
equality is \eqref{eq:lattice-add} for the leg vector of the Kozlov polytope. The bijection is
parts (1)--(3) of Theorem [[thm-chained]].
#+end_proof

#+NAME: cor-small-n
#+begin_corollary
\leanmark{\leanfull}For \(n\le3\), every lattice point of \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) is an ff-vector, for every \(r\) and every
\(\vek d\).
#+end_corollary

#+begin_proof
\(\mathcal L_2=S_2=\lbrace(1,2,0),(1,2,1)\rbrace\) and \(\mathcal L_3=S_3\) (Example
[[ex-koz3-koz4]]), so the two sumsets of Corollary [[cor-additivity]] coincide. For \(n=1\) the
polytope is a point.
#+end_proof

At \(n=4\) this fails already at rank one, by the four points of Example [[ex-koz3-koz4]].

#+begin_export latex
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{img/fig-koz3-dilates.pdf}
\caption{The Kozlov polytopes $\Koz(3)$, $\Koz(3;0,0)=2\Koz(3)$ and
$\Koz(3;0,0,0)=3\Koz(3)$, drawn in the free coordinates $(x_2,x_3)$ (here $x_0=r$, $x_1=3r$).
Dots are the ff-vectors, i.e.\ the Hilbert functions of quotients of $E^r$ by proper monomial
submodules. In all three cases every lattice point is achievable: $5$, $12$ and $22$ points
respectively, as Corollary~\ref{cor-small-n} requires.}
\label{fig:koz3-dilates}
\end{figure}
#+end_export

#+NAME: cor-membership
#+begin_corollary
\leanmark{\leancond}(/Which lattice points are ff-vectors./) A lattice point \(x\) of \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) lies in
\(\FF(n;\vek d)\) if and only if every part of its canonical decomposition lies in \(S_n\), that is,
satisfies the Kruskal--Katona inequalities.
#+end_corollary

#+begin_proof
"If" is trivial. Conversely, by Part II's chaining reformulation for proper submodules
[cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Theorems 23 and 41, with Lemma 40], every element of \(\FF(n;\vek d)\) is the
sum of a chained tuple of elements of \(S_n\); that tuple is a chained tuple of points of
\(\Koz(n)\), so by uniqueness it is the canonical one.
#+end_proof

So membership is effective: compute \(r\) water-filling differences and check \(r\) rank-one
Kruskal--Katona conditions. The new information, compared with Part II, is that the canonical
tuple exists for /every/ lattice point, achievable or not, so that the non-achievable lattice
points are exactly those whose canonical tuple contains a lattice point of \(\Koz(n)\) that is not
an \(f\)-vector. A second characterisation, directly in terms of the sequence, is Amata and Crupi's
module version of the Kruskal--Katona theorem [cite:@AmataCrupi2020KK Theorem 4.2], which holds
for all graded submodules of \(F\); a new proof of it is given in [cite:@SnellmanArticle5].

#+NAME: ex-koz3-d01
#+begin_mexample
\(\Koz(3;0,1)\) has dimension \(3\), \(7\) vertices \((1,4,x_2,x_3,x_4)\) with \((x_2,x_3,x_4)\) one of
#+begin_export latex
\[\small
\begin{array}{lllllll}
(3,0,0), & (3,3,0), & (3,3,1), & (6,0,0), & (6,4,0), & (6,4,1), & (6,3,1),
\end{array}
\]
#+end_export
and \(22\) lattice points, all ff-vectors. In the same coordinates
\[
  \Koz(3;0,1)=\lbrace3\le x_2\le6,\ 3x_3\le x_2+6,\ 3x_4\le x_3,\ 0\le x_4\le1\rbrace ,
\]
with \(6\) facets: the vertices of this system are exactly the \(7\) points above, and none of its
inequalities is redundant. Some canonical decompositions:
#+begin_export latex
\[
\begin{array}{l}
(1,4,3,0,0)=(1,3,0,0)+\shift(1,3,0,0),\\ (1,4,4,3,1)=(1,3,1,0)+\shift(1,3,3,1),\\
(1,4,6,4,0)=(1,3,3,1)+\shift(1,3,3,0),\\ (1,4,6,4,1)=(1,3,3,1)+\shift(1,3,3,1).
\end{array}
\]
#+end_export
The first part's edge count is \(x_2-3\). It can carry the triangle only if it is all of
\((1,3,3,1)\), which forces \(x_2=6\); the chaining condition \(\tp(\vek p_1)\le\fl(\vek p_2)+1\) then
requires \(\fl(\vek p_2)\ge2\), that is, the second part contains all three edges, so \(x_3\ge4\).
These are the points \((1,4,6,4,0)\) and \((1,4,6,4,1)\) above.
#+end_mexample

#+begin_export latex
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{img/fig-koz3-d01.pdf}
\caption{$\Koz(3;0,1)=\Koz(3)+\shift(\Koz(3))$, a $3$-polytope with $7$ vertices in the
coordinates $(x_2,x_3,x_4)$ ($x_0=1$, $x_1=4$), with its slices $x_4=0$ and $x_4=1$. All $22$
lattice points are ff-vectors.}
\label{fig:koz3-d01}
\end{figure}
#+end_export

#+NAME: ex-koz3-d02
#+begin_mexample
\(\Koz(3;0,2)\) is in the product regime (Section [[#sec-setup]]): its windows overlap only where
the later summand is constant (\(x_0=1\), \(x_1=n\)), so the polytope is unimodularly equivalent to
\(\Koz(3)\times\Koz(3)\) (Proposition [[prop-product-regime]]), of dimension \(4\), with \(9\)
vertices, \(6\) facets, and \(25\) lattice points, all ff-vectors (Figure \ref{fig:koz3-d02}).
#+end_mexample

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 5: title font too big, figure slightly too large beside fig:koz3-d01):
# redrawn on a larger canvas with the same font sizes (code/oneoff/ff_pictures.py), and included at
# the same scale as fig:koz3-d01, whose PDF is 403.2 pt wide at \linewidth: this one is 234.7 pt, so

#+begin_export latex
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.582\linewidth]{img/fig-koz3-d02-product.pdf}
\caption{In the product regime the Kozlov polytope splits: $\Koz(3;0,2)\cong\Koz(3)\times\Koz(3)$,
with $(x_2,x_3)\in(1,3)+\Koz(3)$ (outer dashed triangle) and $(x_4,x_5)\in\Koz(3)$ (small
triangles). Each of the $5$ outer lattice points carries a full copy of the $5$ inner ones, and
all $25=5\cdot 5$ lattice points are ff-vectors.}
\label{fig:koz3-d02}
\end{figure}
#+end_export

#+NAME: ex-koz4-d01
#+begin_mexample
\(\Koz(4;0,1)\) has dimension \(4\), \(11\) vertices \((1,5,x_2,x_3,x_4,x_5)\) with
\((x_2,x_3,x_4,x_5)\) one of
#+begin_export latex
\[\small
\begin{array}{llllll}
(4,0,0,0), & (4,6,0,0), & (4,6,4,0), & (4,6,4,1), & (10,0,0,0), & (10,6,4,0),\\
(10,6,4,1), & (10,10,0,0), & (10,10,4,1), & (10,10,5,0), & (10,10,5,1), &
\end{array}
\]
#+end_export
\(214\) lattice points and \(162\) ff-vectors. In the same coordinates
#+begin_export latex
\[
\begin{array}{l}
\Koz(4;0,1)=\lbrace4\le x_2\le10,\ 3x_3\le2x_2+10,\ 4x_4\le x_3+10,\\
\phantom{\Koz(4;0,1)=\lbrace}3x_4\le2x_3,\ 4x_5\le x_4,\ 0\le x_5\le1\rbrace ,
\end{array}
\]
#+end_export
with \(8\) facets: the vertices of this system are exactly the \(11\) points above, and none of
its inequalities is redundant. The \(52\) non-achievable lattice points split, by which
canonical part fails Kruskal--Katona, into \(28\) whose second part fails and \(24\) whose first
part fails; in none do both parts fail. Two examples, in coordinates \((x_0,\dots,x_5)\):
#+begin_export latex
\begin{gather*}
  (1,5,4,2,1,0)=(1,4,0,0,0)+\shift(1,4,2,1,0),\\
  (1,5,6,7,0,0)=(1,4,2,1,0)+\shift(1,4,6,0,0).
\end{gather*}
#+end_export
The second can be checked by hand: in any representation as \(\vek s_1+\shift(\vek s_2)\) with
\(\vek s_i\in S_4\), degree \(2\) forces \(f_2(\vek s_1)=6-4=2\); two edges carry no triangle, so
\(f_3(\vek s_1)=0\), and degree \(3\) forces \(f_2(\vek s_2)=7>\binom42\), which is impossible.

Two more rank-two polytopes, which reappear in Table \ref{tab:fill}. \(\Koz(4;0,0)=2\Koz(4)\) has \(81\) lattice points, of which
\(69\) are ff-vectors; the \(12\) others are drawn in Figure \ref{fig:koz4-d00}. For
\(\Koz(4;0,2)\), \(246\) of the \(386\) lattice points are ff-vectors, and among the \(140\) others
there are, for the first time in these examples, \(16\) whose two canonical parts both fail.
#+end_mexample

#+begin_export latex
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{img/fig-koz4-d00-slices.pdf}
\caption{The rank-two Kozlov polytope $\Koz(4;0,0)=2\Koz(4)$ ($x_0=2$, $x_1=8$), sliced at
$x_4=0,1,2$. Of its $81$ lattice points, $69$ are ff-vectors (filled) and $12$ are not (hollow).
Each non-achievable point is the topmost lattice point of its column ($x_2$, $x_4$ fixed), just
below the facet $\mu_2=\mu_3$.}
\label{fig:koz4-d00}
\end{figure}
#+end_export

# 2026-11-10 (Fable review, Part 1, Organisation 5): cor-idp moved here, after the examples, which
# illustrate cor-membership and now follow it directly.
#+NAME: cor-idp
#+begin_corollary
\leanmark{\leanfull}(/Integer decomposition property./) If \((c_2,\dots,c_n)\) is log-concave, then
\(\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\) has the integer decomposition property (IDP) for every \(\vek d\): every lattice
point of \(t\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\) is a sum of \(t\) lattice points of \(\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\). In
particular every Kozlov polytope is IDP.
#+end_corollary

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 2): the proof no longer opens with a formula.
#+begin_proof
The dilate \(t\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\) is \(\RA(\vek c;\vek d^{\times t})\), where \(\vek d^{\times t}\) repeats
each \(d_i\) \(t\) times:
\[
  \vek d^{\times t}=(\underbrace{d_1,\dots,d_1}_{t},\underbrace{d_2,\dots,d_2}_{t},\dots,\underbrace{d_r,\dots,d_r}_{t}).
\]
By Corollary [[cor-additivity]] a lattice point of it is a sum of \(rt\) shifted
lattice points of \(\rasimp(\vek c)\); regroup them as \(t\) sums each with one point from every
\(i\), and each such sum is a lattice point of \(\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\).
#+end_proof

At \(\vek d=\vek 0\) this is the integer decomposition property of lecture hall simplices
[cite:@BrandenSolus2020], which Hibi, Olsen and Tsuchiya had proved for monotone \(\vek s\) and
conjectured in general [cite:@HibiOlsenTsuchiya2018]; the Kozlov sequence
\((\binom n2,\dots,\binom nn)\) is monotone only for \(n\le5\).

# 2026-11-09 (Jan, round-2 report item 14): Section sec-module-kk (module Kruskal--Katona) and its Appendix
# sec-app-PQ moved to Part V, article/exterior-convex_article5_draft_1.org, cited as [cite:@SnellmanArticle5].

* Discrete volume versus volume
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: sec-volume
:END:

Three sizes of the same polytope compete: \(\lvert\FF(n;\vek d)\rvert\), the number of achievable
Hilbert functions, counted in Part II; the number of lattice points; and the volume. By
Corollary [[cor-additivity]] the first two are cardinalities of sumsets of the nested sets
\(S_n\subseteq\mathcal L_n\).

** Normalisation

Let \(U=\bigcup_i[d_i+2,d_i+n]\), so that \(D=\lvert U\rvert=\dim\Koz(n;\vek d)\) by \eqref{eq:dim}. Every coordinate outside
\(U\) is constant on \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\), so its affine hull is parallel to the coordinate subspace
\(\mathbb R^U\), and the coordinate projection to \(\mathbb R^U\) is an isometry on it that maps the
lattice points of the affine hull onto \(\mathbb Z^U\), a unimodular equivalence onto its image. We
therefore use two volumes: the *Euclidean* \(D\)-dimensional volume \(\vol\), which is the one in
Kupreyeva's thesis [cite:@Kupreyeva2019], and the *normalised* volume
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:NV}
  \NV=D!\,\vol ,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
for which a unimodular simplex has \(\NV=1\) and which equals the sum of the \(h^*\)-vector
(Section [[#sec-ehrhart]]).

#+NAME: prop-simplex-volume
#+begin_proposition
\leanmark{\leanpartial}\(\NV(\rasimp(\vek c))=c_2c_3\cdots c_n\). In particular \(\NV(\Koz(n))=\prod_{k=2}^n\binom nk\) and
\(\vol(\Koz(n))=\frac1{n!}\prod_{k=1}^n\binom nk\).
#+end_proposition

#+begin_proof
In LYM coordinates \(\rasimp(\vek c)\) is the image of the order simplex
\(\lbrace1\ge\mu_2\ge\dots\ge\mu_n\ge0\rbrace\) under \(\operatorname{diag}(c_2,\dots,c_n)\), and the order
simplex is unimodular: its edge vectors \(e_2,e_2+e_3,\dots\) from \(0\) form a basis of
\(\mathbb Z^{n-1}\). Divide by \((n-1)!\) and note \(\binom n1=n\).
#+end_proof

The Euclidean form is Kupreyeva's [cite:@Kupreyeva2019 Section 6.1], proved there by the same
triangular determinant.

** The staircase tiling

Assume every gap \(\delta_i\) \eqref{eq:gaps} is at most \(n-1\), so that \(U=[2,N]\) and \(D=N-1\). A
*staircase index* is \(\vek t=(t_0,\dots,t_r)\) with
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:staircase-index}
  1=t_0\le t_1\le\dots\le t_r=N,\qquad t_{i-1}-d_i\ge1,\qquad t_i-d_i\le n ,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
where \(N=n+d_r\) as before. Its *cell* is
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cell}
  C_{\vek t}=\sum_{i=1}^r\shift^{d_i}\conv\lbrace\tilde F_{\alpha_i},\dots,\tilde F_{\beta_i}\rbrace,
  \qquad \alpha_i=t_{i-1}-d_i,\quad \beta_i=t_i-d_i .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
The face
\(\conv\lbrace\tilde F_\alpha,\dots,\tilde F_\beta\rbrace\) varies exactly in the own coordinates
\((\alpha,\beta]\), so summand \(i\) occupies the global block \((t_{i-1},t_i]\); these blocks partition
\([2,N]\). An *orthoscheme* is a simplex \(\conv\lbrace v_0,\dots,v_m\rbrace\) whose path edges
\(v_1-v_0,\ v_2-v_1,\ \dots,\ v_m-v_{m-1}\) are pairwise orthogonal; we call these edges its
*legs*. The
right-angle simplex \(\rasimp(\vek c)\) is one, since \(\tilde F_{k+1}-\tilde F_k=c_{k+1}\vek e_{k+1}\),
and so is each of its faces \(\conv\lbrace\tilde F_\alpha,\dots,\tilde F_\beta\rbrace\). So
\(C_{\vek t}\) is a lattice translate of a product of orthoschemes on complementary coordinate blocks.

#+NAME: thm-staircase
#+begin_theorem
\leanmark{\leannone}If \((c_2,\dots,c_n)\) is log-concave and every gap is at most \(n-1\), the cells \(C_{\vek t}\)
tile \(\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\): they cover it and have pairwise disjoint interiors. Hence
\[
  \NV\bigl(\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\bigr)=\sum_{\vek t}\binom{D}{t_1-t_0,\ t_2-t_1,\ \dots,\ t_r-t_{r-1}}
  \prod_{i=1}^r\ \prod_{k=t_{i-1}+1}^{t_i}c_{k-d_i}.
\]
#+end_theorem

#+begin_proof
/Cover./ Take the canonical decomposition of \(x\) (Theorem [[thm-chained]]) and put
\(\ell_i=\fl(\vek p_i)+d_i\), \(\tau_i=\tp(\vek p_i)+d_i\); chaining says
\(\tau_i\le\ell_{i+1}\). Choose \(t_i\in[\tau_i,\ell_{i+1}]\cap[d_{i+1}+1,d_i+n]\),
non-empty because \(\tau_i\le d_i+n\), \(\ell_{i+1}\ge d_{i+1}+1\), \(\tau_i\le\ell_{i+1}\)
and \(d_{i+1}+1\le d_i+n\). Then \(t_{i-1}\le\ell_i\le\tau_i\le t_i\), and the barycentric
support \([\fl(\vek p_i),\tp(\vek p_i)]\) of \(\vek p_i\) lies in \([\alpha_i,\beta_i]\), so
\(x\in C_{\vek t}\).

/Disjointness./ Let \(\vek t\ne\vek t'\), let \(i\) be the first index with \(t_i\ne t'_i\), say
\(t_i<t'_i\), and put \(k=t_i+1\) and \(T=\sum_{l>i}c_{k-d_l}\). In \(C_{\vek t}\) the summands
\(l\le i\) vanish in degree \(k\) and the others are at most full, so \(x_k\le T\). In \(C_{\vek t'}\)
the summands \(l<i\) vanish, the summands \(l>i\) are full (their \(\alpha_l\ge k-d_l\)), and summand
\(i\) is non-negative, so \(x_k\ge T\). The hyperplane \(x_k=T\) separates the cells.

/Volume./ An orthoscheme with integer legs has \(\NV\) equal to the product of its legs
(Proposition [[prop-simplex-volume]]), and for a product of lattice polytopes on complementary
coordinate blocks \(D!\,\vol\) is the multinomial coefficient times the product of the factors'
\(\NV\).
#+end_proof

If some gap is at least \(n-1\), \(\RA(\vek c;\vek d)\) is a lattice product of the sub-sums over
the maximal runs with gaps at most \(n-2\), and \(\NV\) is the multinomial in the run dimensions
times the product of the runs' \(\NV\). (A gap of exactly \(n-1\) is covered by both descriptions,
which agree: the later summand is constant on the coordinates the two windows share. When every gap
is at least \(n-1\) this is the product regime, Proposition [[prop-product-regime]] below.) For non-log-concave legs the formula fails, consistently
with the failure of covering: for \(\vek c=(1,2,1,3,1,3)\), \(\vek d=(0,1)\) it gives \(540\) against
the true \(1500\).

#+NAME: cor-rank2-volume
#+begin_corollary
\leanmark{\leannone}For \(0\le d\le n-1\), \(m=n-d\) and \(D=n-1+d\),
\[
  \NV\bigl(\Koz(n;0,d)\bigr)=\NV(\Koz(n))\cdot\sum_{j=1}^{m}\binom{n-1+d}{d+j-1}\prod_{k=j+1}^{j+d}\binom nk .
\]
#+end_corollary

#+begin_proof
The staircase indices are \((1,d+j,n+d)\), \(1\le j\le m\), and the cells are
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:cells-rank2}
  C_j=C_{(1,d+j,n+d)}=\conv\lbrace\tilde F_1,\dots,\tilde F_{d+j}\rbrace+\shift^d\conv\lbrace\tilde F_j,\dots,\tilde F_n\rbrace,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
and
\[
  \NV(C_j)=\binom D{d+j-1}\prod_{k=2}^{d+j}\binom nk\prod_{k=j+1}^n\binom nk .
\]
#+end_proof

At \(d=0\) the sum is \(2^{n-1}\), so \(\NV(2\Koz(n))=2^{n-1}\NV(\Koz(n))\), as it must be; at \(d=n-1\)
there is one cell and \(\NV=\binom{2n-2}{n-1}\NV(\Koz(n))^2\), the product.

#+NAME: ex-volume
#+begin_mexample
\(\Koz(3;0,1)\) has dimension \(D=3\) and two cells (Figure \ref{fig:koz3-d01-tiling}), \(j=1,2\) in the notation of
Corollary [[cor-rank2-volume]]: \(j=1\), staircase index \((1,2,4)\), blocks
\(\lbrace2\rbrace\mid\lbrace3,4\rbrace\), \(\NV=\binom31\cdot3\cdot(3\cdot1)=27\); and \(j=2\), staircase
index \((1,3,4)\), blocks \(\lbrace2,3\rbrace\mid\lbrace4\rbrace\), \(\NV=\binom32\cdot(3\cdot1)\cdot1=9\).
So \(\NV=36\) and \(\vol=36/3!=6\). \(\Koz(4;0,1)\) has dimension \(D=4\) and three cells: \(j=1\) gives
\(\binom41\cdot6\cdot(6\cdot4\cdot1)=576\), \(j=2\) gives \(\binom42\cdot(6\cdot4)\cdot(4\cdot1)=576\), \(j=3\)
gives \(\binom43\cdot(6\cdot4\cdot1)\cdot1=96\); \(\NV=1248\), \(\vol=1248/4!=52\).
#+end_mexample

\begin{figure}[htbp]

$C_{(1,2,4)}=\conv\lbrace\tilde F_1,\tilde F_2\rbrace+\shift\Koz(3)$ is the prism
$[3,6]\times\lbrace0\le3x_4\le x_3\le3\rbrace$, with $\NV=27$; the cell
$C_{(1,3,4)}=\Koz(3)+\shift\conv\lbrace\tilde F_2,\tilde F_3\rbrace$ is the prism
$\lbrace0\le3(x_3-3)\le x_2-3\le3\rbrace\times[0,1]$, with $\NV=9$. They meet in the plane

\label{fig:koz3-d01-tiling}

#+end_export

# 2026-11-13 (Jan, sweep item 6: "explain with more symbols, less words"). Rewritten around the band
# (eq:band), the corner sequence of the path and one formula for the term; content unchanged.
#+NAME: ex-staircase-paths
#+begin_mexample
(/Staircase indices as lattice paths./) Put the vertex \(\shift^{d_i}\tilde F_m\) of summand \(i\)
at the grid point \((m+d_i,i)\): column = global degree, row = summand. The vertices fill the *band*
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:band}
  \mathcal B=\lbrace(k,i)\in\mathbb Z^2:1\le i\le r,\ d_i+1\le k\le d_i+n\rbrace .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
To a staircase index \(\vek t\) attach the east--north lattice path with corners
\[
  (t_0,1)\to(t_1,1)\to(t_1,2)\to(t_2,2)\to\dots\to(t_{r-1},r)\to(t_r,r).
\]
Its north step from row \(i\) to row \(i+1\) lies in \(\mathcal B\) if and only if
\(d_{i+1}+1\le t_i\le d_i+n\), which is \eqref{eq:staircase-index}. So staircase indices are the
east--north paths in \(\mathcal B\) from \((1,1)\) to \((N,r)\), and
\(\vek t^{\uparrow}\le\vek t\le\vek t^{\downarrow}\) for
\(\vek t^{\uparrow}=(1,d_2+1,\dots,d_r+1,N)\) and \(\vek t^{\downarrow}=(1,n,d_2+n,\dots,d_{r-1}+n,N)\),
which step north as early and as late as possible. In row \(i\) the path visits \((k,i)\) for
\(t_{i-1}\le k\le t_i\), that is \(\tilde F_{\alpha_i},\dots,\tilde F_{\beta_i}\) of
\eqref{eq:cell}. Give the east step \((k-1,i)\to(k,i)\) the weight \(c_{k-d_i}\), the leg
\(\tilde F_{k-d_i}-\tilde F_{k-d_i-1}=c_{k-d_i}\vek e_{k-d_i}\). Then the term of \(\vek t\) in
Theorem [[thm-staircase]] is
\[
  \NV(C_{\vek t})=\binom{D}{t_1-t_0,\ \dots,\ t_r-t_{r-1}}\prod_{(k-1,i)\to(k,i)}c_{k-d_i} ,
\]
the product over the \(D=N-1\) east steps. For \(\Koz(3;0,1)\) the rows are \([1,3]\) and
\([2,4]\), \(t_1\in\lbrace2,3\rbrace\), and the two paths are the two cells of
Example [[ex-volume]]. \(\Koz(3;0,1,2)\) has four (Figure \ref{fig:staircase-paths}(b)), and
\(\NV=324+108+54+36=522\).
#+end_mexample

# 2026-11-10 (Fable review, Part 1, Organisation 6): the second paragraph of ex-staircase-paths, the
# literature connection at d = 0, is now this remark.
#+NAME: rem-staircase-cayley
#+begin_remark
(/Equal shifts: the staircase triangulation./) At \(\vek d=\vek 0\) every row of
Example [[ex-staircase-paths]] is \([1,n]\), the band is the whole \(r\times n\) grid, and the paths
are all its \(\binom{n+r-2}{r-1}\) monotone paths from corner to corner. Let
\(\Delta^{n-1}=\conv\lbrace\vek e_1,\dots,\vek e_n\rbrace\) be the standard simplex, and draw the
vertex \((\vek e_i,\vek e_j)\) of \(\Delta^{r-1}\times\Delta^{n-1}\) as the dot in row \(i\) and
column \(j\). Then these paths are the maximal simplices of the /staircase triangulation/ of
\(\Delta^{r-1}\times\Delta^{n-1}\) [cite:@CeballosPadrolSarmiento2015 Example 2.2]. The Cayley
trick sends a simplex \(s\) of a triangulation of \(\Delta^{r-1}\times\Delta^{n-1}\) to the
Minkowski cell \(\sum_i\conv\lbrace\vek e_j:(\vek e_i,\vek e_j)\in s\rbrace\) of \(r\Delta^{n-1}\),
and is a bijection from triangulations to fine mixed subdivisions [cite:@Santos2005 Theorem 1.4 and
Section 1.4;@CeballosPadrolSarmiento2015 Section 2]. The linear map \(\vek e_j\mapsto\tilde F_j\)
takes \(\Delta^{n-1}\) onto \(\rasimp(\vek c)\), and the cell of the path of \(\vek t\) onto
\(C_{\vek t}\). So at \(\vek d=\vek 0\) the staircase tiling is the image of the fine mixed
subdivision of \(r\Delta^{n-1}\) that corresponds to the staircase triangulation
(Figure \ref{fig:staircase-paths}(a)). There it needs no log-concavity:
\(\operatorname{diag}(c_0,\dots,c_n)\) maps \(\rasimp(\vek 1)\) onto \(\rasimp(\vek c)\), and each
cell for \(\vek 1\), which is log-concave, onto the cell for \(\vek c\) with the same index. For
\(\vek d\ne\vek 0\) the diagonal map does not commute with the shifts, and the non-log-concave
leg vector after Theorem [[thm-staircase]] shows that log-concavity cannot then be dropped.
#+end_remark

# 2026-11-09 (Jan: "the remark should be accompanied with / be replaced by an example, with a
# figure"). TikZ by hand; the cells of (a) and the indices and NV values of (b) are those printed by
# ../code/oneoff/staircase_lattice_paths.py, part (C) (runs/2026-11-09_staircase-lattice-paths.txt).
# Compiled standalone with xelatex and pdflatex, checked in colour and greyscale.
#+begin_export latex
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\definecolor{stairblue}{HTML}{2A78D6}
% \stairglyph{n}{d_2}{d_3}{t_1}{t_2}: the staircase index (1,t_1,t_2,d_3+n) of Koz(n;0,d_2,d_3)
% as a lattice path.  Row i (at height i-1) has dots in the columns d_i+1, ..., d_i+n; the band
% lies between the lowest path (north steps in columns n, d_2+n) and the highest (columns
% d_2+1, d_3+1).  The path runs along row i from column t_{i-1} to column t_i.
\newcommand{\stairglyph}[5]{%
  \filldraw[band] (1,0) -- (#1,0) -- (#1,1) -- ({#2+#1},1) -- ({#2+#1},2) -- ({#3+#1},2)
    -- ({#3+1},2) -- ({#3+1},1) -- ({#2+1},1) -- ({#2+1},0) -- cycle;
  \foreach \x in {1,...,#1} {\node[dot] at (\x,0) {}; \node[dot] at ({\x+#2},1) {};
    \node[dot] at ({\x+#3},2) {};}
  \draw[spath] (1,0) -- (#4,0) -- (#4,1) -- (#5,1) -- (#5,2) -- ({#3+#1},2);
  \pgfmathtruncatemacro{\stairN}{#3+#1}%
  \foreach \x in {1,...,#4} {\node[vdot] at (\x,0) {};}
  \foreach \x in {#4,...,#5} {\node[vdot] at (\x,1) {};}
  \foreach \x in {#5,...,\stairN} {\node[vdot] at (\x,2) {};}}
\begin{tikzpicture}[
    font=\scriptsize,
    dot/.style={circle, fill=black!50, inner sep=0pt, minimum size=2.2pt},
    vdot/.style={circle, fill=stairblue, inner sep=0pt, minimum size=3.4pt},
    spath/.style={stairblue, line width=1.2pt, line cap=round, line join=round},
    band/.style={fill=black!10, draw=black!10, line width=4.5pt, line join=round},
    small glyph/.style={x=0.2cm, y=0.2cm,
      dot/.style={circle, fill=black!50, inner sep=0pt, minimum size=1.7pt},
      vdot/.style={circle, fill=stairblue, inner sep=0pt, minimum size=2.6pt},
      spath/.style={stairblue, line width=0.9pt, line cap=round, line join=round},
      band/.style={fill=black!10, draw=black!10, line width=3pt, line join=round}},
    cell/.style={draw=black, line width=0.5pt},
  ]
  % (a) 3Koz(3) = Koz(3;0,0,0) in (x_2,x_3); cells as printed by
  % ../code/oneoff/staircase_lattice_paths.py, part (C)
  \begin{scope}[x=1.1cm, y=1.1cm]
    \node[anchor=south west, font=\small] at (-0.1,3.05) {(a) \(\vek d=(0,0,0)\): \(3\Koz(3)\)};
    \filldraw[cell, fill=stairblue!18] (0,0) -- (3,0) -- (3,1) -- cycle;
    \filldraw[cell, fill=stairblue!18] (3,1) -- (6,1) -- (6,2) -- cycle;

    \filldraw[cell, fill=white] (6,0) rectangle (9,1);

    % triangles (one summand is all of Koz(3)); right angle at the bottom right

      \node[anchor=east] at (\a+2.33,\b+0.22) {\((1,\ta,\tb,3)\)};

  \end{scope}
  % (b) Koz(3;0,1,2): a key (own index above each dot, column below), then the four paths
  \node[anchor=south west, font=\small] at (-0.1cm,-1.3cm) {(b) \(\vek d=(0,1,2)\): \(\Koz(3;0,1,2)\)};

    \filldraw[band] (1,0) -- (3,0) -- (3,1) -- (4,1) -- (4,2) -- (5,2) -- (3,2) -- (3,1)
      -- (2,1) -- (2,0) -- cycle;

    \foreach \i in {1,2,3} \node[font=\tiny, anchor=east] at (0.55,\i-1) {\(i=\i\)};

\caption{Staircase indices as lattice paths (Example~\ref{ex-staircase-paths} and
Remark~\ref{rem-staircase-cayley}). In each small
diagram, row \(i\), counted from below, has dots in the columns \(d_i+1,\dots,d_i+n\), the dot in
column \(k\) standing for the vertex \(\tilde F_{k-d_i}\) of the \(i\)-th summand; the shaded band
lies between the two extreme paths. The path of \(\vek t\) runs along row \(i\) from column
\(t_{i-1}\) to column \(t_i\), and the dots it visits there span the \(i\)-th summand of
\(C_{\vek t}\). (a) \(\Koz(3;0,0,0)=3\Koz(3)\) in the coordinates \((x_2,x_3)\), with \(x_0=3\),
\(x_1=9\): its six cells, each with its index and its path. The shaded triangles are translates of
\(\Koz(3)\), with \(\NV=3\); the rectangles are sums of the edges
\(\conv\lbrace\tilde F_1,\tilde F_2\rbrace\) and \(\conv\lbrace\tilde F_2,\tilde F_3\rbrace\) of two
different summands, with \(\NV=6\). (b) \(\Koz(3;0,1,2)\): the band, with the own index of each
vertex above its dot and the column below, and the four paths with their \(\NV(C_{\vek t})\), which
add up to \(522=\NV(\Koz(3;0,1,2))\).}
\label{fig:staircase-paths}
\end{figure}
#+end_export

#+NAME: prop-product-regime
#+begin_proposition
\leanmark{\leanfull}(/The product regime./) If every gap is at least \(n-1\), \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) is unimodularly
equivalent to \(\Koz(n)^r\). Consequently \(\vol(\Koz(n;\vek d))=\vol(\Koz(n))^r\), the lattice-point
count is \(\lvert\mathcal L_n\rvert^r\), the Ehrhart polynomial (Section [[#sec-ehrhart]]) is
\(L_{\Koz(n)}(t)^r\), and
\(\lvert\FF(n;\vek d)\rvert=\lvert S_n\rvert^r\).
#+end_proposition

#+begin_proof
Consecutive windows overlap only in coordinates where the later summand is constant (\(x_0=1\),
\(x_1=n\)), so the coordinate projection onto the summands' blocks is an affine bijection onto
\(\Koz(n)^r\) that maps lattice points onto lattice points, a unimodular equivalence. Volumes and lattice points of a product, also of its dilates,
multiply. For the last statement, the sumset map \(S_n^r\to\FF(n;\vek d)\) is onto by definition,
and the block projection is a left inverse of it on \(\Koz(n)^r\), so it is a bijection; this is
also [cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Proposition 42].
#+end_proof

#+begin_export latex
\begin{center}\small

\toprule
\(n\) & \(d=0\) & \(d=1\) & \(d=2\) & \(d=3\) & \(d\ge n-1\)\\
\midrule
2 & 2 & 1 & & & 1\\
3 & 6 & 6 & 9/4 & & 9/4\\
4 & 32 & 52 & 56 & 16 & 16\\
5 & 1000/3 & 5125/6 & 64375/36 & 34375/18 & 15625/36\\

\smallskip

\midrule

\captionof{table}{Euclidean volume (top) and normalised volume (bottom) of \(\Koz(n;0,d)\), from
Corollary~\ref{cor-rank2-volume}; every entry was also computed independently with Normaliz. For
\(n=6\), \(d=4\) the Euclidean volume is \(270000\), larger than both neighbours.}
\label{tab:volumes}
\end{center}
#+end_export

The Euclidean volume is not monotone in \(d\), but nothing should be read into this: the dimension
\(D=n-1+d\) changes with \(d\), so the entries measure different kinds of volume. The normalised
volume, although it too depends on the dimension, is monotone in every computed case.

#+NAME: conj-nv-monotone
#+begin_conjecture
\(\NV(\Koz(n;0,d))\) is weakly increasing in \(d\). (Verified for \(n\le7\) and
\(0\le d\le n-1\); for \(d\ge n-1\) the value is constant, by Proposition [[prop-product-regime]].)
#+end_conjecture

** Hilbert functions, lattice points and volume

Volume compares the polytope with itself across \(n\) and \(\vek d\); to compare it with the set of
Hilbert functions, a dimension-free size is needed. Lattice-point counts are dimension-free, and
they are the right comparison for \(\lvert\FF\rvert\). The *fill rate* is
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fill}
  \operatorname{fill}(n;\vek d)=\frac{\lvert\FF(n;\vek d)\rvert}{\#\bigl(\Koz(n;\vek d)\cap\mathbb Z^{N+1}\bigr)},
  \qquad \operatorname{fill}(n)=\frac{\lvert S_n\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal L_n\rvert} .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
