# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 6: are the "three limits" the three cases below, or fill, centroid and
# covariance?): the three cases; now said so.
Three ways of letting the rank \(r\) grow, with \(n\) fixed, are within reach, and the fill rate
behaves differently in each. Cases 2 and 3 use results proved in Section [[#sec-ehrhart]] below.

1. *Product regime.* By Proposition [[prop-product-regime]], \(\operatorname{fill}(n;\vek d)=\operatorname{fill}(n)^r\) whenever
   every gap is at least \(n-1\), with no limit needed; with gaps held in that regime the fill rate
   tends to \(0\) geometrically in \(r\) for \(n\ge4\).
2. *Zero shifts.* Write \(0^r\) for the shift vector of \(r\) zeros. Then
   \(\operatorname{fill}(n;0^r)\to1\) as \(r\to\infty\) (Corollary [[cor-zero-shift]]): in the dilation
   limit the ff-vectors fill the polytope to first order. The centroid and the covariance of the
   ff-vectors converge too, after scaling by \(1/r\) and \(1/r^2\), to those of \(\Koz(n)\)
   (Theorem [[thm-centroid-dilation]]), and so does the whole uniform measure
   (Remark [[rem-weak-convergence]]).
3. *Constant positive gap.* Both counts grow like the Perron roots of transfer matrices, Part II's
   for \(\FF\) and that of Corollary [[cor-transfer]] for the lattice points.

#+NAME: obs-fill
#+begin_observation
For a constant gap \(\delta\ge1\) the ratio of the two Perron roots is below \(1\) for \(n\ge4\), so
the fill rate tends to \(0\) geometrically: for \(n=4,5,6\) and \(\delta=1\) the ratios are
\(0.939\), \(0.890\), \(0.891\); for \(\delta=2\), \(0.796\), \(0.622\), \(0.609\). At rank two the
fill rate is not monotone in \(d\): it is smallest at \(d=n-2\), just before the product regime
(\(0.6373<0.64\) at \(n=4\), \(0.18596<0.18671\) at \(n=5\), \(0.053171<0.053219\) at \(n=6\)). Both
statements are verified by computation only, for \(n\le7\).
#+end_observation

# 2026-11-09 (Jan, review item 22: "Write out the lattice-point counting argument, have agent review it,
# formalise if possible. State the conjecture in the manuscript.") Rank r turned out provable, so a theorem.
# Source: ../working-notes/kupreyeva-limit-proof-2026-11-09.org; two independent reviews, rank one and rank r,
# both "correct as stated", their minor findings applied here. NOTE FOR JAN: not yet checked by you.
# 2026-11-10 (Fable review, "everything but C"): promoted from a subsection of sec-volume to a section
# (Part 2, Organisation 2). What Kupreyeva observed, the first two paragraphs of rem-kupreyeva-limit, now
# opens the section (Organisation 3), and the remark's truncation paragraph follows the definition it
# justifies (Organisation 4).
* Kupreyeva's limit
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: sec-kupreyeva
:END:

Kupreyeva [cite:@Kupreyeva2019 Section 6.2] studied a limit of a different kind: the number of
vertices grows while the size of the faces stays bounded. For fixed \(n\) she counted two things:
- the \(f\)-vectors with \(f_1=p\) and no face of more than \(n\) elements, her \(F^n_p\);
- the lattice points of their convex hull, projected to \((f_2,\dots,f_n)\), her \(T^n_p\).

She found closed forms for \(3\le n\le6\): a polynomial, and a quasi-polynomial in \(p\) of period
\(3\), \(12\), \(60\) and \(60\) for \(n=3,4,5,6\). She checked that the two have the same degree for
\(n\le30\), observed that the ratio of their leading coefficients is a number \(q_n\), and gave an
area argument at \(n=3\). Theorem [[thm-kupreyeva-limit]] proves the observation and explains
\(q_n\) as a volume ratio, and Theorem [[thm-kupreyeva-rank-r]] extends it to every rank.

In this section, as in [cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Section 9], \(p\ge n\) is the number of vertices and
\(n\) bounds the size of a face. Put
# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 1: "split"): one definition per line.
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kup-defs}
\begin{aligned}
  \vek c^{(p)}&=\Bigl(\binom p0,\binom p1,\dots,\binom pn\Bigr),\\
  \Koz^{(p)}(n)&=\rasimp\bigl(\vek c^{(p)}\bigr),\\
  \Koz^{(p)}(n;\vek d)&=\RA\bigl(\vek c^{(p)};\vek d\bigr).
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
#+end_export
At rank \(r\) this truncates every summand at its own degree \(n\), which is the faithful analogue.
The canonical decomposition commutes with this truncation, in the sense that padding the parts by
zeros gives the canonical decomposition of the padded point (Lemma [[lem-kup-truncation]]). The other
natural choice, truncation at global degree \(n\), gives the summands different leg vectors, and is
not covered by Theorem [[thm-chained]].

Let \(S_n^{(p)}\) be the set of \(f\)-vectors \((1,p,f_2,\dots,f_n)\) of the complexes on \([p]\)
that use every vertex and have no face of more than \(n\) elements, and put
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:kup-sets}
  \mathcal L_n^{(p)}=\Koz^{(p)}(n)\cap\mathbb Z^{n+1},\qquad
  \FF^{(p)}(n;\vek d)=\sum_{i=1}^r\shift^{d_i}\bigl(S_n^{(p)}\bigr).
\end{equation}
#+end_export
The latter is Part II's \(\FF^{(p)}_{\mathrm{pr}}(n;\vek d)\) [cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Theorem 60]. At \(p=n\) these are
\(\Koz(n)\), \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\), \(S_n\), \(\mathcal L_n\) and \(\FF(n;\vek d)\). In module terms,
\(\FF^{(p)}(n;\vek d)\) is the set of Hilbert functions of the quotients of
\(\bigoplus_i(E/E_{>n})g_i\), with \(E\) on \(p\) generators, by proper monomial submodules.

The leg vector \(\vek c^{(p)}\) is log-concave, so Theorem [[thm-chained]] and its corollaries hold
for \(\Koz^{(p)}(n;\vek d)\). The \(f\)-vectors of complexes on \([p]\) satisfy \(\mu_{k+1}\le\mu_k\)
in the LYM coordinates \eqref{eq:mu}, here \(\mu_k=f_k/\binom pk\): a face of size \(k+1\) has \(k+1\) faces of size
\(k\), and a face of size \(k\) lies in at most \(p-k\) faces of size \(k+1\). The vertices of
\(\Koz^{(p)}(n)\) are the \(f\)-vectors of the skeleta of the full simplex. Hence
\(\Koz^{(p)}(n)=\conv S_n^{(p)}\): Kupreyeva's \(F^n_p\) is \(S_n^{(p)}\), and her simplex
\(T^n_p\) is \(\Koz^{(p)}(n)\), whose lattice points are \(\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\). We write
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:fill-p}
  \operatorname{fill}^{(p)}(n;\vek d)=\frac{\lvert\FF^{(p)}(n;\vek d)\rvert}
  {\#\bigl(\Koz^{(p)}(n;\vek d)\cap\mathbb Z^{N+1}\bigr)} .
\end{equation}
#+end_export

In LYM coordinates \(\Koz^{(p)}(n)\) is, for every \(p\), the order simplex \(\Delta_n\) of
\eqref{eq:Rn} below (Proposition [[prop-rank-one-H]]). The Kruskal--Katona bound \eqref{eq:kk-cond},
\(f_{k+1}\le f_k^{(k)}\), with the pseudo-power \eqref{eq:kk}, becomes
\(\mu_{k+1}\le\mu_k^{(k+1)/k}\) to leading order in \(p\) (Lemma [[lem-pseudo-power]]). This cuts the
region \(R_n\) out of \(\Delta_n\):
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Rn}
\begin{aligned}
  \Delta_n&=\bigl\lbrace\mu\in\mathbb R^{n-1}:1\ge\mu_2\ge\dots\ge\mu_n\ge0\bigr\rbrace,\\
  R_n&=\bigl\lbrace\mu\in\mathbb R^{n-1}:0\le\mu_2\le1,\ 0\le\mu_{k+1}\le\mu_k^{(k+1)/k}
  \ (2\le k\le n-1)\bigr\rbrace .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
#+end_export
The region \(R_n\) is not convex: its upper boundary is the graph of a convex function.

#+NAME: thm-kupreyeva-limit
#+begin_theorem
\leanmark{\leannone}(/Kupreyeva's limit./) Let \(n\ge2\). As \(p\to\infty\),
\[
  \bigl\lvert\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\bigr\rvert\sim\vol(\Delta_n)\prod_{k=2}^n\binom pk,\qquad
  \bigl\lvert S_n^{(p)}\bigr\rvert\sim\vol(R_n)\prod_{k=2}^n\binom pk,
\]
with \(\vol\Delta_n=1/(n-1)!\) and
\[
  \vol R_n=\prod_{k=2}^{n-1}\frac{2k}{(n-k+1)(n+k)}=\frac{2^{n-2}\,(n+1)!}{(2n-1)!}.
\]
Consequently
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:qn}
  \lim_{p\to\infty}\frac{\lvert S_n^{(p)}\rvert}{\lvert\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\rvert}
  =\frac{\vol R_n}{\vol\Delta_n}=q_n=\frac{(n+1)!}{2\,(2n-1)!!},
\end{equation}
#+end_export
so \(q_2=1\), \(q_3=4/5\), \(q_4=4/7\), \(q_5=8/21\) and \(q_6=8/33\).
#+end_theorem

The proof is in Appendix [[#sec-app-kupreyeva]], and it was found by the language model (see the
disclosure section). It has three steps.
1. In LYM coordinates the lattice \(\mathbb Z^{n-1}\) becomes \(\prod_k\binom pk^{-1}\mathbb Z\),
   whose mesh tends to \(0\) in every coordinate. So the lattice points of a fixed region with null
   boundary, counted with weight \(\prod_k\binom pk^{-1}\), tend to its volume
   (Lemma [[lem-anisotropic-count]]).
2. The pseudo-power satisfies \(f^{(k)}/\binom p{k+1}=(f/\binom pk)^{(k+1)/k}+o(1)\), uniformly in
   \(0\le f\le\binom pk\) (Lemma [[lem-pseudo-power]]). This squeezes the \(f\)-vectors between the
   lattice points of two regions whose volumes tend to \(\vol R_n\).
3. \(\vol R_n\) is an iterated integral (Lemma [[lem-rho]]).

The proof uses neither Theorem [[thm-chained]] nor Part II. At \(n=3\) the value \(4/5\) is already
in Kupreyeva's closed forms, and she also obtained it by an interpolation-and-area argument
[cite:@Kupreyeva2019 Section 4.1]; here \(R_3\) has area \(\int_0^1t^{3/2}\,dt=2/5\) against \(1/2\) for
the triangle \(\Delta_3\). A second proof, from Linusson's enumeration of \(f\)-vectors
[cite:@Linusson1999], follows the first in the appendix, and Remark [[rem-kupreyeva-linusson]] there
combines the two into the rate: \(\lvert S_n^{(p)}\rvert/\lvert\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\rvert=q_n+O(1/p)\).

The rank-\(r\) analogue holds for every shift vector. Group the shifts by value, into \(g\)
distinct values \(D_j\) with multiplicities \(m_j\), and put
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:aj}
\begin{gathered}
  \lbrace d_1,\dots,d_r\rbrace=\lbrace D_1<\dots<D_g\rbrace,\qquad m_j=\#\lbrace i:d_i=D_j\rbrace,\\
  a_1=2,\qquad a_j=\max(2,\,n-D_j+D_{j-1}+1)\quad(j\ge2),\qquad \ell_j=n+1-a_j .
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
#+end_export
Here \(a_j-1\) is the own degree up to which, for almost every lattice point, the summands with shift
\(D_j\) are full: below \(a_j\) they are constant (\(j=1\)) or held full by the summands of the
previous shift (\(j\ge2\)); \(\ell_j\) is the number of the remaining own degrees
\(a_j,\dots,n\), in which they are free. For \(2\le a\le b\) let
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:rho}
  \rho(a,b)=\prod_{k=a}^{b-1}\frac{2k}{(b-k+1)(b+k)}
\end{equation}
#+end_export
be the volume of \(\lbrace0\le\mu_a\le1,\ 0\le\mu_{k+1}\le\mu_k^{(k+1)/k}\ (a\le k<b)\rbrace\), and
put \(\rho(b+1,b)=1\). For \(m\ge1\) let
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Phi}
  \Phi_n(a;m)=\sum_{a-1=s_0\le s_1\le\dots\le s_m=n}\ \prod_{l=1}^m\rho(s_{l-1}+1,s_l) .
\end{equation}
#+end_export

#+NAME: thm-kupreyeva-rank-r
#+begin_theorem
\leanmark{\leannone}(/Kupreyeva's limit at rank \(r\)./) For every \(n\ge2\) and every shift vector \(\vek d\),
\[
  \lim_{p\to\infty}\operatorname{fill}^{(p)}(n;\vek d)
  =\prod_{j=1}^g\frac{\ell_j!\,\Phi_n(a_j;m_j)}{m_j^{\ell_j}} .
\]
In particular:
1. If every gap \(\delta_i\) is positive, the limit is \(q_n\prod_{i=2}^rq(n,\delta_i)\), where
   #+begin_export latex
   \begin{equation}\label{eq:qndelta}
     q(n,\delta)=\frac{2^{\delta-1}\,(n-1)!\,(2n-\delta)!}{(n-\delta)!\,(2n-1)!}
     \quad(1\le\delta\le n-1),\qquad q(n,\delta)=q_n\quad(\delta\ge n-1).
   \end{equation}
   #+end_export
   So \(q(n,1)=1\), and in the product regime the limit is \(q_n^r\).
2. If \(\vek d=(0,\dots,0)\), the limit is
   \(\vol(R_n+\dots+R_n)/\vol(r\Delta_n)=(n-1)!\,\Phi_n(2;r)/r^{n-1}\), the Minkowski sum having
   \(r\) terms. For \(n=3\) it is \(1-1/(5r)\).
#+end_theorem

In words: at a positive gap \(\delta\), almost every lattice point has its later summand full in
its own degrees up to \(n-\delta\). Only the top \(\min(\delta,n-1)\) degrees are free, and the
Kruskal--Katona conditions on them form a rank-one problem on fewer coordinates, with limit
\(q(n,\delta)\). At equal shifts the summands share their coordinates, and the canonical
decomposition stacks them, so the limit is a Minkowski sum of copies of the non-convex region
\(R_n\). As \(r\to\infty\) that ratio tends to \(1\), the \(p\)-direction counterpart of
Corollary [[cor-zero-shift]]. The proof is in Appendix [[#sec-app-kupreyeva]]. It uses
Corollary [[cor-membership]], with \(p\) in place of \(n\), and so rests on Part II's chaining
reformulation; the volume formula at equal shifts also uses Theorem [[thm-staircase]].

#+NAME: ex-kupreyeva-values
#+begin_mexample
For \(n=3\), Theorem [[thm-kupreyeva-rank-r]] gives the limits \(9/10\), \(4/5\) and \(16/25\) at
\(\vek d=(0,0)\), \((0,1)\) and \((0,2)\), and \(14/15\) and \(4/5\) at \((0,0,0)\) and
\((0,1,2)\). For \(n=4\) it gives \(107/140\), \(4/7\), \(24/49\) and \(16/49\) at \(\vek d=(0,0)\),
\((0,1)\), \((0,2)\) and \((0,3)\), and \(88/105\) at \((0,0,0)\). Among these only the product regime
gives \(q_n^r\). For \(n\ge3\) and positive gaps this is general: \(q(n,\delta+1)/q(n,\delta)=
2(n-\delta)/(2n-\delta)<1\), so \(q(n,\delta)>q_n\) for \(\delta<n-1\), and the limit exceeds \(q_n^r\)
unless every gap is at least \(n-1\). At \(\vek d=(0,1)\) the limit is \(q_n\) itself, because the shifted summand is
generically full except in its top degree, where Kruskal--Katona imposes nothing.

We compared these limits with the exact fill rates in eighteen cases: rank one for \(n=3,4,5\),
and fifteen shift vectors of rank \(2\) to \(4\) for \(n=3,4\), up to \(p=30000\) for \(n=3\),
\(p=2000\) for \(n=4\) and \(p=120\) for \(n=5\). In every case
\(p\bigl(\operatorname{fill}^{(p)}(n;\vek d)-\lim\bigr)\) settles to a constant, so the error
appears to be of order \(1/p\); Figure \ref{fig:kupreyeva-fill} shows ten of the cases. In all
eighteen the approach is from below, and slow. It need not be: at \(n=4\) and
\(\vek d=(0,1,1)\), not among them, it is from above. At rank one the constant is about \(-0.80\) for \(n=3\), \(-1.66\) for \(n=4\) and
\(-2.19\) for \(n=5\). For comparison, at \(p=n=5\) the fraction is \(0.43\) and at \(p=10\) it is
\(0.18\), against \(q_5\approx0.38\); the asymptotic regime starts later.
#+end_mexample

# 2026-11-13 (Jan, sweep item 7: "could there be a plot of the fill-rates approaching their asymptotic
# limits accompanying ex-kupreyeva-values?"). Made by ../code/oneoff/fig_kupreyeva_fill_convergence.py,
# exact counts with the functions of kupreyeva_limit_rank_r.py; numbers in
# ../runs/2026-11-13_kupreyeva-fill-convergence.txt, which also checks the (0,1,1) sentence above.
# The same edit corrected "fifteen shift vectors ... in all": the 2026-11-09 run has eighteen cases.
#+begin_export latex
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{img/fig-kupreyeva-fill.pdf}
\caption{Kupreyeva's limit (Example~\ref{ex-kupreyeva-values}): the fill rate
$\operatorname{fill}^{(p)}(n;\vek d)$ of \eqref{eq:fill-p} against the number $p$ of vertices,
on a logarithmic scale, for $n=3$ (left), $n=4$ (right) and five shift vectors, from exact counts.
Top: the dashed lines are the limits of Theorem~\ref{thm-kupreyeva-rank-r}, labelled on the right;
at $n=3$ the curves for $(0)$ and $(0,1)$, both with limit $q_3=4/5$, almost coincide. The left
ends, at $p=n$, are the fill rates $\operatorname{fill}(n;\vek d)$ of Section~\ref{sec-volume}.
Bottom: $p$ times the error, which settles to a negative constant: the approach is from below, at
rate $1/p$.}
\label{fig:kupreyeva-fill}
\end{figure}
#+end_export

#+NAME: rem-kupreyeva-limit
#+begin_remark
(/The limits in \(p\) and in \(n\)./) Since \((2n-1)!!=(2n)!/(2^nn!)\), the value \eqref{eq:qn} is
\(q_n=2^{n-1}(n+1)/\binom{2n}n\), and Stirling's formula, through \(\binom{2n}n\sim4^n/\sqrt{\pi n}\),
gives \(q_n\sim(n+1)\sqrt{\pi n}/2^{n+1}\). The limits \(p\to\infty\) and \(n\to\infty\) do not
commute in any simple way: the fill rate at \(p=n\) falls much faster (Table \ref{tab:fill}: \(\operatorname{fill}(8)\approx0.027\) against
\(q_8=64/715\approx0.090\)).
#+end_remark

* Ehrhart theory
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: sec-ehrhart
:END:

For a lattice polytope \(P\) of dimension \(D\), the Ehrhart polynomial \(L_P\) and the
\(h^*\)-polynomial \(h^*_P\) are given by
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ehrhart}
  L_P(t)=\#\bigl(tP\cap\mathbb Z^{N+1}\bigr),\qquad
  \sum_{t\ge0}L_P(t)z^t=\frac{h^*_P(z)}{(1-z)^{D+1}} .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
\(L_P\) is a polynomial of degree \(D\), whose leading coefficient is the relative volume of \(P\):
its volume normalised so that a fundamental domain of the lattice parallel to the affine hull of
\(P\) has volume \(1\). For the Kozlov polytopes this is \(\vol P\), as in Section [[#sec-volume]].
# 2026-11-10 (Jan, round 5): reciprocity stated here, once. It was used in prop-hstar-props (2), (3)
# and cor-gorenstein without being stated. Locators checked against the authors' final draft of the
# second edition (Theorem 4.1 Ehrhart-Macdonald, 4.4 series form, 4.5 degree and smallest dilate).
The coefficients of \(h^*_P\) are non-negative. Write
\(L^\circ_P(t)=\#\bigl(t\relint P\cap\mathbb Z^{N+1}\bigr)\) for the number of lattice points in the
relative interior of \(tP\). /Ehrhart--Macdonald reciprocity/ says that
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:reciprocity}
  L_P(-t)=(-1)^DL^\circ_P(t)\quad(t\ge1),\qquad
  \sum_{t\ge1}L^\circ_P(t)z^t=\frac{z^{D+1}h^*_P(1/z)}{(1-z)^{D+1}} .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
The *codegree* of \(P\) is the least \(t\ge1\) for which \(tP\) has an interior lattice point,
that is, with \(L^\circ_P(t)>0\). Let \(s=\deg h^*_P\). The lowest term on the right of
\eqref{eq:reciprocity} is \(h^*_sz^{D+1-s}\), so the codegree is \(D+1-s\), and the number of
interior lattice points of that dilate is the top coefficient \(h^*_s\). For all of this see
[cite:@BeckRobins2015 Chapters 3 and 4, in particular Theorems 4.1, 4.4 and 4.5]. We call \(P\)
*Gorenstein* if \(h^*_P\) is palindromic, \(h^*_i=h^*_{s-i}\); by a theorem of Stanley this is
equivalent to the Ehrhart ring of \(P\) being a Gorenstein ring [cite:@Stanley1996].

** The Kozlov simplex

#+NAME: thm-hstar
#+begin_theorem
\leanmark{\leannone}Write \(c_k=\binom nk\) (or take any integral leg vector). For residue vectors \(\vek z=(z_2,\dots,z_n)\) with
\(0\le z_k<c_k\), put \(\rho_1=0\), \(\rho_k=z_k/c_k\), and
\(\asc(\vek z)=\#\lbrace k\in[1,n-1]:\rho_k<\rho_{k+1}\rbrace\). Then
\(h^*_i(\Koz(n))=\#\lbrace\vek z:\asc(\vek z)=i\rbrace\).
#+end_theorem

This is the specialisation to \(\Koz(n)\) of Savage and Schuster's formula for the \(h^*\)-polynomial
of a lecture hall polytope as an \(\vek s\)-Eulerian polynomial [cite:@SavageSchuster2012], in the
form in which it is quoted in [cite:@SavageVisontai2015;@HibiOlsenTsuchiya2018]; we include the
short proof, which is the standard fundamental-parallelepiped count.

#+begin_proof
Cone over the projected simplex in \(\mathbb R\times\mathbb R^{n-1}\), with generators
\(w_j=(1,c_2,\dots,c_j,0,\dots,0)\), \(j=1,\dots,n\). For a lattice simplex, \(h^*_i\) is the number
of lattice points at height \(i\) in \(\Pi=\lbrace\sum\lambda_jw_j:0\le\lambda_j<1\rbrace\)
[cite:@BeckRobins2015 Chapter 3]. A point \(\sum\lambda_jw_j\) has coordinates \(x_k=c_k\mu_k\) with
\(\mu_k=\sum_{j\ge k}\lambda_j\) for \(k\ge2\), and height \(t=\lambda_1+\mu_2\); it is integral iff
\(t\in\mathbb Z\) and \(\mu_k\in\rho_k+\mathbb Z\), where \(\rho_k=(x_k\bmod c_k)/c_k\). Conversely the
residues determine the point: \(\lambda_k=\mu_k-\mu_{k+1}\in[0,1)\) with \(\mu_{n+1}=0\) forces
\(\mu_n=\rho_n\) and, descending, \(\mu_k=\rho_k+m_k\) with
\(m_k=m_{k+1}+[\rho_k<\rho_{k+1}]\); and \(\lambda_1=t-\mu_2\in[0,1)\) forces
\(t=\lceil\mu_2\rceil=m_2+[\rho_2>0]=\asc(\vek z)\). So \(x\mapsto\vek z\) is a bijection from
\(\Pi\cap\mathbb Z^n\) onto \(\prod_k\mathbb Z/c_k\), and the height is \(\asc(\vek z)\).
#+end_proof

# 2026-11-10 (Fable review, Part 2, Organisation 14): was a parenthesis after the proof.
#+NAME: rem-hstar-misprint
#+begin_remark
(/A misprint in the literature./) Some restatements of Theorem [[thm-hstar]], for instance
[cite:@HibiOlsenTsuchiya2018 Lemma 1.1], count ascents of the residue vector after an auxiliary
entry \(e_0\) with \(s_0=1\), whose ratio \(e_0/s_0\) plays the part of our \(\rho_1\). They print the
convention \(e_0=1\) where \(e_0=0\), that is \(\rho_1=0\), is meant; with \(e_0=1\) one gets
\((14,10)\) instead of \((1,16,7)\) at \(n=4\), with \(\vek s=(6,4,1)\). (In the order \((1,4,6)\) of
Proposition [[prop-lecture-hall]] the two conventions agree.)
#+end_remark

#+NAME: ex-hstar
#+begin_mexample
At \(n=3\) (dimension \(2\)), \((c_2,c_3)=(3,1)\): \(z_3=0\), and \(\asc=[0<\rho_2]\), so \(h^*=(1,2)\) and
\(L_{\Koz(3)}(t)=(t+1)(3t+2)/2=1,5,12,22,35,\dots\). At \(n=4\), \((c_2,c_3,c_4)=(6,4,1)\), \(24\) residue vectors:
\(h^*=(1,16,7)\) and \(L_{\Koz(4)}(t)=(t+1)^2(4t+1)\). The \(h^*\)-vectors for \(5\le n\le8\), of dimension \(n-1\), are
#+begin_export latex
\[\small
\begin{array}{ll}
n=5: & (1,157,308,34)\\
n=6: & (1,1975,14184,10253,587)\\
n=7: & (1,60986,1054938,2043353,607906,14391)\\
n=8: & (1,3576516,143878949,624510000,523655643,80268620,939711).
\end{array}
\]
#+end_export
The sums are \(\NV(\Koz(n))=\prod_{k\ge2}\binom nk\), and \(h^*_1=\lvert\mathcal L_n\rvert-n\). The
sequence \(\lvert\mathcal L_n\rvert=1,2,5,20,162,1981,60993,3576524\) (\(n=1,\dots,8\)), the number of
lattice points of the Kozlov simplex, was not in the OEIS [cite:@OEIS] when checked in October 2026.
#+end_mexample

# 2026-11-10 (Fable review, Part 2, Organisation 6 and Style 21): lem-interior-points moved here from the
# codegree subsection, so that prop-hstar-props (2)-(3) can use it, and part (2) now names its two points
# itself, for every m >= 2 at once; the old proof went through m = 2 and Lemma lem-relint-sum.
# NOTE FOR JAN: the direct proof of (2) is new (round 4) and not yet checked by you.
#+NAME: lem-interior-points
#+begin_lemma
\leanmark{\leannone}Let \(n\ge3\) and \(c_k=\binom nk\). For \(2\le j\le n\) let \(y_j=1\), let
\(y_k=\lfloor c_ky_{k+1}/c_{k+1}\rfloor+1\) for \(k=j-1,\dots,2\), downward, and let \(y_k=0\) for
\(k>j\).
1. If \(j\le n-1\), the projection of \(\Koz(n)\) to the coordinates \(x_0,\dots,x_j\) is the
   \((j-1)\)-simplex \(\lbrace1=\mu_1\ge\mu_2\ge\dots\ge\mu_j\ge0\rbrace\), and
   \((1,n,y_2,\dots,y_n)\) is a lattice point of \(\Koz(n)\) whose projection lies in the relative
   interior of that simplex.
2. If \(j=n\), then for every \(m\ge2\) the two lattice points \((m,mn,y_2,y_3,\dots,y_n)\) and
   \((m,mn,y_2+1,y_3,\dots,y_n)\) lie in the relative interior of \(m\Koz(n)\).
#+end_lemma

#+begin_proof
In both parts \(\mu_j=y_j/c_j=1/c_j>0\); \(\mu_k>\mu_{k+1}\) for \(2\le k<j\) by the choice of
\(y_k\); and \(\mu_k\le\mu_{k+1}+1/c_k\), so \(\mu_2\le1/c_j+\sum_{k=2}^{j-1}1/c_k\le1/c_j+(j-2)/n\),
since \(c_k\ge n\) for \(1\le k\le n-1\).
(1) The projection sends \(\tilde F_1,\dots,\tilde F_j\) to their truncations and
\(\tilde F_{j+1},\dots,\tilde F_n\) to that of \(\tilde F_j\); by Proposition [[prop-rank-one-H]] the
hull is the chain set stated, with barycentric coordinates \(\mu_k-\mu_{k+1}\) (\(\mu_{j+1}=0\)), so its
relative interior is \(1>\mu_2>\dots>\mu_j>0\). Here \(c_j\ge n\), so \(\mu_2\le(j-1)/n<1\). Setting
\(\mu_{j+1}=\dots=\mu_n=0\) keeps the chain, so the point lies in \(\Koz(n)\).
(2) By Proposition [[prop-rank-one-H]], the relative interior of \(m\Koz(n)\) is the set of points
with \(x_0=m\), \(x_1=mn\) and \(m>\mu_2>\dots>\mu_n>0\). For the first point \(c_n=1\), so
\(\mu_2\le1+(n-2)/n\). For the second, \(\mu_2\) is larger by \(1/c_2=2/(n(n-1))\), which keeps
\(\mu_2>\mu_3\), and \(1+(n-2)/n+2/(n(n-1))<2\), an inequality equivalent to \(n>2\). So both points
satisfy the strict chain, with \(\mu_2<2\le m\).
#+end_proof

# 2026-11-13 (sweep, inventory): part (4) used w_j, defined only in the proof of thm-hstar; now it
# says so. Same sweep: fill(n), used in sec-volume and rem-kupreyeva-limit, is now in eq:fill; and
# thm-beta-continuous' proof said x -> Lx + b with L undefined, now beta: x -> bar-beta x + b.
#+NAME: prop-hstar-props
#+begin_proposition
\leanmark{\leannone}Let \(n\ge3\).
1. \(h^*_{\Koz(n)}\) is real-rooted, hence log-concave and unimodal [cite:@SavageVisontai2015].
2. \(\Koz(n)\) has no interior lattice point and \(2\Koz(n)\) has one: the codegree is \(2\) and
   \(\deg h^*=n-2\). Consequently \((t+1)\) divides \(L_{\Koz(n)}(t)\).
3. \(\Koz(n)\) is not Gorenstein.
4. The fundamental-parallelepiped group \(\mathbb Z^n/\langle w_j\rangle\), with the cone generators
   \(w_j\) of the proof of Theorem [[thm-hstar]], is \(\bigoplus_{k=2}^n\mathbb Z/\binom nk\).
#+end_proposition

#+begin_proof
(2) On the relative interior the valid inequalities \(x_n\ge0\) and \(x_{n-1}\le c_{n-1}=n\) are
strict, since neither is tight on all of \(\Koz(n)\); for a lattice point that means \(x_n\ge1\) and
\(x_{n-1}\le n-1\), contradicting \(x_n\le x_{n-1}/n\). So \(\Koz(n)\) has no interior lattice point,
while \(2\Koz(n)\) has one by Lemma [[lem-interior-points]](2). Reciprocity \eqref{eq:reciprocity}
gives \(L(-1)=\pm L^\circ(1)=0\).
# 2026-11-09 (Jan, round-2 report item 20.1: "simplify the proof as much as possible"): the n = 3 case
# split is gone. 2026-11-10: the two points are now those of Lemma lem-interior-points(2).
(3) By Lemma [[lem-interior-points]](2), \(2\Koz(n)\) has at least two interior lattice points. By
\eqref{eq:reciprocity} their number is \(h^*_{n-2}\), whereas a Gorenstein polytope has palindromic \(h^*\), so
\(h^*_{n-2}=h^*_0=1\). (This is the case \(r=1\) of Corollary [[cor-gorenstein]].)
(4) The basis change \(w_1\), \(w_j-w_{j-1}=c_je_j\) diagonalises the lattice.
#+end_proof

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 1: "split"): both polynomials displayed; inline they broke across lines.
# The observation no longer opens with a formula.
The polynomials \(L_{\Koz(n)}\) have positive coefficients for \(n\le6\); for example
\[
  L_{\Koz(5)}(t)=\tfrac16(t+1)(125t^3+250t^2+105t+6).
\]

#+NAME: obs-positivity
#+begin_observation
The Ehrhart polynomial of \(\Koz(7)\) is
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation*}
\begin{split}
  L_{\Koz(7)}(t)={}&1-594t-\tfrac{5397}8t^2+\tfrac{173313}{16}t^3\\
  &+\tfrac{414687}{16}t^4+\tfrac{324135}{16}t^5+\tfrac{84035}{16}t^6
\end{split}
\end{equation*}
#+end_export
(computed by the transfer matrix of Corollary [[cor-transfer]] and independently by Normaliz), so
\(\Koz(7)\) is not Ehrhart positive. Lecture hall simplices that are not Ehrhart positive were first exhibited by Liu and Solus
[cite:@LiuSolus2019 Theorem 4.3], and a second family, \(\vek s=(a,\dots,a,a+1)\), by Caicedo,
Juhnke and Poullot [cite:@CaicedoJuhnkePoullot2025 Theorem 3.5]; \(\Koz(7)\), with
\(\vek s=(21,35,35,21,7,1)\), belongs to neither family and is a natural new instance. By
Proposition [[prop-linear-coeff]] below, no \(\Koz(7;\vek d)\) is Ehrhart positive.
#+end_observation

** Lattice points and Ehrhart polynomials by transfer matrix

Let \(A^{\mathcal L}[\tau][\iota]=\#\lbrace x\in\mathcal L_n:\tp(x)=\tau,\ \indeg(x)=\iota\rbrace\), and
build from it \(T^{\mathcal L}(\delta)\) and \(\vek u^{\mathcal L}\) exactly as Part II builds
\(T'_n(\delta)\) and \(\vek u'_n\) from the counts on \(S_n\)
[cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Definition 28 and Theorem 41]:
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:transfer}
\begin{aligned}
  T^{\mathcal L}(\delta)[\tau][\tau']&=\sum_{\iota>\tau-\delta}A^{\mathcal L}[\tau'][\iota]
  =\#\lbrace x\in\mathcal L_n:\tp(x)=\tau',\ \indeg(x)+\delta>\tau\rbrace,\\
  u^{\mathcal L}[\tau]&=\sum_\iota A^{\mathcal L}[\tau][\iota]=\#\lbrace x\in\mathcal L_n:\tp(x)=\tau\rbrace .
\end{aligned}
\end{equation}
#+end_export
In the next corollary \(\vek u^{\mathcal L}\) is a row vector and \(\vek 1\) the all-ones column vector
of length \(n\), both indexed by \(\tau\in[1,n]\) like the rows of \(T^{\mathcal L}(\delta)\).

#+NAME: cor-transfer
#+begin_corollary
\leanmark{\leanfull}\(\#\bigl(\Koz(n;\vek d)\cap\mathbb Z^{N+1}\bigr)=\vek u^{\mathcal L}\,T^{\mathcal L}(\delta_2)\cdots
T^{\mathcal L}(\delta_r)\,\vek 1\), and, with \(T_0=T^{\mathcal L}(0)\),
\[
  L_{\Koz(n;\vek d)}(t)=\vek u^{\mathcal L}\,T_0^{\,t-1}\,T^{\mathcal L}(\delta_2)\,T_0^{\,t-1}\,T^{\mathcal L}(\delta_3)\cdots
  T^{\mathcal L}(\delta_r)\,T_0^{\,t-1}\,\vek 1 .
\]
#+end_corollary

#+begin_proof
By Corollary [[cor-additivity]], lattice points are in bijection with chained \(\mathcal L_n\)-tuples,
and counting chained tuples by a transfer-matrix product is Part II's rearrangement
[cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Section 7], which uses nothing about \(S_n\). The dilate is
\(t\Koz(n;\vek d)=\Koz(n;\vek d^{\times t})\), with \(\vek d^{\times t}\) as in the proof of
Corollary [[cor-idp]].
#+end_proof

\(T_0\) is upper unitriangular: a non-zero entry needs \(\tau'\ge\tau\), because
\(\indeg\le\tp+1\); the diagonal entry counts only the vertex \(\tilde F_\tau\); and the superdiagonal
entry is \(T_0[\tau][\tau+1]=c_{\tau+1}\), the choices of \(x_{\tau+1}\in[1,c_{\tau+1}]\). The same holds
for Part II's \(T'_n(0)\) on its principal block \(\tau\in[1,n]\) (Part II, Lemma 44), since a full \(\tau\)-skeleton plus any \(m\le\binom n{\tau+1}\) faces of the
next size is a complex. We write
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:T0}
  T_0=T^{\mathcal L}(0)=I+\mathcal N ,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
so that \(\mathcal N\) is strictly upper triangular, with superdiagonal \((c_2,\dots,c_n)\).

#+NAME: cor-zero-shift
#+begin_corollary
\leanmark{\leanpartial}\(L_{\Koz(n)}(t)=\vek u^{\mathcal L}T_0^{\,t-1}\vek 1\), a polynomial of degree \(n-1\) with leading
coefficient \(\prod_{k\ge2}\binom nk/(n-1)!\). The count
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ar}
  a(r)=\bigl\lvert\FF(n;0^r)\bigr\rvert ,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
is a polynomial in \(r\) with the same degree and the same leading coefficient
[cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Theorem 36]. Hence
\(\operatorname{fill}(n;0^r)=a(r)/L_{\Koz(n)}(r)\to1\) as \(r\to\infty\).
#+end_corollary

#+begin_proof
By [cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Theorem 41], \(a(r)=\vek u'_n\,T'_n(0)^{r-1}\vek 1\), and by
Corollary [[cor-transfer]], \(L_{\Koz(n)}(t)=\vek u^{\mathcal L}T_0^{\,t-1}\vek 1\).
By \eqref{eq:T0}, \(T_0^{t-1}=\sum_k\binom{t-1}k\mathcal N^k\), and \(\mathcal N^{n-1}\) has the single entry
\(\prod_\tau\mathcal N[\tau][\tau+1]=\prod_{k\ge2}c_k\) at \((1,n)\). Both \(\vek u^{\mathcal L}\) and
Part II's \(\vek u'_n\), restricted to that block, have first entry \(1\), the only point with \(\tp=1\) being \((1,n,0,\dots,0)\),
and both superdiagonals are \((c_2,\dots,c_n)\).
#+end_proof

The statement about \(a(r)\), degree and leading coefficient included, is
[cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Theorem 36]; what is new is the lattice-point count and the comparison.
Since \(\Koz(n;0^r)=r\Koz(n)\), the difference \(L_{\Koz(n)}(r)-a(r)\) counts the lattice points of
the dilate that are not ff-vectors. By Corollary [[cor-zero-shift]] it is a polynomial in \(r\) of degree at most
\(n-2\), and it vanishes for \(n\le3\) by Corollary [[cor-small-n]]. For \(n=4\) and \(n=5\) it
is therefore decided by four of its values.

# 2026-11-05, at Jan's request: was the sentence "At n = 4, a(r) = ... and L(r) = ...", with the
# deficiency 2r(r+1) only observed for r <= 10 in sec-centroid. The n = 5 case is new; values from
# code/oneoff/zero_shift_deficiency.py (independent of the transfer matrices).
#+NAME: cor-deficiency
#+begin_corollary
\leanmark{\leannone}For every \(r\ge1\),
#+begin_export latex
\[
  \begin{alignedat}{2}
  n=4:&\qquad& a(r)&=(r+1)(4r^2+3r+1),\\
      &&       L_{\Koz(4)}(r)&=(r+1)^2(4r+1),\\
      &&       L_{\Koz(4)}(r)-a(r)&=2r(r+1);\\[4pt]
  n=5:&&       a(r)&=\tfrac16(r+1)(125r^3+45r^2+34r+6),\\
      &&       L_{\Koz(5)}(r)&=\tfrac16(r+1)(125r^3+250r^2+105r+6),\\
      &&       L_{\Koz(5)}(r)-a(r)&=\tfrac16r(r+1)(205r+71).
  \end{alignedat}
\]
#+end_export
#+end_corollary

#+ATTR_LATEX: :options [Proof (computational)]
#+begin_proof
In the proof of Corollary [[cor-zero-shift]], \(T_0\) and \(T'_n(0)\) are upper unitriangular, so
\(a(r)\) and \(L_{\Koz(n)}(r)\) are \(\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{r-1}k w_k\) for constants \(w_k\): polynomials
in \(r\) of degree at most \(n-1\), for every \(r\ge1\), with equal leading coefficients. A polynomial
of degree at most \(n-1\) is determined by \(n\) values. At \(r=1,\dots,5\),
\(a=16,69,184,385,696\) and \(L_{\Koz(4)}=20,81,208,425,756\) for \(n=4\), and
\(a=70,627,2592,7385,16926\) and \(L_{\Koz(5)}=162,1108,3964,10355,22406\) for \(n=5\); the stated
polynomials take these values. The values were computed independently of the transfer matrices,
\(a(r)\) as the \(r\)-fold sumset of \(S_n\) listed by the Kruskal--Katona theorem and
\(L_{\Koz(n)}(r)\) as the number of integer points \(x\) with \(x_0=r\), \(x_1=nr\) and
\(r\ge x_2/\binom n2\ge\dots\ge x_n/\binom nn\ge0\) (Proposition [[prop-rank-one-H]]); see
Section [[#sec-methods]].
#+end_proof

At \(n=5\) the cubic through the differences at \(r=1,\dots,4\) predicts the fifth, \(5480\), as the
degree bound says it must.

# 2026-11-10 (Fable review, Part 2, Organisation 5): the subsection "Rank two and higher" is split in three,
# examples first; obs-hstar-unimodal closes the section.
** Rank two and higher: examples

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 3: "complete sentences, maybe present first part in tabular form").
# The three polynomials are now a table with the dimension D of eq:dim; "not N = 4" dropped (D <= N-1
# always, so N was no real contrast); h*(Koz(3)) = (1,2) is now cited from ex-hstar, so that "not
# (1,2)^2" can be read. Numbers unchanged; checked 2026-11-14: L(1) = 22, 25, 214 are the lattice-point
# counts of runs/2026-10-20_article3-overnight-push/2026-10-20_centroids_moments.txt, and the
# h*-vectors of the second table give these L.
#+NAME: ex-ehrhart-rank2
#+begin_mexample
Three Ehrhart polynomials at rank two, with the dimension \(D\) of each polytope from \eqref{eq:dim}:
#+begin_export latex
\[\small
\begin{array}{lcl}
 & D & L(t)\\[2pt]
\Koz(3;0,1) & 3 & 1+5t+10t^2+6t^3\\
\Koz(3;0,2) & 4 & 1+5t+\tfrac{37}4t^2+\tfrac{15}2t^3+\tfrac94t^4\\
\Koz(4;0,1) & 4 & 1+12t+54t^2+95t^3+52t^4
\end{array}
\]
#+end_export
In each case the degree of the polynomial is the dimension of the polytope. The polytope
\(\Koz(3;0,2)\) is in the product regime, so its Ehrhart polynomial is the square of
\(L_{\Koz(3)}(t)=\tfrac12(t+1)(3t+2)\) (Proposition [[prop-product-regime]] and Example [[ex-hstar]]). Its
\(h^*\)-vector \((1,20,29,4)\) is not the square \((1,4,4)\) of \(h^*_{\Koz(3)}=(1,2)\): the Ehrhart
polynomial of a product is the product of the Ehrhart polynomials, but the \(h^*\)-polynomial is not.
The linear coefficients are \(5=2\cdot\tfrac52\) for the two polytopes with \(n=3\) and \(12=2\cdot6\)
for \(\Koz(4;0,1)\), twice those of \(L_{\Koz(3)}\) and \(L_{\Koz(4)}\), as Proposition [[prop-linear-coeff]]
below requires.

The next table lists \(h^*\)-vectors at rank two and three, each with the dimension \(D\) of its
polytope:
#+begin_export latex
\[\small
\begin{array}{lcl@{\qquad}lcl}
 & D & h^* & & D & h^*\\[2pt]
\Koz(3;0,0) & 2 & (1,9,2) & \Koz(4;0,0) & 3 & (1,77,107,7)\\
\Koz(3;0,1) & 3 & (1,18,17) & \Koz(4;0,1) & 4 & (1,209,773,265)\\
\Koz(3;0,2) & 4 & (1,20,29,4) & \Koz(4;0,2) & 5 & (1,380,2940,3008,391)\\
\Koz(3;0,1,2) & 4 & (1,90,318,113) & \Koz(4;0,3) & 6 & (1,393,3782,5702,1593,49)\\
 & & & \Koz(4;0,1,2) & 5 & (1,2052,22428,29456,5103).
\end{array}
\]
#+end_export
#+end_mexample

** Linear and quadratic coefficients

The proofs of the next two propositions stay inside the transfer matrix. They rest on one identity
between the matrices of Corollary [[cor-transfer]], indexed by \(\tau\in[1,n]\) (Lemma
[[lem-row-shift]]), and on a polynomial form of the powers of \(T_0\). As noted after Corollary
[[cor-transfer]], \(T_0\) is upper unitriangular, \(T_0=I+\mathcal N\) by \eqref{eq:T0}. Put
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:U}
  U(t)=\sum_{k=0}^{n-1}\binom{t-1}k\mathcal N^k=U_0+tU_1+t^2U_2+\cdots ,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
a matrix whose entries are polynomials in \(t\), with \(U(t)=T_0^{\,t-1}\) for \(t\ge1\); here
\(U_j\) is the matrix of the coefficients of \(t^j\). Since \(\binom{-1}k=(-1)^k\) and \(\mathcal N\)
is nilpotent, \(U_0=\sum_k(-\mathcal N)^k=T_0^{-1}\). Write \(\vek e_1\) for the first unit row
vector.

#+NAME: lem-row-shift
#+begin_lemma
\leanmark{\leanfull}For \(\delta\ge0\), row \(\tau\) of \(T^{\mathcal L}(\delta)\) is row \(\max(\tau-\delta,1)\) of \(T_0\).
Consequently \(\vek e_1T^{\mathcal L}(\delta)=\vek u^{\mathcal L}=\vek e_1T_0\) and
\(T^{\mathcal L}(\delta)\,T_0^{-1}\vek 1=\vek 1\).
#+end_lemma

#+begin_proof
Row \(\tau\) of \(T^{\mathcal L}(\delta)\) counts the \(x\in\mathcal L_n\) with \(\tp(x)=\tau'\) and
\(\indeg(x)>\tau-\delta\). Every \(x\in\mathcal L_n\) has \(x_0=1\) and \(x_1=n\), so \(\indeg(x)\ge2\),
and the condition \(\indeg(x)>\sigma\) is vacuous for \(\sigma\le1\). Hence the condition with
\(\tau-\delta\) is the condition with \(\max(\tau-\delta,1)\), which is that of row
\(\max(\tau-\delta,1)\) of \(T_0\). For \(\tau=1\) this gives \(\vek e_1T^{\mathcal L}(\delta)=\vek e_1T_0\),
and row \(1\) of \(T_0\), with its vacuous condition, is \(\vek u^{\mathcal L}\). Finally, entry
\(\tau\) of \(T^{\mathcal L}(\delta)T_0^{-1}\vek 1\) is entry \(\max(\tau-\delta,1)\) of
\(T_0T_0^{-1}\vek 1=\vek 1\).
#+end_proof

#+NAME: prop-linear-coeff
#+begin_proposition
\leanmark{\leanfull}\([t^1]\,L_{\Koz(n;\vek d)}(t)=r\cdot[t^1]\,L_{\Koz(n)}(t)\) for every \(\vek d\).
#+end_proposition

#+begin_proof
Substituting the matrix \(U(t)\) of \eqref{eq:U} for every \(T_0^{\,t-1}\) in the
display of Corollary [[cor-transfer]] gives a polynomial that agrees with \(L_{\Koz(n;\vek d)}\) for
\(t\ge1\), hence equals it:
\(L_{\Koz(n;\vek d)}(t)=\vek u^{\mathcal L}U(t)A_2U(t)\cdots A_rU(t)\vek 1\) with
\(A_i=T^{\mathcal L}(\delta_i)\), and \(U_0=T_0^{-1}\).

The \(t^1\)-coefficient is the sum over \(j\in[1,r]\) of the product in which the \(j\)-th factor
\(U(t)\) is replaced by \(U_1\) and every other by \(U_0\). All gaps \(\delta_i\) are non-negative,
so Lemma [[lem-row-shift]] applies to every \(A_i\). On the left,
\(\vek u^{\mathcal L}U_0=\vek e_1T_0T_0^{-1}=\vek e_1\) and \(\vek e_1A_i=\vek u^{\mathcal L}\), so
\(\vek u^{\mathcal L}U_0A_2U_0\cdots U_0A_j=\vek u^{\mathcal L}\). On the right, \(A_iU_0\vek 1=\vek 1\),
so \(A_{j+1}U_0\cdots A_rU_0\vek 1=\vek 1\). The \(j\)-th term is therefore
\(\vek u^{\mathcal L}U_1\vek 1\), the case \(r=1\), which is \([t^1]\,L_{\Koz(n)}(t)\), and there are
\(r\) terms.
#+end_proof

The same reduction gives the constant term:
\(L_{\Koz(n;\vek d)}(0)=\vek u^{\mathcal L}U_0A_2U_0\cdots A_rU_0\vek 1=\vek u^{\mathcal L}U_0\vek 1=\vek e_1\vek 1=1\),
as Ehrhart's theorem requires. Proposition [[prop-linear-coeff]] also follows from a theorem of
McMullen [cite:@McMullen1977], found independently by Bernstein, which we quote in the form of
[cite:@HaaseJuhnkeSanyalTheobald2017 Theorem 2.3].

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 4: "cite and state McMullen as a theorem, so that the conditions of that
# thm become clear (Minkowski sum of polytopes of same dim?)"). No: any lattice polytopes, of any
# dimensions; lambda_i = 0 allowed. Quoted from Haase-Juhnke-Kubitzke-Sanyal-Theobald, EJC 24(1)
# (2017) P1.10, Theorem 2.3 (same number in arXiv v2), which credits Bernstein and McMullen and points
# to Gruber's book, Theorem 19.4; McMullen's own theorem number is still unchecked (paper not
# obtained), so none is given. A cited theorem: no Lean mark, not in the status table.
#+NAME: thm-mcmullen
#+begin_theorem
(/McMullen/.) Let \(P_1,\dots,P_r\subseteq\mathbb R^M\) be lattice polytopes, of any dimensions. There is
a polynomial \(E(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_r)\) such that
\[
  \#\bigl((\lambda_1P_1+\dots+\lambda_rP_r)\cap\mathbb Z^M\bigr)=E(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_r)
  \qquad\text{for all }(\lambda_1,\dots,\lambda_r)\in\mathbb Z_{\ge0}^r,
\]
where \(0P_i=\lbrace0\rbrace\). Its degree in \(\lambda_i\) is \(\dim P_i\).
#+end_theorem

The polytopes need not have the same dimension, and some \(\lambda_i\) may vanish. Setting
\(\lambda_l=0\) for \(l\ne i\) gives \(E(0,\dots,0,\lambda_i,0,\dots,0)=L_{P_i}(\lambda_i)\), so the part of
\(E\) of degree one is \(\sum_i\ell_i\lambda_i\), with \(\ell_i\) the linear coefficient of \(L_{P_i}\),
and the constant term is \(1\). Now take \(M=N+1\) and \(P_i=\shift^{d_i}\Koz(n)\), which has the
Ehrhart polynomial of \(\Koz(n)\) because \(\shift^{d_i}\) maps \(\mathbb Z^{n+1}\) bijectively onto the
lattice points of its image. Then \(E(t,\dots,t)=L_{\Koz(n;\vek d)}(t)\), whose linear coefficient is
\(r\ell_1\): this is Proposition [[prop-linear-coeff]]. The argument given above avoids
Theorem [[thm-mcmullen]], and is the one checked in Lean (Section [[#sec-methods]]).

# 2026-11-09: prop-quadratic-coeff .. cor-gorenstein from ../working-notes/hstar-sketches-proofs-2026-11-09.org
# (Jan, item 20: check the three sketches and add them); independently reviewed, all four claims "correct as
# stated", its seven minor findings applied. NOTE FOR JAN: not yet checked by you.
#+NAME: prop-quadratic-coeff
#+begin_proposition
\leanmark{\leannone}Let \(a_1=[t^1]\,L_{\Koz(n)}\), \(a_2=[t^2]\,L_{\Koz(n)}\) and, for \(\delta\ge0\),
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Qn}
  Q_n(\delta)=\vek u^{\mathcal L}U_1\,T^{\mathcal L}(\delta)\,U_1\vek 1,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
with \(U_1\) as in \eqref{eq:U}. For every \(\vek d\),
\[
  [t^2]\,L_{\Koz(n;\vek d)}(t)=r\,a_2+\sum_{1\le j<k\le r}Q_n(d_k-d_j).
\]
Moreover \(Q_n(\delta)=[t^2]\,L_{\Koz(n;0,\delta)}-2a_2\), \(Q_n(0)=2a_2\), and \(Q_n(\delta)=a_1^2\) for
\(\delta\ge n-1\).
#+end_proposition

#+begin_proof
Use the expansion \eqref{eq:U} to second order in the product
\[
  \vek u^{\mathcal L}U(t)A_2U(t)\cdots A_rU(t)\vek 1
\]
of the proof of Proposition [[prop-linear-coeff]]. Its \(t^2\)-coefficient is the sum of the \(r\)
products with one factor \(U_2\) and \(U_0\) elsewhere, and of the \(\binom r2\) products with \(U_1\)
in positions \(j<k\) and \(U_0\) elsewhere. The two reductions of that proof,
\(\vek u^{\mathcal L}U_0A_2U_0\cdots U_0A_j=\vek u^{\mathcal L}\) on the left and
\(A_{k+1}U_0\cdots A_rU_0\vek 1=\vek 1\) on the right, turn a product of the first kind into
\(\vek u^{\mathcal L}U_2\vek 1=a_2\) (the case \(r=1\)), and one of the second kind into
\(\vek u^{\mathcal L}U_1M_{jk}U_1\vek 1\) with \(M_{jk}=A_{j+1}U_0A_{j+2}U_0\cdots U_0A_k\).

By Lemma [[lem-row-shift]], \(A_i=S(\delta_i)T_0\), where \(S(\delta)\) is the \(0/1\)-matrix whose row
\(\tau\) has its single \(1\) in column \(\max(\tau-\delta,1)\). Hence \(A_iU_0=S(\delta_i)\), and
\(S(\delta)S(\delta')=S(\delta+\delta')\) because \(\max(\max(\tau-\delta,1)-\delta',1)=\max(\tau-\delta-\delta',1)\).
So \(M_{jk}=S(\delta_{j+1})\cdots S(\delta_k)\,T_0=S(d_k-d_j)\,T_0=T^{\mathcal L}(d_k-d_j)\), and the
term is \(Q_n(d_k-d_j)\). The case \(r=2\) gives the first identity. At \(\delta=0\),
\(L_{\Koz(n;0,0)}(t)=L_{\Koz(n)}(2t)\) has \(t^2\)-coefficient \(4a_2\). For \(\delta\ge n-1\) every row of
\(S(\delta)\) is \(\vek e_1\), so \(T^{\mathcal L}(\delta)=\vek 1\,\vek e_1T_0=\vek 1\,\vek u^{\mathcal L}\)
and \(Q_n(\delta)=(\vek u^{\mathcal L}U_1\vek 1)^2=a_1^2\), as Proposition [[prop-product-regime]]
requires.
#+end_proof

As for Proposition [[prop-linear-coeff]], Theorem [[thm-mcmullen]] gives the same shape: the part of degree
two of the polynomial \(E\) of that theorem, with \(P_i=\shift^{d_i}\Koz(n)\), is
\(\sum_i\alpha_i\lambda_i^2+\sum_{j<k}\beta_{jk}\lambda_j\lambda_k\), with \(\beta_{jk}\) determined by the
pair \(P_j,P_k\): \(E\) is valid when some \(\lambda_i\) vanish, and deleting the first
\(d_j\) coordinates, the inverse of \(\shift^{d_j}\), carries the pair to \(\Koz(n)\),
\(\shift^{d_k-d_j}\Koz(n)\).

#+NAME: rem-quadratic-coeff
#+begin_remark
So the \(t^2\)-coefficient at every rank is read off rank two, through the \(n\) numbers
\(Q_n(0),\dots,Q_n(n-1)\):
#+begin_export latex
\[\small
\begin{array}{rll}
n & a_2 & Q_n(0),\dots,Q_n(n-1)\\
3 & 3/2 & 3,\ 7,\ 25/4\\
4 & 9 & 18,\ 36,\ 36,\ 36\\
5 & 355/6 & 355/3,\ 949/4,\ 10231/36,\ 11021/36,\ 1369/4\\
6 & 1033/4 & 1033/2,\ 465/4,\ -91,\ -703/4,\ -2477/4,\ 1/4
\end{array}
\]
#+end_export
For \(3\le n\le5\) every entry is positive, so \(L_{\Koz(n;\vek d)}\) has positive coefficients of \(t^0\),
\(t^1\) and \(t^2\) for every \(\vek d\) (Proposition [[prop-linear-coeff]]). At \(n=6\) one pair at
distance \(4\) outweighs two rank-one terms: the \(t^2\)-coefficient of \(L_{\Koz(6;0,4)}\) is
\(2\cdot\tfrac{1033}4-\tfrac{2477}4=-\tfrac{411}4\), so \(\Koz(6;0,4)\) is not Ehrhart positive, and
\(\Koz(6;0,2,4)\), with \(3\cdot\tfrac{1033}4-2\cdot91-\tfrac{2477}4=-\tfrac{53}2\), is another.
#+end_remark

** Codegree and the Gorenstein property

#+NAME: lem-relint-sum
#+begin_lemma
\leanmark{\leannone}Let \(A,B\subseteq\mathbb R^M\) be polytopes, \(V_A\) the linear space parallel to the affine hull of
\(A\), and \(\pi\colon\mathbb R^M\to\mathbb R^M/V_A\) the quotient map. If \(a\in\relint A\), \(b\in B\)
and \(\pi(b)\in\relint\pi(B)\), then \(a+b\in\relint(A+B)\). In particular
\(\relint A+\relint B\subseteq\relint(A+B)\), and \(\relint A+B\subseteq\relint(A+B)\) when the affine hull of
\(B\) is parallel to a subspace of \(V_A\).
#+end_lemma

#+begin_proof
A point \(x\) of a polytope \(C\) lies in \(\relint C\) if and only if every linear functional \(\ell\) with
\(\ell(x)=\max_C\ell\) is constant on \(C\). If \(x\in\relint C\) and \(\ell(y)<\ell(x)\) for some \(y\in C\),
then \(x+\varepsilon(x-y)\in C\) for small \(\varepsilon>0\), where \(\ell\) is larger; conversely, a point of
the relative boundary lies in a proper face, which is the set of maximisers of a functional that is
not constant on \(C\) [cite:@Ziegler1995 Chapter 2]. Now let \(\ell(a+b)=\max_{A+B}\ell\). Since
\(\max_{A+B}\ell=\max_A\ell+\max_B\ell\), we have \(\ell(a)=\max_A\ell\) and \(\ell(b)=\max_B\ell\). The first
makes \(\ell\) constant on \(A\), so \(\ell\) vanishes on \(V_A\) and equals \(\bar\ell\circ\pi\) for a linear
\(\bar\ell\). The second says that \(\bar\ell\) attains its maximum over \(\pi(B)\) at \(\pi(b)\), so
\(\bar\ell\) is constant on \(\pi(B)\) and \(\ell\) on \(B\). Hence \(\ell\) is constant on \(A+B\). If
\(b\in\relint B\), the same argument shows \(\pi(b)\in\relint\pi(B)\); if the affine hull of \(B\) is
parallel to a subspace of \(V_A\), then \(\pi(B)\) is a point.
#+end_proof

The easy half of the codegree is immediate from Lemma [[lem-relint-sum]].

#+NAME: prop-codegree
#+begin_proposition
\leanmark{\leannone}Let \(n\ge3\). The codegree of \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) is at most \(2\), and equals \(2\) when \(r=1\) or
\(d_r\ge d_{r-1}+2\).
#+end_proposition

#+begin_proof
By Lemma [[lem-relint-sum]], \(\relint(2\Koz(n;\vek d))\supseteq\sum_i\relint(2\shift^{d_i}\Koz(n))\), and
each summand contains a lattice point by Lemma [[lem-interior-points]](2). If \(d_r\ge d_{r-1}+2\), the coordinates \(N-1,N\) belong
to the last summand only, and the argument of Proposition [[prop-hstar-props]](2) applies to them.
#+end_proof

#+NAME: thm-codegree
#+begin_theorem
\leanmark{\leannone}Let \(n\ge3\), and call an index \(i\in[1,r]\) *exposed* if \(d_i\) is not repeated from below
(\(i=1\) or \(d_{i-1}<d_i\)) and the gap after it is large (\(i=r\) or \(d_{i+1}-d_i\ge n-1\)).
1. The codegree of \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) is \(2\) if some index is exposed, and \(1\) otherwise.
2. If \(c\) is the codegree, \(c\Koz(n;\vek d)\) has at least two lattice points in its relative
   interior.
In particular, if every gap is at most \(n-2\), the codegree is \(1\) if and only if \(r\ge2\) and
\(d_{r-1}=d_r\).
#+end_theorem

Equivalently, in terms of the maximal runs of \(\vek d\) with gaps at most \(n-2\) (the factors of the
lattice product described after Theorem [[thm-staircase]]), the codegree is \(1\) if and only if every
run ends in a repeated value.

#+begin_proof
Write \(P=\Koz(n;\vek d)\) and \(P_i=\shift^{d_i}\Koz(n)\). We use, as in the proof of Proposition
[[prop-hstar-props]](2), that a valid inequality that is not tight on all of a polytope is strict on its
relative interior.

/An exposed index./ Let \(i\) be exposed and \(k=d_i+n\). For \(j<i\), \(d_j<d_i\), so summand \(j\) has
own index \(k-d_j>n\) and contributes \(0\) to \(x_k\); for \(j>i\), \(d_j\ge d_i+n-1\), so its own index
is at most \(1\) and it contributes a constant. Hence \(x_k=\beta+(\vek p_i)_n\) with \(\beta\in\mathbb Z\)
constant on \(P\), and \((\vek p_i)_n\) takes the values \(0\) and \(1\) (at \(\tilde F_1\) and
\(\tilde F_n\)). So \(\beta<x_k<\beta+1\) on \(\relint P\), which has no lattice point, and the codegree
is \(2\) by Proposition [[prop-codegree]].

/No exposed index./ For an integer \(T\), or \(T=\infty\), let \(\tau_T\) project \(\mathbb R^{N+1}\) to the
coordinates \(x_0,\dots,x_T\), and put \(P_{\le s}=P_1+\dots+P_s\) and \(P_X=\sum_{i\in X}P_i\). Call
\(i\le s\) exposed at \((s,T)\) if \(d_i\) is not repeated from below and either \(i<s\) and
\(d_{i+1}-d_i\ge n-1\), or \(i=s\) and \(T-d_s\ge n\); exposed at \((r,\infty)\) is exposed. We show by
induction on \(s\): /if \(T\ge d_s+2\) (no condition if \(s=0\)) and no index is exposed at \((s,T)\), there is a lattice point
\(b\in P_{\le s}\) with \(\tau_T(b)\in\relint\tau_T(P_{\le s})\)./ For \(s=0\) take \(b=0\). Let \(s\ge1\).

If \(T-d_s\le n-1\), let \(k\) be the largest index with \(d_k\le T-n\) (\(k=0\) if there is none) and
\(A=\lbrace k+1,\dots,s\rbrace\). For \(i\in A\), \(2\le T-d_i\le n-1\), so \(\tau_T(P_i)\) is \(\shift^{d_i}\)
of the projection of \(\Koz(n)\) to its first \(T-d_i+1\) coordinates, and Lemma
[[lem-interior-points]](1) gives a lattice point \(\vek y_i\in P_i\) with
\(\tau_T(\vek y_i)\in\relint\tau_T(P_i)\). By Lemma [[lem-relint-sum]], \(a=\sum_{i\in A}\vek y_i\) has
\(\tau_T(a)\in\relint\tau_T(P_A)\). The linear space parallel to \(\tau_T(P_A)\) is
\(\sum_{i\in A}\mathbb R^{[d_i+2,T]}=\mathbb R^{[d_{k+1}+2,T]}\), and the quotient by it is \(\tau_{T'}\) with
\(T'=d_{k+1}+1\). If \(k\ge1\), then \(d_k\le T-n<d_{k+1}\), so \(T'\ge d_k+2\), and \(k\) is exposed at
\((k,T')\) exactly when \(d_{k+1}-d_k\ge n-1\) and \(d_k\) is not repeated from below, that is, when
it is exposed at \((s,T)\); the indices below \(k\) are unaffected. By induction there is a lattice
point \(b\in P_{\le k}\) with \(\tau_{T'}(b)\in\relint\tau_{T'}(P_{\le k})\), and Lemma
[[lem-relint-sum]] gives \(\tau_T(a+b)\in\relint\tau_T(P_{\le s})\).

If \(T-d_s\ge n\), then, \(s\) not being exposed, \(s\ge2\) and \(d_{s-1}=d_s\). Let \(m\ge2\) be the number
of \(i\le s\) with \(d_i=d_s\), \(k=s-m\) and \(A=\lbrace k+1,\dots,s\rbrace\). Here \(\tau_T\) is injective on
\(P_A=m\shift^{d_s}\Koz(n)\), which has a lattice point \(a\) in its relative interior by Lemma
[[lem-interior-points]](2). The quotient by \(\mathbb R^{[d_s+2,d_s+n]}\) keeps the coordinates up to
\(d_s+1\) and those above \(d_s+n\), which vanish on \(P_{\le k}\); on \(\tau_T(P_{\le k})\) it is
therefore \(\tau_{T'}\) with \(T'=d_s+1\), up to these zero coordinates. If \(k\ge1\), then
\(T'\ge d_k+2\), and \(k\) is exposed at \((k,T')\) exactly when it is exposed at \((s,T)\), since
\(d_{k+1}=d_s\). Induction and Lemma [[lem-relint-sum]] finish as before.

At \((s,T)=(r,\infty)\) this gives a lattice point in \(\relint P\), so the codegree is \(1\).

/Two points./ If the codegree is \(1\), the induction at \((r,\infty)\) starts in the second case, where
Lemma [[lem-interior-points]](2) offers two choices of \(a\); with the same \(b\) they give two distinct
interior lattice points. If it is \(2\), choose for each \(i\) a lattice point \(\vek q_i\) in
\(\relint(2P_i)\) (Lemma [[lem-interior-points]](2), shifted); \(\sum_i\vek q_i\in\relint(2P)\) by Lemma
[[lem-relint-sum]], and replacing \(\vek q_r\) by a second such point gives another.

If every gap is at most \(n-2\), only \(i=r\) can be exposed, and it is unless \(r\ge2\) and
\(d_{r-1}=d_r\).
#+end_proof

#+NAME: ex-codegree-product
#+begin_mexample
\(\Koz(3;0,2,2)\) has \(d_2=d_3\), but its gap \(2=n-1\) makes index \(1\) exposed, and its codegree is
\(2\). The projection to \((x_2,x_3)\) and \((x_4,x_5)\) is a unimodular equivalence onto a translate
of \(\Koz(3)\times2\Koz(3)\), by the remark on runs after Theorem [[thm-staircase]]: the first summand
contributes \(x_2-2\) and \(x_3-6\), the other two contribute \(x_4\) and \(x_5\). So the condition
\(d_{r-1}=d_r\) alone does not decide the codegree: \(\Koz(3;0,0,2)\) is the same product, with
\(d_2\ne d_3\), while \(\Koz(3;0,0,2,2)\), with the same last pair as \(\Koz(3;0,2,2)\), has no exposed
index and codegree \(1\). \(\Koz(3;0,2,2)\) and \(\Koz(3;0,0,2)\) both have \(L(t)=L_{\Koz(3)}(t)\,L_{\Koz(3)}(2t)=\tfrac12(t+1)(3t+2)(2t+1)(3t+1)\), dimension
\(4\), \(h^*=(1,55,130,30)\), and \(30=2\cdot15\) interior lattice points in the second dilate, as
\eqref{eq:reciprocity} requires of the top coefficient.
#+end_mexample

#+NAME: cor-gorenstein
#+begin_corollary
\leanmark{\leannone}For \(n\ge3\), no Kozlov polytope \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) is Gorenstein: \(h^*_s\ge2\), \(s=\deg h^*\).
#+end_corollary

#+begin_proof
By \eqref{eq:reciprocity} \(h^*_s\) is the number of lattice points in the relative interior of
\(c\Koz(n;\vek d)\), \(c\) the codegree, which is at least \(2\) by Theorem [[thm-codegree]](2); and
\(h^*_0=1\).
#+end_proof

#+NAME: obs-hstar-unimodal
#+begin_observation
In all \(42\) computed cases (\(n\le7\), ranks \(2\) to \(4\)) the \(h^*\)-polynomial of \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\)
is real-rooted, so its \(h^*\)-vector is log-concave and unimodal, as at rank one (Proposition
[[prop-hstar-props]]). In the \(39\) of them with \(n\ge3\) it is not palindromic, as Corollary [[cor-gorenstein]] requires;
the three with \(n=2\) have \(h^*=(1,1)\), which is. Ehrhart
positivity fails at \(n=6\) as well: \(L_{\Koz(6;0,4)}(t)\) has \(t^2\)-coefficient \(-411/4\), which is \(2a_2+Q_6(4)\)
(Remark [[rem-quadratic-coeff]]), although
\(L_{\Koz(6)}\) and \(L_{\Koz(6)}^2=L_{\Koz(6;0,5)}\) are positive (by the transfer matrix, and for
\(\Koz(6;0,4)\) independently by Barvinok's algorithm).
#+end_observation

* The centroid of the Hilbert functions and the centroid of the polytope
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: sec-centroid
:END:

Three uniform measures live on \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\): on the ff-vectors \(\FF(n;\vek d)\) (which is
\(S_n\) at rank one), on all lattice points, and the continuous one. For the question "where do the
Hilbert functions sit inside their convex hull" the first is the natural one, with each distinct
Hilbert function counted once. At rank one we work in the LYM coordinates
\(\mu_k=x_k/\binom nk\) of \eqref{eq:mu}, in which the barycentric coordinates are \(\lambda_j=\mu_j-\mu_{j+1}\), so that
"where a centroid sits relative to the vertices" and "its \(\mu\)-profile" are the same information.
At rank \(r\) we use
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:nu}
  \nu_k=\frac{x_k}{\dim F_k},\qquad \dim F_k=\sum_{i=1}^r\binom n{k-d_i},
\end{equation}
#+end_export
the fraction of the degree-\(k\) part of \(F\) that survives in the quotient \(F/M\); at rank one
\(\nu_k=\mu_k\). These are not barycentric coordinates: for \(r\ge2\) the polytope
\(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) is not a simplex, each \(\nu_k\) lies in \([0,1]\), and their sum is not \(1\). As
at rank one, they say how full the quotient is in each degree. We write
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:centroid}
  c(X)=\frac1{\lvert X\rvert}\sum_{x\in X}x\quad(X\text{ finite}),\qquad
  c(X)=\frac1{\vol X}\int_Xx\,dx\quad(X\text{ a polytope})
\end{equation}
#+end_export
for the centroid of the uniform measure on \(X\), the integral taken with Lebesgue measure on the
affine hull of \(X\). It is not to be confused with the leg vector \(\vek c\).

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 8: "Can sec-centroid be reorganized so it is clear what is shown for
# Koz(n), dilates of Koz(n), general Koz(n; d)? ... a very short initial or final passage"; answer in
# article3-sweep-2026-11-14b.org: "let us put in the table from above"). The summary table of that
# report, with its cross-references made labels; no reorganisation. A float, like the moment tables.
# Cells ragged right by \raggedright, rows ended by \tabularnewline (the array package is not loaded).
Table \ref{tab:centroid-summary} sorts the results of this section and of Section [[#sec-moments]] by
polytope: \(\Koz(n)\) itself, its dilates \(r\Koz(n)=\Koz(n;0^r)\), and \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) in general.
Equal shifts \(\vek d=(d,\dots,d)\) give \(\shift^d\bigl(r\Koz(n)\bigr)\), a copy of the dilate.

#+begin_export latex
\begin{table}[tbp]
\centering\footnotesize
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.15}
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.12\linewidth}p{0.42\linewidth}p{0.39\linewidth}@{}}
\toprule
polytope & centroid & spread\\
\midrule
\(\Koz(n)\)
& \raggedright All three located: the polytope's exactly (Prop.~\ref{prop-centroid-simplex}). The
  \(\beta\)-symmetry holds for the polytope (Thm.~\ref{thm-beta-continuous}), for the lattice points
  iff \(n\) is prime (Thm.~\ref{thm-beta-lattice}, Obs.~\ref{obs-beta-composite}), and for the
  \(f\)-vectors up to \(\varepsilon_n\) (Thm.~\ref{thm-alexander-core}), which sit one vertex lower.
  Table~\ref{tab:centroids-rank1}, Figure~\ref{fig:mu}.
& \raggedright The polytope exactly (Prop.~\ref{prop-second-moments}). \(f\)-vectors, lattice points and polytope
  compared by computation only (Obs.~\ref{obs-concentration},
  Tables~\ref{tab:concentration}--\ref{tab:profile-n24}).\tabularnewline[3pt]
\(r\Koz(n)\)
&\raggedright The ff-vectors' centroid, scaled by \(1/r\), tends to the polytope's with error \(O(1/r)\)
  (Thm.~\ref{thm-centroid-dilation}(1)), exactly so at \(n=3\) (Thm.~\ref{thm-n3-equal-shifts}). The
  lattice-point centroid of \(t\Koz(n)\) is known exactly for \(n\le5\) (Prop.~\ref{prop-dilates}).
  Figure~\ref{fig:mu-rank-r}.
& \raggedright The covariance, scaled by \(1/r^2\), tends to the polytope's with error \(O(1/r)\)
  (Thm.~\ref{thm-centroid-dilation}(2)), and the whole measure converges weakly
  (Rem.~\ref{rem-weak-convergence}). The polytope's covariance grows like \(r^2\), the independent
  sum's like \(r\) (Prop.~\ref{prop-pushforward}(3)).\tabularnewline[3pt]
\(\Koz(n;\vek d)\)
& \raggedright Five centroids, which pair off in the product regime (Prop.~\ref{prop-pushforward}(1),(2));

& \raggedright The same for the covariances (Prop.~\ref{prop-pushforward}(2), Obs.~\ref{obs-pushforward-fails}).

\caption{What Sections~\ref{sec-centroid} and~\ref{sec-moments} show, polytope by polytope.}

#+end_export

** Rank one: exact symmetries

#+NAME: prop-centroid-simplex
#+begin_proposition
\leanmark{\leanfull}The centroid of \(\Koz(n)\) has \(\mu_k=(n-k+1)/n\); all its barycentric coordinates equal \(1/n\),
and its mean vertex index \(\sum_jj\lambda_j=\sum_k\mu_k\) is \((n+1)/2\).
#+end_proposition

#+begin_proof
The centroid of a simplex is the average of its vertices, and \(\tilde F_j\) has \(\mu_k=1\) iff
\(k\le j\).
#+end_proof

#+NAME: lem-beta
#+begin_lemma
\leanmark{\leanfull}Let \(n\ge3\). The affine map \(\beta\) of \(\mathbb R^{n+1}\) fixing \(x_0,x_1,x_{n-1},x_n\) and, in LYM
coordinates, sending \(\mu_k\mapsto1+\mu_{n-1}-\mu_{n-k}\) for \(2\le k\le n-2\), is an involution of
\(\Koz(n)\). It permutes the vertices, \(\tilde F_j\leftrightarrow\tilde F_{n-1-j}\) for
\(1\le j\le n-2\), and fixes \(\tilde F_{n-1}\) and \(\tilde F_n\). On the codimension-two face
\(\Koz(n)^\circ=\Koz(n)\cap\lbrace\mu_{n-1}=0\rbrace=\conv(\tilde F_1,\dots,\tilde F_{n-2})\) it is
\(x_k\mapsto\binom nk-x_{n-k}\), the Alexander duality map \(D_n\) of [cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Definition 13].
#+end_lemma

#+begin_proof
Since \(\mu_{n-1}\) is fixed, applying the map twice returns \(\mu_k\). The vertex \(\tilde F_j\),
\(j\le n-2\), has \(\mu_{n-1}=0\) and \(\mu_k=[k\le j]\), and its image has
\(\mu_k=1-[n-k\le j]=[k\le n-1-j]\); \(\tilde F_{n-1}\) and \(\tilde F_n\) have \(\mu_k=1\) for
\(k\le n-1\), and are fixed. An affine map permuting the vertices of a simplex maps it onto itself.
#+end_proof

#+NAME: thm-beta-continuous
#+begin_theorem
\leanmark{\leanfull}For \(n\ge3\) the uniform measure on \(\Koz(n)\) is \(\beta\)-invariant. Hence its centroid satisfies
\(\mu_k+\mu_{n-k}=1+\mu_{n-1}\) for \(1\le k\le n-1\), and its covariance matrix is \(\beta\)-invariant:
\(\bar\beta\Sigma\bar\beta^{\mathsf T}=\Sigma\), where \(\Sigma\) is the covariance matrix in the coordinates
\((x_0,\dots,x_n)\) and \(\bar\beta\) the matrix of the linear part of \(\beta\).
#+end_theorem

#+begin_proof
An affine bijection of a polytope onto itself preserves the normalised relative Lebesgue measure.
The centroid of a \(\beta\)-invariant measure is fixed by \(\beta\), which in LYM coordinates is the
identity stated, and the image of a measure with covariance \(\Sigma\) under \(\beta:x\mapsto\bar\beta x+b\) has
covariance \(\bar\beta\Sigma\bar\beta^{\mathsf T}\).
#+end_proof

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 5): an opening sentence before the list; the Lean mark moved with it
# (code/leanmarks_article3.py puts it on the first line of the statement, so a re-run agrees).
#+NAME: thm-beta-lattice
#+begin_theorem
\leanmark{\leanfull}Let \(n\ge3\), and let \(\beta\) be the involution of Lemma [[lem-beta]]. Whether it preserves the
lattice points of \(\Koz(n)\) depends on the arithmetic of \(n\).
1. If \(n\) is prime, \(\beta\) maps \(\mathcal L_n\) bijectively onto itself; so the lattice-point
   centroid satisfies \(\mu_k+\mu_{n-k}=1+\mu_{n-1}\) exactly, and the covariance matrix
   \(\Sigma\) of the uniform measure on \(\mathcal L_n\) is \(\beta\)-invariant in the sense of
   Theorem [[thm-beta-continuous]], \(\bar\beta\Sigma\bar\beta^{\mathsf T}=\Sigma\).
2. If \(n\ge4\) is composite, \(\beta\) does not preserve \(\mathcal L_n\), so the uniform measure on
   \(\mathcal L_n\) is not \(\beta\)-invariant.
#+end_theorem

#+begin_proof
(1) \(\beta(x)_k=\binom nk+\binom nk\,x_{n-1}/n-x_{n-k}\), and \(n\mid\binom nk\) for \(0<k<n\) when \(n\)
is prime. (2) Let \(\ell\) be a prime divisor of \(n\), so \(2\le\ell\le n/2\le n-2\). Since
\(\binom n\ell=(n/\ell)\binom{n-1}{\ell-1}\) and the last base-\(\ell\) digit of \(n-1\) is \(\ell-1\),
Lucas's theorem gives \(\binom{n-1}{\ell-1}\equiv1\pmod\ell\), so \(v_\ell\binom n\ell=v_\ell(n)-1\) and
\(\binom n\ell/n\notin\mathbb Z\). The \(f\)-vector with \(f_k=\binom nk\) for \(k\le n-2\),
\(f_{n-1}=1\), \(f_n=0\) lies in \(\mathcal L_n\), and \(\beta(x)_\ell=\binom n\ell/n\).
#+end_proof

For instance at \(n=4\), \(\ell=2\): the \(f\)-vector \((1,4,6,1,0)\) has
\(\beta(x)_2=6+6\cdot\tfrac14-6=\tfrac32\notin\mathbb Z\). On the face \(\Koz(n)^\circ\), where \(\beta\) is
Alexander duality, \(\beta\) maps lattice points to lattice points for every \(n\). Part (2) does not by
itself show that the centroid identity fails for composite \(n\), since a non-symmetric set can have a
symmetric mean.

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 7: verified-only claims that blend into the text get an environment).
# Was the end of the previous paragraph ("that it does fail was checked for every composite n <= 18,
# and that it holds for every prime n <= 19"); wording otherwise unchanged. Source: the overnight run
# of 2026-10-20 (review_centroids_check.py logs n <= 14; the range to 19 is as stated before).
#+NAME: obs-beta-composite
#+begin_observation
For every composite \(n\le18\) the lattice-point centroid of \(\Koz(n)\) violates the identity
\(\mu_k+\mu_{n-k}=1+\mu_{n-1}\) for some \(k\), and for every prime \(n\le19\) it satisfies it, as
Theorem [[thm-beta-lattice]](1) requires.
#+end_observation

#+NAME: thm-alexander-core
#+begin_theorem
\leanmark{\leanfull}(/The Alexander core./) Let \(n\ge3\), \(S_n^\circ=\lbrace\vek s\in S_n:s_{n-1}=0\rbrace\) and
\(S_n^{\mathrm{top}}=S_n\setminus S_n^\circ\).
1. Alexander duality restricts to an involution of \(S_n^\circ\); hence the centroid of \(S_n^\circ\)
   satisfies \(\mu_k+\mu_{n-k}=1\) for \(1\le k\le n-1\) and \(\mu_{n-1}=\mu_n=0\).
2. \(\lvert S_n^{\mathrm{top}}\rvert=\sum_{m=0}^{n-1}\lvert S_m\rvert\), with \(\lvert S_0\rvert=1\).
3. With \(\varepsilon_n=\lvert S_n^{\mathrm{top}}\rvert/\lvert S_n\rvert\), the centroid of \(S_n\)
   satisfies \(0\le\mu_k+\mu_{n-k}-1\le\varepsilon_n\) for \(1\le k\le n-1\) and
   \(0\le\mu_n\le\mu_{n-1}\le\varepsilon_n\), and its mean vertex index
   \(\sum_jj\lambda_j=\sum_{k=1}^n\mu_k\) (Proposition [[prop-centroid-simplex]]) lies in
   \([(n-1)/2,\ (n-1)/2+(n+1)\varepsilon_n/2]\).
#+end_theorem

#+begin_proof
(1) Let \(\vek s\in S_n^\circ\); then \(s_n=0\) too. By [cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Lemma 14],
\(D_n(\vek s)\) is an \(f\)-vector of an order ideal, with \(D_n(\vek s)_0=1-s_n=1\),
\(D_n(\vek s)_1=n-s_{n-1}=n\) and \(D_n(\vek s)_{n-1}=n-s_1=0\), so \(D_n(\vek s)\in S_n^\circ\); and \(D_n\)
is an involution, \(\mu_k\mapsto1-\mu_{n-k}\). (2) If a proper \(\Delta\) has a face of size \(n-1\),
relabel so that \([n-1]\in\Delta\); then \(\Delta=2^{[n-1]}\cup\lbrace\sigma\cup\lbrace n\rbrace:\sigma\in\Gamma\rbrace\)
with \(\Gamma=\operatorname{lk}_\Delta(n)\) a non-void complex on a subset of \([n-1]\), and
\(f_k(\Delta)=\binom{n-1}k+f_{k-1}(\Gamma)\). So \(f(\Delta)\mapsto f(\Gamma)\) is a bijection from
\(S_n^{\mathrm{top}}\) onto the \(f\)-vectors of non-void complexes on vertex sets contained in
\([n-1]\), which are partitioned by their number \(m\) of vertices into copies of \(S_m\). (3) The
centroid of \(S_n\) is \((1-\varepsilon_n)\,c(S_n^\circ)+\varepsilon_n\,c(S_n^{\mathrm{top}})\). On
\(S_n^{\mathrm{top}}\), \(\mu_k+\mu_{n-k}-1=(f_{k-1}(\Gamma)+f_{n-k-1}(\Gamma))/\binom nk\in[0,1]\);
\(\mu_{n-1}\) vanishes on the core and is at most \(1\); and
\(\sum_k\mu_k=\tfrac12\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}(\mu_k+\mu_{n-k})+\mu_n\).
#+end_proof

Since \(\lvert S_n\rvert\) grows much faster than \(\sum_{m<n}\lvert S_m\rvert\),
\(\varepsilon_n\to0\): its values for \(n=3,\dots,8\) are \(0.8\), \(0.5625\), \(0.357\), \(0.208\),
\(0.112\) and \(0.0573\), and it is \(0.0026\) at \(n=12\). So, asymptotically, the centroid of the
\(f\)-vectors lies in the codimension-two face \(\Koz(n)^\circ\) and is Alexander self-dual up to
\(O(\varepsilon_n)\). Its mean vertex index \(\sum_jj\lambda_j\) is the average of \(j\) when the
vertex \(\tilde F_j\), the \(f\)-vector of the \((j-1)\)-skeleton, is drawn with probability
\(\lambda_j\); by Theorem [[thm-alexander-core]](3) it lies between \((n-1)/2\) and
\((n-1)/2+(n+1)\varepsilon_n/2\), against
\((n+1)/2\) for the centroid of the simplex (Proposition [[prop-centroid-simplex]]). So, measured in
vertices, the \(f\)-vectors sit on average *one vertex lower* than the simplex: nearer to the skeleta
of one dimension less.

#+NAME: rem-one-map
#+begin_remark
(/One map, three behaviours./) In LYM coordinates the identity
\(\mu_k+\mu_{n-k}=1+\mu_{n-1}\) says that the profile \(k\mapsto\mu_k\) is point-symmetric about
\((n/2,(1+\mu_{n-1})/2)\). It holds exactly for the polytope, where \(\mu_{n-1}=2/n\) and the centre
is \((n/2,\tfrac12+\tfrac1n)\); exactly for the lattice points when \(n\) is prime; and up to
\(\varepsilon_n\) for the \(f\)-vectors, where \(\mu_{n-1}\to0\). The centre is at \(k=n/2\) in every
case; what differs is its height. For lattice points \(\mu_{n-1}\) stays large (\(0.126\) at \(n=7\)),
because the facet \(\mu_n=0\) carries almost every lattice point while the face \(\mu_{n-1}=0\) does
not.
#+end_remark

#+NAME: ex-centroid-n3
#+begin_mexample
At \(n=3\), where \(\Koz(3)\) is a triangle, \(S_3=\mathcal L_3\) in \((x_2,x_3)\) is \(\lbrace(0,0),(1,0),(2,0),(3,0),(3,1)\rbrace\). Its
centroid is \((9/5,1/5)\), that is \(\mu=(3/5,1/5)\), with barycentric coordinates
\((2/5,2/5,1/5)\): by hand, in \((x_2,x_3)\), \((1,0)=\tfrac23\tilde F_1+\tfrac13\tilde F_2\) and
\((2,0)=\tfrac13\tilde F_1+\tfrac23\tilde F_2\), so, listing the points in order,
\(\lambda_1=(1+\tfrac23+\tfrac13+0+0)/5=\tfrac25\). Kupreyeva's row \(p=3\) of
[cite:@Kupreyeva2019 Table 4.2], \((1.80,0.20)\), is this centroid \((9/5,1/5)\) of \(S_3\), since the
\(f\)-vectors in her \(T^3_3\) are \(S_3\). The centroid of the triangle \(\Koz(3)\) is \((2,1/3)\), \(\mu=(2/3,1/3)\),
barycentric \((\tfrac13,\tfrac13,\tfrac13)\). Her row \(p=4\) of Table 5.1 is the centroid
\((x_2,x_3,x_4)=(17/4,19/16,1/16)\) of \(S_4\).
#+end_mexample

#+begin_export latex
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{@{}rlrrrrrr@{}}
\toprule

\midrule

4 & lattice & 0.6833 & 0.3375 & 0.0500 & & &\\
4 & \(\Koz(n)\) & 0.7500 & 0.5000 & 0.2500 & & &\\

5 & lattice & 0.7407 & 0.4815 & 0.2222 & 0.0062 & &\\
5 & \(\Koz(n)\) & 0.8000 & 0.6000 & 0.4000 & 0.2000 & &\\

6 & lattice & 0.8024 & 0.5814 & 0.3600 & 0.1508 & 0.0005 &\\
