\end{tabular}
\captionof{table}{LYM coordinates of the three centroids at rank one. Exact values, for example
\(c(S_5)=(1,5,\tfrac{561}{70},\tfrac{43}{10},\tfrac9{14},\tfrac1{70})\), are in the
computation log (Section~\ref{sec-methods}).}
\label{tab:centroids-rank1}
\end{center}
#+end_export

The lattice points that are not \(f\)-vectors are those whose profile stays high in the upper
coordinates: a plateau \(\mu_k\approx\mu_{k+1}<1\) is exactly what the Kruskal--Katona bound, roughly
\(\mu_{k+1}\lesssim\mu_k^{(k+1)/k}\), forbids. The \(f\)-vectors avoid the top vertices
\(\tilde F_{n-1}\), \(\tilde F_n\), put less weight than the lattice points on \(\tilde F_1\), and pile up
on \(\tilde F_j\) with \(j\) near \(n/2\) (Figure \ref{fig:mu}).

#+begin_export latex
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{img/fig-mu-projection.pdf}
\caption{The $f$-vectors of proper complexes on $[n]$, $n=5,6,7$, projected to the normalised
coordinates $(\mu_2,\mu_3)=(x_2/\binom n2,x_3/\binom n3)$, in which $\Koz(n)$ projects onto the
triangle $1\ge\mu_2\ge\mu_3\ge0$. The area of a filled dot grows with the number of $f$-vectors
over it; hollow dots are lattice points of the triangle hit by no $f$-vector ($21$, $51$, $119$
of $66$, $171$, $400$). The Kruskal--Katona boundary approaches $\mu_3=\mu_2^{3/2}$ (dashed);
the centroid of $S_n$ ($\star$) sits well inside it, below the centroid of the simplex
$\Koz(n)$ ($\diamond$).}
\label{fig:mu}
\end{figure}
#+end_export

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 8: "five points" wants an adjective): "five centroids"; each of the five
# is the centroid of a measure, three uniform and two push-forwards (prop-pushforward(1)).
** Rank two and higher: five centroids

At rank \(r\) there are five natural centroids, of three uniform measures and two push-forwards:
the centroid of \(\FF(n;\vek d)\); the
*independent sum* \(\sum_i\shift^{d_i}c(S_n)\), which is the centroid of the push-forward of the
uniform measure on \(S_n^r\), that is, the expected value of \(\sum_i\shift^{d_i}\vek s_i\) for
independent uniform \(\vek s_i\in S_n\); the lattice-point
centroid; the centroid of \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\); and \(\sum_i\shift^{d_i}c(\Koz(n))\), the centroid of a
sum of independent uniform points of the summands.

#+NAME: prop-pushforward
#+begin_proposition
1. \leanmark{\leanfull}The two independent sums are the centroids of the push-forward measures, by linearity of
   expectation, and the covariances of those measures are the sums of the shifted rank-one
   covariances.
2. In the product regime the centroid of \(\FF\) equals the independent sum of the \(c(S_n)\), the
   centroid of \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) equals the independent sum of the \(c(\Koz(n))\), the same two
   identities hold for the covariance matrices, and the lattice-point centroid is the sum of the shifted lattice-point centroids
   of \(\Koz(n)\).
3. For equal shifts \(\vek d=0^r\), \(\Koz(n;0^r)=r\Koz(n)\) has centroid \(r\,c(\Koz(n))\), but
   covariance \(r^2\) times that of \(\Koz(n)\), against \(r\) times for the independent sum.
#+end_proposition

#+begin_proof
(1) is linearity and independence. (2) The sumset map \(S_n^r\to\FF\) is onto, and
\(\lvert\FF\rvert=\lvert S_n\rvert^r\) [cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Proposition 42], so it is a bijection, so the uniform measure on \(\FF\) is the push-forward of the
uniform measure on \(S_n^r\); the polytope is a lattice product (Proposition [[prop-product-regime]])
and Fubini applies to it and to its lattice points. (3) Dilation.
#+end_proof

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 7): was a sentence of running text ("Outside the product regime the other
# identities fail in every case computed: ..."). The log of 2026-10-20 showed this for the centroids
# only; code/oneoff/moments_tables_check.sage now checks every identity of (2) in the 16 cases (centroids
# exactly, covariances through their diagonals; lattice points except at n = 5, d = (0,3)).
#+NAME: obs-pushforward-fails
#+begin_observation
Outside the product regime the identities of Proposition [[prop-pushforward]](2) fail in each of the
\(16\) computed cases, those of Table \ref{tab:rank-r-variance} without an asterisk, with one exception:
at \(\vek d=0^r\) the centroid of the polytope is still the independent sum, by (3). For instance
\(c(\FF(n;0^r))\ne r\,c(S_n)\) already at \(n=3\), \(r=2\) (Example [[ex-centroid-rank2]]).
#+end_observation

#+NAME: thm-n3-equal-shifts
#+begin_theorem
\leanmark{\leanfull}For every \(r\ge1\), \(\FF(3;0^r)=r\Koz(3)\cap\mathbb Z^4\). In \((x_2,x_3)\) these are the lattice
points of the triangle \(0\le3x_3\le x_2\le3r\); there are \((r+1)(3r+2)/2\) of them, and their
centroid is
\[
  (x_0,x_1,x_2,x_3)=\Bigl(r,\ 3r,\ \frac{3r(2r+1)}{3r+2},\ \frac{r^2}{3r+2}\Bigr),
  \quad \nu_2=\frac{2r+1}{3r+2}\to\frac23,\quad \nu_3=\frac r{3r+2}\to\frac13 .
\]
So the uniform measure on \(\FF(3;0^r)\) has the same limiting centroid as the polytope, while the
independent sum stays at \(\nu=(3/5,1/5)\) for every \(r\).
#+end_theorem

#+begin_proof
The triangle \(\Koz(3)\) is the union of the three unimodular triangles
\(\conv\lbrace(i,0),(i+1,0),(3,1)\rbrace\), \(i=0,1,2\), so it is IDP, and \(r\Koz(3)\cap\mathbb Z^4\) is
\(r\)-fold sums of \(\mathcal L_3=S_3\), which is \(\FF(3;0^r)\); the reverse inclusion is
\(\FF\subseteq\conv\). (It is also Corollaries [[cor-additivity]] and [[cor-small-n]].) Write
\(x_2=3q+s\), \(0\le q<r\), \(s\in\lbrace0,1,2\rbrace\), or \(x_2=3r\); there are
\(\lfloor x_2/3\rfloor+1\) choices of \(x_3\). So the number of points is
\(\sum_{q<r}3(q+1)+(r+1)=(r+1)(3r+2)/2\), \(\sum x_3=\sum_{q<r}3q(q+1)/2+r(r+1)/2=r^2(r+1)/2\), and
\(\sum x_2=\sum_{q<r}(q+1)(9q+3)+3r(r+1)=3r(r+1)(2r+1)/2\). Divide.
#+end_proof

#+NAME: ex-centroid-rank2
#+begin_mexample
\(\FF(3;0,0)\) consists of the twelve lattice points of the triangle \(2\Koz(3)\), with multiplicities (the
number of ordered pairs of elements of \(S_3\) summing to the point) as exponents. The columns
headed \(\nu\) below are the coordinates \eqref{eq:nu}, \(x_k/\dim F_k\), not barycentric
coordinates, so they need not sum to \(1\):
\[
  (0,0)^1(1,0)^2(2,0)^3(3,0)^4(4,0)^3(5,0)^2(6,0)^1\quad(3,1)^2(4,1)^2(5,1)^2(6,1)^2\quad(6,2)^1 .
\]
In
\((x_2,x_3)\):
#+begin_export latex
\[\small
\begin{array}{lcc}
 & (x_2,x_3) & (\nu_2,\nu_3)\\[2pt]
\FF(3;0,0)=\text{lattice points} & (15/4,\,1/2) & (0.625,\,0.250)\\
\text{independent sum } 2c(S_3) & (18/5,\,2/5) & (0.600,\,0.200)\\
\Koz(3;0,0)=\text{independent sum of }c(\Koz(3)) & (4,\,2/3) & (0.667,\,0.333)
\end{array}
\]
#+end_export
\(\FF(3;0,1)\) has \(22\) points (three of the \(25\) pairs collide) and equals the lattice-point
set of the \(3\)-polytope \(\Koz(3;0,1)\). In \((x_2,x_3,x_4)\):
#+begin_export latex
\[\small
\begin{array}{lcc}
 & (x_2,x_3,x_4) & (\nu_2,\nu_3,\nu_4)\\[2pt]
\FF(3;0,1)=\text{lattice points} & (51/11,\,2,\,5/22) & (0.773,\,0.500,\,0.227)\\
\text{independent sum of }c(S_3) & (24/5,\,2,\,1/5) & (0.800,\,0.500,\,0.200)\\
\Koz(3;0,1) & (37/8,\,7/3,\,3/8) & (0.771,\,0.583,\,0.375)\\
\text{independent sum of }c(\Koz(3)) & (5,\,7/3,\,1/3) & (0.833,\,0.583,\,0.333)
\end{array}
\]
#+end_export
The continuous centroid of \(\Koz(3;0,1)\) can be found by hand from a triangulation into five
tetrahedra of total volume \(6\), and was confirmed by an independent integration. In the product regime,
\(\FF(3;0,2)\), with \(\Koz(3;0,2)\) of dimension \(4\), all five centroids pair off as Proposition [[prop-pushforward]] says: \(\FF\), the
independent sum and the lattice points at \((1,3,\tfrac{14}5,\tfrac{16}5,\tfrac95,\tfrac15)\), the polytope
and its independent sum at \((1,3,3,\tfrac{10}3,2,\tfrac13)\).
#+end_mexample

# 2026-11-09 (Jan: "Adversarial review, then formalisation if feasible, then add it"). Was Conjecture
# conj-centroid-dilation. Proof: ../working-notes/centroid-dilation-corollary-2026-11-09.org, revised after an
# adversarial review (verdict "correct with fixable gaps"; all eight findings addressed: interpolation at
# t = 1..n+1, the polynomials of part (3) printed, c_k defined, w_{n-1} spelled out, kappa_n tied to (3),
# n >= 2). Checks: code/oneoff/centroid_dilation_check.py, centroid_dilation_Rk_polys.py. Lean (partial):
# LEAN/ExteriorConvex/Centroid/Dilation.lean. NOTE FOR JAN: not yet checked by you.
# 2026-11-13 (Jan, sweep item 4: is the leg vector integral in all that follows sec-lattice?). It is;
# but this proof and prop-dilates' called the centroid coordinate binom(n,k)(n-k+1)/n "c_k", the
# letter of the legs, and it is not an integer. Now \bar x_k.
# 2026-11-13 (Jan, sweep item 2): Lambda_t numbered once here (eq:Lambda), with index t; Remark
# rem-weak-convergence and prop-dilates cite it. B_k joined eq:Sigmak. In the proof kappa_n, which
# clashed with the bold Kozlov vector, is now theta_n.
#+NAME: thm-centroid-dilation
#+begin_theorem
\leanmark{\leanpartial}Fix \(n\ge2\) and, for an integer \(t\ge1\), let
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Lambda}
  \Lambda_t=t\Koz(n)\cap\mathbb Z^{n+1} .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
On \(r\Koz(n)\) the
coordinates \(x_0=r\) and \(x_1=nr\) are constant; for the others use \(\nu_k=x_k/(r\binom nk)\),
\(2\le k\le n\), the LYM coordinates divided by \(r\). The target values are those of
\(c(\Koz(n))\) at \(r=1\), \(\nu_k=\mu_k=(n-k+1)/n\) (Proposition [[prop-centroid-simplex]]).
1. For every \(r\ge1\) and \(2\le k\le n\),
   \[
     \bigl\lvert\nu_k\bigl(c(\FF(n;0^r))\bigr)-\nu_k\bigl(c(\Lambda_r)\bigr)\bigr\rvert\le1-\operatorname{fill}(n;0^r),
   \]
   and both \(1-\operatorname{fill}(n;0^r)\) and \(\nu_k(c(\Lambda_r))-(n-k+1)/n\) are \(O(1/r)\). Hence
   \(c(\FF(n;0^r))/r\to c(\Koz(n))\) as \(r\to\infty\), with error \(O(1/r)\) in every coordinate.
2. The covariance matrix of the uniform measure on \(\FF(n;0^r)\), divided by \(r^2\), tends to that
   of \(\Koz(n)\), with error \(O(1/r)\) in every entry.
3. For every \(r\ge1\) and \(2\le k\le n\), \(\bigl\lvert\nu_k\bigl(c(\FF(n;0^r))\bigr)-(n-k+1)/n\bigr\rvert\) is
   less than \(13/(16r)\) when \(n=4\), and less than \(199/(100r)\) when \(n=5\).
#+end_theorem

#+begin_proof
/This proof was found by the model; see the disclosure section./ Write \(F_r=\FF(n;0^r)\),
\(f_r=\operatorname{fill}(n;0^r)\), \(L=L_{\Koz(n)}\), \(a(r)=\lvert F_r\rvert\) as in \eqref{eq:ar}, and
\(\bar x_k=x_k\bigl(c(\Koz(n))\bigr)=\binom nk(n-k+1)/n\) for \(2\le k\le n\).

/Inclusion and range./ \(F_r=S_n+\dots+S_n\subseteq\Koz(n)+\dots+\Koz(n)=r\Koz(n)\), and \(F_r\)
consists of integer points, so \(F_r\subseteq\Lambda_r\) (this is also Corollary [[cor-additivity]]).
By Proposition [[prop-rank-one-H]] every point of \(r\Koz(n)\) has \(x_0=r\), \(x_1=nr\) and
\(r\ge x_2/\binom n2\ge\dots\ge x_n/\binom nn\ge0\), so \(0\le\nu_k\le1\) on \(\Lambda_r\).

/Mixing./ Let \(A\subseteq B\) be finite with \(A\ne\emptyset\), let \(g\) take values in \([0,1]\) on
\(B\), write \(\mathbb E_Xg\) for the average over \(X\), and \(f=\lvert A\rvert/\lvert B\rvert\). If
\(C=B\setminus A\) is non-empty, then \(\mathbb E_Bg=f\,\mathbb E_Ag+(1-f)\,\mathbb E_Cg\), so
\(\mathbb E_Ag-\mathbb E_Bg=(1-f)(\mathbb E_Ag-\mathbb E_Cg)\) and
\(\lvert\mathbb E_Ag-\mathbb E_Bg\rvert\le1-f\); if \(C\) is empty, both sides vanish. For \(A=F_r\),
\(B=\Lambda_r\) and the linear function \(g=\nu_k\) this is the inequality in (1).

/The fill rate./ In the proof of Corollary [[cor-zero-shift]], \(a(r)\) and \(L(r)\) are, for \(r\ge1\),
of the form \(\sum_{j=0}^{n-1}\binom{r-1}jw_j\) with constants \(w_j\) (different for the two), and the
top constants agree: both are \(\prod_{k=2}^n\binom nk\), the single non-zero entry of
\(\mathcal N^{n-1}\), times the first entry \(1\) of the row vector. So \(L-a\) is a polynomial in
\(r\) of degree at most \(n-2\), while \(L\) has degree \(n-1\) and leading coefficient
\(\prod_{k\ge2}\binom nk/(n-1)!=\vol\Koz(n)>0\). Hence \(1-f_r=(L(r)-a(r))/L(r)=O(1/r)\), and
\(r(1-f_r)\) tends to \(\theta_n=[r^{n-2}](L-a)/\vol\Koz(n)\).

/The lattice-point centroid./ Forgetting \(x_0\) and \(x_1\) maps \(t\Koz(n)\) onto \(tK\), where
\(K=\lbrace1\ge y_2/\binom n2\ge\dots\ge y_n/\binom nn\ge0\rbrace\subseteq\mathbb R^{n-1}\) is a
full-dimensional lattice simplex; as in Section [[#sec-volume]] the map is a lattice-preserving
isometry on the affine hull, so it maps \(\Lambda_t\) onto \(tK\cap\mathbb Z^{n-1}\), \(c(\Koz(n))\)
onto \(c(K)\), and \(\vol\Koz(n)=\vol K\). For \(2\le k\le n\) let
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Qk}
  Q_k=\bigl\lbrace(y,z)\in\mathbb R^{n-1}\times\mathbb R:\ y\in K,\ 0\le z\le y_k\bigr\rbrace .
\end{equation}
#+end_export
It is the convex hull of the points \((v,0)\) and \((v,v_k)\), \(v\) a vertex of \(K\), since a point
\((y,sy_k)\) with \(y=\sum_v\lambda_vv\) and \(0\le s\le1\) equals
\(\sum_v\lambda_v\bigl((1-s)(v,0)+s(v,v_k)\bigr)\). So \(Q_k\) is a lattice polytope; it has dimension
\(n\), because \(y_k>0\) on the interior of \(K\), and volume \(\int_Ky_k\,dy=\vol K\cdot\bar x_k\) by
Fubini. As \(tQ_k=\lbrace(y,z):y\in tK,\ 0\le z\le y_k\rbrace\) and \(y_k\) is a non-negative integer
at a lattice point,
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Sigmak}
\begin{gathered}
  \#\bigl(tQ_k\cap\mathbb Z^n\bigr)=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_t}(x_k+1)=\Sigma_k(t)+L(t),\qquad
  \Sigma_k(t)=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_t}x_k ,\\
  B_k(t)=\Sigma_k(t)-\bar x_k\,t\,L(t) .
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
#+end_export
By Ehrhart's theorem [cite:@BeckRobins2015], applied to \(Q_k\) and to \(K\), \(\Sigma_k\) agrees for
\(t\ge1\) with a polynomial of degree \(n\) with leading coefficient \(\vol\Koz(n)\,\bar x_k\), so
\(B_k\) has degree at most \(n-1\). Therefore
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:betak}
  \nu_k\bigl(c(\Lambda_t)\bigr)-\frac{n-k+1}n=\frac{B_k(t)}{\binom nk\,t\,L(t)}
  =\frac{\beta_k}t+O\Bigl(\frac1{t^2}\Bigr),\qquad \beta_k=\frac{[t^{n-1}]B_k}{\binom nk\vol\Koz(n)} ,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
which completes (1); moreover
\(\limsup_{r\to\infty}r\,\lvert\nu_k(c(F_r))-(n-k+1)/n\rvert\le\theta_n+\lvert\beta_k\rvert\). The
constants \(\beta_k\) computed below at \(n=4,5\) are the first-order corrections of
Proposition [[prop-dilates]].

/Second moments./ For \(2\le k,l\le n\) the mixing step with \(g=\nu_k\nu_l\in[0,1]\) gives
\(\lvert\mathbb E_{F_r}[\nu_k\nu_l]-\mathbb E_{\Lambda_r}[\nu_k\nu_l]\rvert\le1-f_r\). The polytope
\(Q_{kl}=\lbrace(y,z,w):y\in K,\ 0\le z\le y_k,\ 0\le w\le y_l\rbrace\) is, by the same
interpolation in \(z\) and in \(w\) separately, the convex hull of the points
\((v,\varepsilon v_k,\eta v_l)\) with \(\varepsilon,\eta\in\lbrace0,1\rbrace\); it has dimension \(n+1\)
(also for \(k=l\)) and volume \(\int_Ky_ky_l\,dy\), and
\(\#(tQ_{kl}\cap\mathbb Z^{n+1})=\sum_{x\in\Lambda_t}(x_k+1)(x_l+1)\). So
\(\sum_{x\in\Lambda_t}x_kx_l\) agrees for \(t\ge1\) with a polynomial of degree \(n+1\) with leading
coefficient \(\int_Ky_ky_l\,dy\), and \(\mathbb E_{\Lambda_t}[\nu_k\nu_l]\) is within \(O(1/t)\) of the
corresponding moment \(\mathbb E[\mu_k\mu_l]\) of \(\Koz(n)\). With (1), and since all these numbers
lie in \([0,1]\), where \(\lvert ab-a'b'\rvert\le\lvert a-a'\rvert+\lvert b-b'\rvert\), the covariance
\(\mathbb E_{F_r}[\nu_k\nu_l]-\mathbb E_{F_r}[\nu_k]\,\mathbb E_{F_r}[\nu_l]\) is within \(O(1/r)\) of the
covariance of \(\mu_k\) and \(\mu_l\) on \(\Koz(n)\). This is (2) in the coordinates \(\nu\); in the
coordinates \(x\) both covariances are multiplied by \(\binom nk\binom nl\), and \(x_0\), \(x_1\) have
variance \(0\) on both sides.

/The constants in (3)./ By Corollary [[cor-deficiency]], \(1-f_r=2r/((r+1)(4r+1))<1/(2r)\) at \(n=4\),
and \(1-f_r=r(205r+71)/(125r^3+250r^2+105r+6)<41/(25r)\) at \(n=5\); the two bounds are \(\theta_4/r\)
and \(\theta_5/r\). By the degree bounds just proved, \(L\) and \(\Sigma_k\) are determined by their values
at \(t=1,\dots,n+1\), and computed from these (and checked at \(t=n+2\)) they give, at \(n=4\),
with \(L(t)=(t+1)^2(4t+1)\),
\[
  B_2=-\tfrac12t(t+1)(5t+3),\qquad B_3=-\tfrac12t(t+1)(8t+5),\qquad B_4=-\tfrac14t(t+1)(5t+3),
\]
so \(\beta=(-\tfrac5{48},-\tfrac14,-\tfrac5{16})\); and at \(n=5\), with
\(6L(t)=(t+1)(125t^3+250t^2+105t+6)\) and \(v(t)=125t^2+165t-2\),
\[
  B_2=-\tfrac16t(t+1)v,\qquad B_3=-\tfrac13t(t+1)v,\qquad B_4=-\tfrac14t(t+1)v,
\]
\[
  B_5=-\tfrac1{120}t(t+1)(875t^2+1095t-86),
\]
so \(\beta=(-\tfrac1{10},-\tfrac15,-\tfrac3{10},-\tfrac7{20})\). Every \(B_k\) is negative for \(t\ge1\),
and \(\lvert\beta_k\rvert\binom nkL+B_k\) equals, at \(n=4\),
\(\tfrac18(t+1)(13t+5)\), \(\tfrac12(t+1)(5t+2)\), \(\tfrac1{16}(t+1)(13t+5)\), and at \(n=5\),
\(\tfrac16(t+1)(17t+1)(5t+6)\), \(\tfrac13(\dots)\), \(\tfrac14(\dots)\) (the same product) and
\(\tfrac1{120}(t+1)(131t+7)(5t+6)\), all positive. So
\(\lvert\nu_k(c(\Lambda_t))-(n-k+1)/n\rvert=-B_k/(\binom nk\,t\,L)\le\lvert\beta_k\rvert/t\) for every
\(t\ge1\), with \(\lvert\beta_k\rvert\le5/16\) at \(n=4\) and \(\le7/20\) at \(n=5\). Adding:
\(1/2+5/16=13/16\) and \(41/25+7/20=199/100\).
#+end_proof

Theorem [[thm-n3-equal-shifts]] is the case \(n=3\), exactly; for \(n\le3\) every lattice point of the
dilate is an ff-vector (Corollary [[cor-small-n]]), so the content is at \(n\ge4\). There the missing
lattice points are of lower order (Corollary [[cor-zero-shift]]), and that alone forces the centroid;
which lattice points are missing does not matter. The bounds are far from sharp. At \(n=4\), \(r=10\),
the centroid of the ff-vectors, \(\nu=(0.744,0.468,0.217)\), is within \(0.008\) of the lattice-point
centroid and within \(0.033\) of \((3/4,1/2,1/4)\), against \(1-\operatorname{fill}=0.044\) and
\(13/160\approx0.081\); at \(n=5\), \(r=9\), it is within \(0.018\) of the lattice-point centroid,
against \(1-\operatorname{fill}=0.154\). Part (2) says that at equal shifts the uniform measure on
the Hilbert functions spreads like the polytope \(r\Koz(n)\), quadratically in \(r\), and not like the
independent sum, whose covariance grows linearly (Proposition [[prop-pushforward]](3)). At a constant
positive gap the fill rate tends to \(0\) (Observation [[obs-fill]]), and the argument says nothing.

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, answer to item 6 of article3-sweep-2026-11-14b.org: "Yes, add that. Maybe 'at fixed
# n, as r -> infty' to be very explicit"). Mean vertex indices: 2.43 from the nu-centroid quoted above,
# 2.07 = baryMean of S_4 (runs/2026-11-14_profile-large-n.txt).
In the barycentric reading of Theorem [[thm-alexander-core]](3), part (1) says that the offset found
there does not survive dilation. At rank one the \(f\)-vectors sit about one vertex lower than the
simplex as \(n\) grows, whereas at fixed \(n\), as \(r\to\infty\), the mean vertex index of
\(c(\FF(n;0^r))/r\) tends to \((n+1)/2\), that of \(c(\Koz(n))\), with error \(O(1/r)\). At \(n=4\) it is
\(2.07\) for \(r=1\) and \(2.43\) for \(r=10\), against \(2.5\).

Figure \ref{fig:mu-rank-r} shows Corollary [[cor-zero-shift]] and Theorem [[thm-centroid-dilation]] at
\(n=6\), in the coordinates of Figure \ref{fig:mu}.

# 2026-11-10 (Jan, round 5: "put the n=6 figure in the paper"). Made by ../code/oneoff/fig_mu_rank_r.py;
# counts in img/README.org. |FF| = 457, 9904, 67448 (Part II's tables for r = 2).
#+begin_export latex
\begin{figure}[htbp]
\centering
\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{img/fig-mu-rank-r-n6.pdf}
\caption{The ff-vectors of $\FF(6;0^r)$, $r=1,2,3$, projected to the coordinates
$(\nu_2,\nu_3)=\bigl(x_2/(r\binom62),x_3/(r\binom63)\bigr)$ of \eqref{eq:nu}, in which
$\Koz(6;0^r)=r\Koz(6)$ projects onto the triangle $1\ge\nu_2\ge\nu_3\ge0$. The panel $r=1$ is the
middle panel of Figure~\ref{fig:mu}; the conventions are those of that figure, with dot areas scaled
by $1/r^2$ along with the grid. The dashed curve $r\nu_3=G(r\nu_2)$,
$G(v)=\lfloor v\rfloor+(v-\lfloor v\rfloor)^{3/2}$, is the upper edge of the Minkowski sum of $r$
copies of the region below $\mu_3=\mu_2^{3/2}$, scaled by $1/r$: the Kruskal--Katona boundary
becomes $r$ arches. The lattice points hit by no ff-vector, $51$ of $171$, $102$ of $641$ and $153$
of $1411$, shrink to thin lenses along the diagonal (Corollary~\ref{cor-zero-shift}), and the
centroid of the ff-vectors ($\star$) approaches that of the polytope ($\diamond$)
(Theorem~\ref{thm-centroid-dilation}).}
\label{fig:mu-rank-r}
\end{figure}
#+end_export

# 2026-11-09 (Jan, round-2 report item 21.2: "Add that"). From ../working-notes/centroid-dilation-corollary-
# 2026-11-09.org, section "Remark: weak convergence". NOTE FOR JAN: not yet checked by you.
#+NAME: rem-weak-convergence
#+begin_remark
(/Weak convergence./) The proof of Theorem [[thm-centroid-dilation]] gives more. Write, as there,
\(K\subseteq\mathbb R^{n-1}\) for the projection of \(\Koz(n)\) to the coordinates
\((x_2,\dots,x_n)\), and \(\Lambda_t\) as in \eqref{eq:Lambda}. For a monomial
\(x^\alpha=\prod_{k\ge2}x_k^{\alpha_k}\), add to \(K\) one coordinate \(0\le z_i\le y_{k(i)}\) for each of
its \(\lvert\alpha\rvert\) factors, \(k(i)\) being the index of the \(i\)-th factor. Over \(y\in K\) the
fibre is a box, so the resulting polytope is the
convex hull of the points \((v,\text{corner of the box over }v)\), \(v\) a vertex of \(K\), a lattice
polytope whose dilate by \(t\) has \(\sum_{x\in\Lambda_t}\prod_k(x_k+1)^{\alpha_k}\) lattice points. By
induction on \(\lvert\alpha\rvert\), \(\sum_{x\in\Lambda_t}x^\alpha\) agrees for \(t\ge1\) with a polynomial
of degree \(n-1+\lvert\alpha\rvert\) whose leading coefficient is \(\int_Ky^\alpha\,dy\). With the mixing
step of that proof for \(g=\nu^\alpha\in[0,1]\), every moment of the uniform measure on \(\FF(n;0^r)/r\) converges to
the corresponding moment of the uniform probability measure on \(\Koz(n)\). All these measures live on
the compact set \(\Koz(n)\), where polynomials are dense in the continuous functions
(Stone--Weierstrass), so the uniform measure on \(\FF(n;0^r)/r\) converges weakly to the uniform
measure on \(\Koz(n)\). Parts (1) and (2) of the theorem are its first two moments, with a rate.
#+end_remark

# 2026-11-10 (Fable review, Part 2, Organisation 8): this subsection, about a first moment, moved here
# from sec-moments.
** Lattice points of dilates

# 2026-11-11 (Jan, round-4 report item 4: "go with D"). The paragraph cited Brion-Vergne for a weighted
# Euler-Maclaurin statement that they prove only for weight 1 (Jan read the paper and could not find it;
# neither could Claude). Part III uses only weight 1 and the linear weights x_k, and the linear case
# reduces to weight 1 below. Beck-Robins Theorem 5.6 checked in the authors' final draft of the second
# edition. Check, including the boundary term for Koz(n), n <= 5:
# code/oneoff/brion_vergne_second_coefficient_check.py, runs/2026-11-11_brion-vergne-second-coefficient-check.txt.
# NOTE FOR JAN: the reduction through Q is not yet checked by you.
For a lattice polytope \(P\) of dimension \(D\), the Ehrhart polynomial \(L_P\) of
Section [[#sec-ehrhart]] has leading coefficient the relative volume of \(P\), and next coefficient
half the sum of the relative volumes of its facets [cite:@BeckRobins2015 Theorem 5.6]. The same
holds for the sum of a linear form \(\ell\) with integer coefficients, nonnegative on \(P\). As in
Remark [[rem-weak-convergence]], the lattice polytope \(Q=\{(x,z):x\in P,\ 0\le z\le\ell(x)\}\) has
\(\sum_{x\in tP}(\ell(x)+1)\) lattice points in \(tQ\). Its facets are \(P\); the graph of \(\ell\)
over \(P\), of the same relative volume; and, over each facet \(F\) of \(P\), the region under
\(\ell\), of relative volume \(\int_F\ell\). So
\[
  \sum_{x\in tP\cap\mathbb Z^{N+1}}\ell(x)=L_Q(t)-L_P(t)
  =t^{D+1}\int_P\ell+\frac{t^D}2\sum_F\int_F\ell+O(t^{D-1}),
\]
all integrals with the lattice-normalised measures. For polynomial weights on a simple lattice
polytope, such as \(\Koz(n)\), the corresponding statement follows from the Euler--Maclaurin formula of Brion and Vergne
[cite:@BrionVergne1997 Theorem 2.15]. For \(\Koz(n)\), with \(\ell=x_k\), this predicts the lattice
centroid of \(t\Koz(n)\) to be \(t\,c(\Koz(n))+b+O(1/t)\) for a constant vector \(b\).

# 2026-11-09 (Jan, round-2 report item 21.1: "Yes, compute that"). Was Observation obs-dilates (verified only).
# Exact computation: code/oneoff/lattice_centroid_dilates_exact.py, output runs/2026-11-09_lattice-centroid-dilates-exact.txt;
# the n = 4, 5 polynomials are those already in the proof of cor-centroid-dilation. NOTE FOR JAN: not yet checked by you.
#+NAME: prop-dilates
#+begin_proposition
\leanmark{\leannone}(/The lattice-point centroid of \(t\Koz(n)\), \(n\le5\)./) Let \(\Lambda_t\) be as in
\eqref{eq:Lambda} and, as in Theorem [[thm-centroid-dilation]], \(\nu_k=x_k/(t\binom nk)\), the LYM coordinates of
\(t\Koz(n)\) divided by \(t\). For every integer \(t\ge1\) the coordinates of the lattice-point centroid
\(c(\Lambda_t)\) are as follows.
1. For \(n=3\): \(\nu_2=\frac23-\frac1{3(3t+2)}\) and \(\nu_3=\frac13-\frac2{3(3t+2)}\).
2. For \(n=4\): \(\nu_2=\frac34-\frac{5t+3}{12(t+1)(4t+1)}\), \(\nu_3=\frac12-\frac{8t+5}{8(t+1)(4t+1)}\) and
   \(\nu_4=\frac14-\frac{5t+3}{4(t+1)(4t+1)}\).
3. For \(n=5\), with \(u(t)=125t^3+250t^2+105t+6\) and \(v(t)=125t^2+165t-2\):
   \(\nu_2=\frac45-\frac{v}{10u}\), \(\nu_3=\frac35-\frac v{5u}\), \(\nu_4=\frac25-\frac{3v}{10u}\) and
   \(\nu_5=\frac15-\frac{875t^2+1095t-86}{20u}\).
Every correction is negative, and to first order the centroid is
#+begin_export latex
\[\small
\begin{array}{ll}
n=3: & \bigl(\tfrac23-\tfrac1{9t},\ \tfrac13-\tfrac2{9t}\bigr)\\[2pt]
n=4: & \bigl(\tfrac34-\tfrac5{48t},\ \tfrac12-\tfrac1{4t},\ \tfrac14-\tfrac5{16t}\bigr)\\[2pt]
n=5: & \bigl(\tfrac45-\tfrac1{10t},\ \tfrac35-\tfrac1{5t},\ \tfrac25-\tfrac3{10t},\ \tfrac15-\tfrac7{20t}\bigr)
\end{array}
\]
#+end_export
up to \(O(1/t^2)\).
#+end_proposition

#+begin_proof
As in the proof of Theorem [[thm-centroid-dilation]], the Ehrhart polynomial
\(L_{\Koz(n)}(t)=\lvert\Lambda_t\rvert\) and the sum \(\Sigma_k(t)\) of \eqref{eq:Sigmak} agree for
\(t\ge1\) with polynomials of degree \(n-1\) and \(n\), so they are determined by their values at
\(t=1,\dots,n+1\). Computing these values by listing \(\Lambda_t\) (and checking at \(t=n+2\) and
\(t=n+3\)) gives \(L_{\Koz(n)}\) and the \(B_k\) of \eqref{eq:Sigmak}: for \(n=3\),
\(L_{\Koz(3)}(t)=\tfrac12(t+1)(3t+2)\), \(B_2=-\tfrac12t(t+1)\) and \(B_3=-\tfrac13t(t+1)\); for
\(n=4,5\), the polynomials displayed at the end of the proof of Theorem [[thm-centroid-dilation]].
Then, by \eqref{eq:betak},
\(\nu_k\bigl(c(\Lambda_t)\bigr)-(n-k+1)/n=B_k(t)/\bigl(\binom nk\,t\,L_{\Koz(n)}(t)\bigr)\), and
cancelling \(t(t+1)\) gives the formulas. Their numerators \(5t+3\), \(8t+5\), \(v(t)\) and
\(875t^2+1095t-86\) are
positive for \(t\ge1\), so every correction is negative; expanding in \(1/t\) gives the table.
#+end_proof

The \(1/t\) corrections agree with the boundary term computed independently from the facets.
Heuristically they are negative because, for small \(t\), the facet \(\mu_n=0\) carries nearly all
lattice points.

* Discrete moments versus moments
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: sec-moments
:END:

#+NAME: prop-second-moments
#+begin_proposition
1. \leanmark{\leannone}For the uniform measure on a \(D\)-simplex with vertices \(v_0,\dots,v_D\) and \(s=\sum_av_a\),
   \(\mathbb E[x_ix_j]=\bigl(\sum_av_{a,i}v_{a,j}+s_is_j\bigr)/\bigl((D+1)(D+2)\bigr)\).
2. For \(\Koz(n)\), in LYM coordinates and for \(2\le k\le l\le n\),
   \(\operatorname{Cov}(\mu_k,\mu_l)=(k-1)(n-l+1)/\bigl(n^2(n+1)\bigr)\). More precisely,
   \(\mu_2\ge\dots\ge\mu_n\) are the order statistics of \(n-1\) independent uniform variables on
   \([0,1]\), \(\mu_k\) being the \((n-k+1)\)-th smallest.
#+end_proposition

#+begin_proof
(1) A uniform point is \(\sum_a\lambda_av_a\) with \(\lambda\) Dirichlet\((1,\dots,1)\) on
\(m=D+1\) parameters, for which \(\mathbb E[\lambda_a\lambda_b]=(1+\delta_{ab})/(m(m+1))\). (2) In LYM
coordinates the vertices are \((1^j,0^{n-j})\), \(m=n\), \(\sum_jv_{j,k}v_{j,l}=n-l+1\) and
\(s_k=n-k+1\); with \(a=n-k+1\) and \(b=n-l+1\),
\(\mathbb E[\mu_k\mu_l]=(b+ab)/(n(n+1))\), and subtracting \(ab/n^2\) gives \(b(n-a)/(n^2(n+1))\). The
uniform measure on the order simplex is the law of the sorted sample.
#+end_proof

#+NAME: ex-moments-n3
#+begin_mexample
At \(n=3\), in \((x_2,x_3)\), the covariance of the five \(f\)-vectors is
\(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}34/25&6/25\\6/25&4/25\end{smallmatrix}\right)\), and that of the triangle
\(\left(\begin{smallmatrix}1/2&1/12\\1/12&1/18\end{smallmatrix}\right)\): the discrete set is
\(2.7\) times more spread in \(x_2\), because its five points sit on the boundary. For
\(\Koz(3;0,1)\), in \((x_2,x_3,x_4)\), the covariance of the \(22\) ff-vectors and that of the polytope
are
#+begin_export latex
\[\small
\begin{pmatrix}160/121&3/11&9/242\\3/11&18/11&3/11\\9/242&3/11&85/484\end{pmatrix}
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
\begin{pmatrix}47/64&7/48&1/64\\7/48&13/18&5/48\\1/64&5/48&13/192\end{pmatrix}.
\]
#+end_export
#+end_mexample

For small \(n\), then, the discrete measure is more spread than the continuous one; for large \(n\)
the opposite holds, and markedly. Tables \ref{tab:concentration} to \ref{tab:profile-n24} show this
at rank one for \(n\le26\), and Table \ref{tab:rank-r-variance} at rank two and three.

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 7: "Can some tables be added to sec-moments, in particular, to illustrate
# obs-concentration? Strong suggestion."). Three rank-one tables and one rank-r table. The inline list
# n*sd = 1.875, ... is now column 3 of tab:concentration. Every number is from
# runs/2026-11-14_profile-large-n.txt (code/oneoff/profile_large_n.py, the chain DP, rerun; identical to
# the 2026-10-20 log) typeset by code/oneoff/moments_tables.py; independently,
# code/oneoff/moments_tables_check.sage (runs/2026-11-14_moments-tables-check.txt) recomputes the rank-r
# table from S_n built out of all complexes, plain sumsets and a pulling triangulation, and the rank-one
# columns for n = 4, 6, 8 by enumeration. All agree. The four tables are floats (table[htbp]), unlike
# the article's other tables: as non-floating center blocks, Table 7's caption fell onto the next page.
#+NAME: obs-concentration
#+begin_observation
(/The \(f\)-vectors concentrate./) For even \(n\le26\), the standard deviation of the middle
coordinate \(\mu_{n/2}\) under the uniform measure on \(S_n\) is about \(1.78/n\) (Table
\ref{tab:concentration}), whereas both the lattice-point and the continuous measures have standard
deviation of order \(n^{-1/2}\); by Proposition [[prop-second-moments]] the continuous one is
\(\sqrt{(k-1)(n-k+1)/(n^2(n+1))}\approx1/(2\sqrt n)\). The standard deviations of the middle coordinate
\(\mu_{\lceil n/2\rceil}\) for \(S_n\) and for \(\Koz(n)\), as functions of \(n\), cross between \(n=14\) and
\(n=15\). So, as \(n\) grows, a uniformly random proper \(f\)-vector has an increasingly
deterministic LYM profile, a sigmoid symmetric about \(k=n/2\) (Table \ref{tab:profile-n24}: at
\(n=24\), \(\mu\approx0.915\) at \(k=n/4\) and \(\approx0.085\) at \(3n/4\)). Let \(J\) be the barycentric
index: the index of a vertex drawn with probability \(\lambda_J\) at a random point. For the simplex
\(J\) is uniform on \([n]\), with variance \((n^2-1)/12\). Under the uniform measure on \(S_n\),
\(\operatorname{Var}(J)/n^2\) increases slowly, from \(0.026\) to \(0.032\) over \(12\le n\le26\),
against \(1/12\) for the simplex (Table \ref{tab:barycentric-index}). All verified by exact dynamic
programming, \(n\le26\).
#+end_observation

* Methods and verification
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: sec-methods
:END:

Every theorem above has a written proof. The two original groups of results (lattice points and
volume, centroids) were in addition re-derived line by line in an adversarial review by a second,
separately prompted instance of the language model, and checked by exact computation with code
written for that review, without access to the code used for the paper. The later results
(Theorem [[thm-centroid-dilation]], Kupreyeva's limit, and Theorem [[thm-codegree]] with its
companions) were each reviewed in the same way, line by line, and checked by computation as listed
below. None of these reviews was by a human colleague; the author's own check is described in the
disclosure section. The code, the run logs and the Lean library are in the public repository
[[https://gitlab.liu.se/jansn19/exterior-convex]]; below, file names refer to its directory
=verification-code/oneoff/=, and the run logs are under =verification-code/runs/=. A frozen snapshot of the code, the run logs and the Lean library is archived on Zenodo
[cite:@SnellmanZenodoSeries] under the DOI
[[https://doi.org/10.5281/zenodo.24288404][10.5281/zenodo.24288404]], which resolves to its latest version.

#+LATEX: \begingroup\sloppy

# 2026-11-14: inside *...* emphasis write h^{\ast}, not h^*. Org lets a "*" followed by "\" close the
# emphasis (backslash is in the post-characters of org-emphasis-regexp-components), so in
# "*... \(h^*\).*" the bold ended inside the fragment and the fragment was exported as escaped text,
# with no LaTeX error. h^{*} fails the same way. Jan's workaround of the same morning, a local macro
# \hstar, also worked; \ast needs no macro. code/build_article.sh now counts such spills.
- *Lattice points, volume and the staircase tiling.* Lattice-point counts by Normaliz
  [cite:@BrunsIchim2010], by direct sumsets and by chained tuples; normalised volumes by Normaliz
  against Theorem [[thm-staircase]] (\(97\) cases); the lattice-path reading of staircase indices
  (Example [[ex-staircase-paths]]) for \(n\le7\), \(r\le4\) and all gaps at most \(n-1\), and its
  identification at \(\vek d=\vek 0\) with the staircase triangulation (Remark
  [[rem-staircase-cayley]]) for \(n\le5\), \(r\le4\) (=staircase_lattice_paths.py=).
- *Ehrhart series and \(h^{\ast}\).* Ehrhart series by Normaliz against Corollary [[cor-transfer]]
  wherever \(\NV\le3\cdot10^7\), and for four rank-two polytopes, \(\Koz(6;0,4)\) among them, also by
  Barvinok's algorithm, in the =barvinok= library [cite:@Verdoolaege2007]; \(h^*\) by the ascent
  formula, by the \(\vek s\)-Eulerian sum (\(n\le6\)) and by Normaliz (\(n\le7\)), the \(n=8\) vector by
  the ascent formula alone. Script: =kozlov_volume_ehrhart.py=, review
  =review_volume_ehrhart_check.py=. Real-rootedness of the \(h^*\)-polynomials in
  Observation [[obs-hstar-unimodal]] by exact real-root counting with Sturm sequences, a count equal
  to the degree meaning that all roots are real (=hstar_realrooted_check.py=).
- *Codegree, quadratic coefficient, deficiency.*
  Theorem [[thm-codegree]], Corollary [[cor-gorenstein]] and Proposition [[prop-quadratic-coeff]] against a
  separately written transfer-matrix computation for every \(\vek d\) with every gap at most \(n\),
  \(3\le n\le7\) and rank up to \(5\), \(4\), \(3\) (\(1854\) cases), and against the stored \(h^*\)-vectors; the
  codegree and the interior lattice points also from convex hulls computed in SageMath for \(n=3,4\),
  and the interior points of Lemma [[lem-interior-points]] by membership tests
  (=hstar_codegree_t2_check.py=, =hstar_codegree_sage_crosscheck.sage=,
  =interior_points_direct_check.py=). The values in the proof of Corollary [[cor-deficiency]]: sumsets of
  \(S_n\) listed by the Kruskal--Katona theorem, and integer LYM chains, \(n=4,5\), \(r\le5\)
  (=zero_shift_deficiency.py=); at \(n=4\) also by enumerating every complex on \([4]\).
- *Centroids and moments.* Exact dynamic programming over the chain sets (\(n\le26\)), sumsets,
  triangulation integrals in SageMath, Normaliz integrals, Kupreyeva's tables. Scripts:
  =centroids_moments.py=, =centroids_moments.sage=, =profile_large_n.py=, review
  =review_centroids_check.py=. The tables of Section [[#sec-moments]] are typeset from a rerun of
  =profile_large_n.py= (=moments_tables.py=); Table \ref{tab:rank-r-variance},
  Observation [[obs-pushforward-fails]] and the rank-one columns for \(n=4,6,8\) were recomputed
  without that code, from \(S_n\) built out of all complexes on \([n]\), plain sumsets and a pulling
  triangulation of \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) (=moments_tables_check.sage=). For Theorem [[thm-centroid-dilation]]: the inequalities of its proof,
  the polynomial degrees and leading coefficients, and the constants, by exact enumeration for
  \(n\le6\) (=centroid_dilation_check.py=); the polynomials \(L\), \(\Sigma_k\), \(B_k\) of part (3), by
  interpolation at \(t=1,\dots,n+1\) and a check at \(t=n+2\) (=centroid_dilation_Rk_polys.py=). For
  Proposition [[prop-dilates]]: the same interpolation for \(n=3,4,5\), checked at \(t=n+2,n+3\)
  (=lattice_centroid_dilates_exact.py=).
- *Kupreyeva's limit.* Exact counts of \(S_n^{(p)}\) by Linusson's recursion and, independently, by
  sums of pseudo-powers; lattice points by floor sums; both checked against brute-force sumsets for
  \(p\le7\). Fill rates for \(n=3\) (\(p\le30000\)), \(n=4\) (\(p\le2000\)) and, at rank one,
  \(n=5\) (\(p\le120\)); the limits in exact rational arithmetic; the volumes of the Minkowski sums
  \(R_n+\dots+R_n\) for \(n\le5\), \(r\le3\) also by numerical quadrature. Scripts:
  =kupreyeva_limit_rank_r.py=, =kupreyeva_qn_volume_ratio.py=.
- *Figures.* =ff_pictures.py=; Figure \ref{fig:mu-rank-r} by =fig_mu_rank_r.py=.
#+LATEX: \endgroup

** Formal verification
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: sec-lean
:END:

# 2026-10-29, at Jan's request: shortened to a legend; the status of each result is now a disc in
# the margin, inserted by ../code/leanmarks_article3.py. The itemised list of Lean files and
# declarations that stood here (2026-10-21) is kept verbatim in
# ../working-notes/formalisation-campaign.org, section "Article III".
Much of this paper is also proved in the proof assistant Lean 4, on top of its mathematical
library Mathlib [cite:@deMouraUllrich2021;@Mathlib2020]. The Lean files are in the directory
=lean/= of the public repository [[https://gitlab.liu.se/jansn19/exterior-convex]] and of its
snapshot on Zenodo [cite:@SnellmanZenodoSeries], with the code and the run logs described above. A
mark in the margin beside a result, or beside one of its parts, records how far the formal proof
goes:
- \leanfull{} proved in Lean, with no =sorry=, from the three axioms that Mathlib uses throughout
  (propositional extensionality, choice and the soundness of quotients) and nothing else;
- \leancond{} proved in the same way, assuming the surjectivity half of Part II's chaining
  reformulation [cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Theorems 23 and 41, with Lemma 40], which enters as an
  explicit hypothesis of the formal statement, not as an axiom;
- \leanpartial{} part of the statement proved as for \leanfull{}: a special case or the
  combinatorial core;
- \leannone{} not formalised.
The file =lean/README.md=, in the repository and in the snapshot,
pairs each result with its formal statement, says what a partial mark covers, and records where a
formal definition differs in form from the printed one (the normalised volume, the uniform measure
on a dilate).

* Open problems
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: sec-open
:END:

1. *Ehrhart theory at rank \(r\).* Unimodality and real-rootedness of \(h^*_{\Koz(n;\vek d)}\)
   (Observation [[obs-hstar-unimodal]]); Conjecture [[conj-nv-monotone]]; and the signs of the Ehrhart
   coefficients beyond \(t^2\), which Propositions [[prop-linear-coeff]] and [[prop-quadratic-coeff]]
   reduce to ranks one and two.
2. *Fill rates.* Prove that \(\lvert S_n\rvert/\lvert\mathcal L_n\rvert\to0\), find its rate, and prove
   that the Perron-root ratio of Observation [[obs-fill]] is below \(1\) for \(n\ge4\) and every gap
   \(\delta\ge1\). At equal shifts, find the polynomial \(L_{\Koz(n)}(r)-a(r)\) for general \(n\):
   it vanishes for \(n\le3\), and at \(n=4,5\) (Corollary [[cor-deficiency]]) it is divisible by
   \(r(r+1)\). Is it always?
3. *Kupreyeva's limit beyond the first order.* Theorems [[thm-kupreyeva-limit]] and
   [[thm-kupreyeva-rank-r]] give the limit of \(\operatorname{fill}^{(p)}(n;\vek d)\) as
   \(p\to\infty\). In every computed case (Example [[ex-kupreyeva-values]]),
   \(p\bigl(\operatorname{fill}^{(p)}(n;\vek d)-\lim\bigr)\) appears to converge, to about
   \(-4/5\) at \(n=3\), \(r=1\) and \(-1.66\) at \(n=4\), \(r=1\). Find the second-order term,
   which should come from the lattice points near the Kruskal--Katona boundary; at rank one the
   error is \(O(1/p)\) (Remark [[rem-kupreyeva-linusson]]), and at rank \(r\) its order is not proved.
   And relate \(q_n\) to the fill rate at \(p=n\), where open problem 2 asks for the
   rate.
4. *Centroids.* The analogue of Theorem [[thm-centroid-dilation]] at a constant positive gap, where
   the fill rate tends to \(0\) and its argument fails; the limiting LYM profile of the centroid of
   \(S_n\) as \(n\to\infty\), and the constant in Observation [[obs-concentration]]; a rank-\(r\) analogue
   of the involution \(\beta\), which would have to come from a duality of modules, since
   \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) is not a simplex.
# 2026-11-13 (Jan, sweep item 9): open problems 5 and 6 removed, as not immediately related to this
# part of the series. They read:
#  5. *Beyond proper monomial submodules.* Amata and Crupi's theorem
#     [cite:@AmataCrupi2020KK Theorem 4.2] (see also [cite:@SnellmanArticle5]) describes the Hilbert
#     functions of all graded quotients of \(F\), with arbitrary generator degrees. Describe their convex
#     hull, compare it with \(\Koz(n;\vek d)\) and with the permissive hull of Part I, and determine which
#     of its lattice points are achievable; the theorem is a criterion, not yet a geometric description.
#  6. *The \(\mathbb Z^r\)-graded refinement.* The \(\mathbb Z^r\)-graded Hilbert function of an ids-module
#     is a tuple of independent \(f\)-vectors, so its achievable set is a product. Determine the set of
#     \(\mathbb Z^r\)-graded Hilbert functions of Amata--Crupi lex submodules inside that product, and its
#     convex hull, a canonical section of the map from the product to the sumset.
# 2026-11-09 (Jan): open problem 7 removed for now, as somewhat out of scope. It read:
#  7. *Sköldberg's equivalence at the module level*, transporting Betti numbers between modules over the
#     exterior face ring and over the artinified Stanley--Reisner ring; and the lifting of Gröbner bases of
#     submodules of \(F\) to the free associative algebra, where the "squeezing" correction of Nilsson and
#     Snellman [cite:@NilssonSnellman2003] is defined by term parts only.


#+begin_export latex
\appendix
#+end_export

* Proof of the canonical chained decomposition
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: sec-app-chained
:END:

This appendix proves Theorem [[thm-chained]]. The notation is that of Section [[#sec-lattice]]: an
integral leg vector \(\vek c\) with \((c_2,\dots,c_n)\) log-concave where needed, a shift vector
\(\vek d\) with gaps \(\delta_i\) \eqref{eq:gaps}, the LYM coordinates \(\mu_k=p_k/c_k\) \eqref{eq:mu} of a point \(\vek p\) of
\(\rasimp(\vek c)\), its statistics \(\fl\) and \(\tp\) of \eqref{eq:fl} and \eqref{eq:tp}, and the
water-filling quantities \(Q_i(k)\) of \eqref{eq:water}. A remark on what is new closes it.

#+ATTR_LATEX: :options [Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm-chained}]
#+begin_proof
/Uniqueness and the formula./ Let the tuple be chained and fix a global degree \(k\). If summand
\(i\) is non-zero in degree \(k\), then \(k-d_i\le\tp(\vek p_i)\), and chaining gives
\(k\le\fl(\vek p_l)+d_l\) for every \(l>i\), so every later summand is full in degree \(k\) (or
has capacity \(c_{k-d_l}=0\) there). Hence the summands, read in the order of \(i\), are zero, then
at most one partial, then full, and the amount carried by summands \(1,\dots,i\) in degree \(k\) is
\(Q_i(k)\). The possible values of \(x_k\) are the partial capacity sums
\(C_{\ge a}=\sum_{l\ge a}c_{k-d_l}\) and the open intervals between consecutive ones, and these are
pairwise disjoint because every \(c_s\) present is positive. So \(x_k\) determines each summand's
coordinate in degree \(k\), and every own coordinate of every summand occurs in some global degree.

# REWRITTEN 2026-10-21 at Jan's request to follow the Lean proof (Lattice/ChainedExistence.lean,
# exists_chained_lym): one linear functional maximised instead of a lexicographic minimiser, and
# the lowered block ends at tp(p_i) instead of the least drop j_1. The previous text is in git.
/Existence./ The set of representations of \(x\), tuples \((\mu^{(1)},\dots,\mu^{(r)})\) of LYM
chains \(1=\mu_1\ge\mu_2\ge\dots\ge\mu_n\ge0\) (\(\mu_k=(\vek p)_k/c_k\) as in \eqref{eq:mu}, cf. Proposition
[[prop-rank-one-H]]) with \(\sum_i\shift^{d_i}(\vek c\cdot\mu^{(i)})=x\), the product taken
entrywise, is a non-empty compact polytope. On it the linear functional
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:Psi}
  \Psi=\sum_{i=1}^r i\,\bigl\lVert\vek p_i\bigr\rVert_1,\qquad
  \bigl\lVert\vek p_i\bigr\rVert_1=\sum_{j}c_j\mu^{(i)}_j ,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
attains its maximum. Take a maximiser and suppose it is not chained, at some \(i\): with
\(u=\fl(\vek p_{i+1})\), \(T=\tp(\vek p_i)\) and \(\delta=d_{i+1}-d_i\), we have \(T>u+\delta\).
Then \(u<T-\delta\le n\), so \(\mu^{(i+1)}_u=1>\mu^{(i+1)}_{u+1}\), and \(\mu^{(i)}_T>0=\mu^{(i)}_{T+1}\)
(with \(\mu_{n+1}=0\)). Put \(j_0=u+\delta+1\le T\). For small \(\varepsilon>0\) (two strict
inequalities, at the two ends below, fix how small) replace
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:exchange}
  \mu^{(i)}_j\mapsto\mu^{(i)}_j-\varepsilon,\qquad
  \mu^{(i+1)}_{j-\delta}\mapsto\mu^{(i+1)}_{j-\delta}+\varepsilon\,c_j/c_{j-\delta}\qquad(j_0\le j\le T).
\end{equation}
#+end_export
The global coordinate \(j+d_i=(j-\delta)+d_{i+1}\) changes by \(-\varepsilon c_j+\varepsilon c_j=0\),
so \(x\) is unchanged. Summand \(i\) remains a chain: it is lowered by the same amount on the whole
interval \([j_0,T]\), so only the two ends matter; at the left end lowering only helps, at the right
end \(\mu^{(i)}_T-\varepsilon\ge0=\mu^{(i)}_{T+1}\), and \(\mu^{(i)}_1=1\) is untouched since
\(j_0\ge2\). Summand \(i+1\) is raised on its own indices \(m\in[u+1,T-\delta]\subseteq[2,n]\) by
\(\eta_m=\varepsilon c_{m+\delta}/c_m\). Inside, for \(u+1\le m<T-\delta\), the chain needs
\(\eta_m\ge\eta_{m+1}\), that is \(c_{m+\delta}/c_m\ge c_{m+1+\delta}/c_{m+1}\), a product of
\(\delta\) comparisons \(c_{q+1}/c_q\ge c_{q+2}/c_{q+1}\) for \(m\le q\le m+\delta-1\), so
\(3\le q+1\le m+\delta\le T-1\le n-1\), which is the log-concavity of \((c_2,\dots,c_n)\). At the
right end raising only helps, and at the left end \(1=\mu_u\ge\mu_{u+1}+\eta_{u+1}\) for small
\(\varepsilon\), because \(\mu_{u+1}<1\); by the monotonicity just shown, every raised entry is
then at most \(1\). The exchange moves the mass
\(\varepsilon\sum_{j=j_0}^{T}c_j>0\) from summand \(i\) to summand \(i+1\):
\(\lVert\vek p_i\rVert_1\) falls by this amount and \(\lVert\vek p_{i+1}\rVert_1\) rises by
\(\sum_m\eta_mc_m\), the same amount. So \(\Psi\) rises by it, contradicting maximality.

/Integrality./ Each coordinate of each part is \(c_s\), \(0\), or \(x_k-C_{\ge a+1}\), an integer.
#+end_proof

#+NAME: rem-chained-credit
#+begin_remark
(/What is new./) At \(\vek d=(0,\dots,0)\) the lattice-point version of Theorem [[thm-chained]],
existence and uniqueness, is Brändén and Solus's decomposition theorem for lecture hall polytopes
[cite:@BrandenSolus2020 Theorem 2.1] (Theorem 1 in the arXiv version): their relation
\(\lambda\trianglelefteq\gamma\), "\(\lambda\le\gamma\), and \(\gamma_i=s_i\) wherever
\(\lambda_i\ne0\)", is chaining with \(\delta=0\), and their existence proof is the decomposition
\(\lambda=(\lambda\wedge s)+((\lambda-s)\vee0)\). For \(f\)-vectors and all \(\vek d\), uniqueness and the
water-filling formula are [cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Lemma 24], and existence of a chained tuple of /\(f\)-vectors/
for every ff-vector is the content of Part II's chaining reformulation, proved there from Amata and
Crupi's lex submodules. What is new here is positive gaps for arbitrary points, and that is where
log-concavity enters: for \(\delta=0\) the inside-block condition is vacuous. The hypothesis is
needed. For \(\vek c=(1,2,1,3,1,3)\) and \(\vek d=(0,1)\) the polytope has \(101\) lattice points,
the sumset of lattice points only \(70\), and existence fails.
#+end_remark

* Proof of Kupreyeva's limit
:PROPERTIES:
:CUSTOM_ID: sec-app-kupreyeva
:END:

The proofs in this appendix were found by the model; see the disclosure section. Throughout,
\(n\ge2\), \(p\ge2n+2\), \(\mu_k=x_k/\binom pk\) are the LYM coordinates \eqref{eq:mu}, and
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:gk}
  g_k(t)=t^{(k+1)/k},\qquad t\ge0 ,
\end{equation}
#+end_export
so that the upper boundary of \(R_n\) in \eqref{eq:Rn} is \(\mu_{k+1}=g_k(\mu_k)\).

# 2026-11-13 (Jan, sweep item 2: rename the clashing letters): K -> Omega and Lambda_h -> Gamma_h
# here; in Section sec-centroid K is the projection of Koz(n) and Lambda_t its lattice points.
#+NAME: lem-anisotropic-count
#+begin_lemma
\leanmark{\leannone}Let \(\Omega\subseteq\mathbb R^D\) be bounded, with boundary of Lebesgue measure zero, and for
\(\vek h\in(0,\infty)^D\) let \(\Gamma_{\vek h}=h_1\mathbb Z\times\dots\times h_D\mathbb Z\). Then
\(h_1\cdots h_D\cdot\#(\Gamma_{\vek h}\cap\Omega)\to\vol\Omega\) as \(\max_jh_j\to0\).
#+end_lemma

#+begin_proof
Put \(\eta=\max_jh_j\) and \(Q(y)=y+\prod_j[0,h_j)\) for \(y\in\Gamma_{\vek h}\). These boxes
partition \(\mathbb R^D\). Each has volume \(h_1\cdots h_D\) and contains \(y\), and any two of its
points differ by less than \(\eta\) in every coordinate. Let \(\overline\Omega_\eta\) be the set of
points at sup-distance at most \(\eta\) from the closure \(\overline\Omega\), and \(\Omega^\circ_{-\eta}\)
the set of points whose closed sup-ball of radius \(\eta\) lies in the interior \(\Omega^\circ\). If
\(y\in\Gamma_{\vek h}\cap\Omega\), then \(Q(y)\subseteq\overline\Omega_\eta\). If
\(z\in\Omega^\circ_{-\eta}\cap Q(y)\), then \(Q(y)\subseteq\Omega^\circ\), so \(y\in\Omega\). Hence
\[
  \vol\Omega^\circ_{-\eta}\le h_1\cdots h_D\cdot\#(\Gamma_{\vek h}\cap\Omega)\le\vol\overline\Omega_\eta .
\]
As \(\eta\to0\), \(\overline\Omega_\eta\) decreases to the compact set \(\overline\Omega\) and
\(\Omega^\circ_{-\eta}\) increases to the open set \(\Omega^\circ\). So the bounds tend to
\(\vol\overline\Omega\) and \(\vol\Omega^\circ\), both equal to \(\vol\Omega\) because \(\partial\Omega\) is null.
#+end_proof

#+NAME: lem-pseudo-power
#+begin_lemma
\leanmark{\leannone}Let \(k\ge1\) and \(p\ge2k+2\), and put \(\eta_s=s^2/(p-s+1)\). For every integer
\(0\le f\le\binom pk\), with \(\mu=f/\binom pk\),
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:epsk}
  \biggl\lvert\frac{f^{(k)}}{\binom p{k+1}}-\mu^{(k+1)/k}\biggr\rvert\le\varepsilon_k(p)
  :=\eta_{k+1}+\frac{(k-1)\,(k+1)!\,2^{k+1}}{p}+(k+1)\Bigl(\eta_k+\frac kp\Bigr)^{1/k},
\end{equation}
#+end_export
and \(\varepsilon_k(p)\to0\) as \(p\to\infty\).
#+end_lemma

#+begin_proof
If \(f=0\), both sides vanish. Otherwise let
\(f=\binom{a_k}k+\binom{a_{k-1}}{k-1}+\dots+\binom{a_t}t\) be the Macaulay representation
\eqref{eq:macaulay}, and put \(a=a_k\). Then \(\binom ak\le f<\binom{a+1}k\). Since \(f\le\binom pk\),
we have \(a\le p\), with \(a=p\) only if \(f=\binom pk\). Put \(\alpha=a/p\) and
\(\beta_s(b)=\binom bs/\binom ps\).

First, \(\lvert\beta_s(b)-(b/p)^s\rvert\le\eta_s\) for integers \(0\le b\le p\) and \(1\le s\le p\).
- If \(b\ge s-1\), then \(\beta_s(b)=\prod_{i<s}(b-i)/(p-i)\) and \((b/p)^s\) are products of \(s\)
  numbers in \([0,1]\). Their difference is at most
  \(\sum_{i<s}\lvert b/p-(b-i)/(p-i)\rvert=\sum_{i<s}i(p-b)/(p(p-i))\le s^2/(p-s+1)\).
- If \(b\le s-2\), then \(\beta_s(b)=0\) and \((b/p)^s\le s/p\le\eta_s\).

Next, \(\mu\) lies between \(\beta_k(a)\) and \(\beta_k(a+1)\), or equals \(1=\alpha^k\) if \(a=p\).
Since \(((a+1)/p)^k\le\alpha^k+k/p\), this gives \(\lvert\mu-\alpha^k\rvert\le\eta_k+k/p\). Using
\(\lvert u^{1/k}-v^{1/k}\rvert\le\lvert u-v\rvert^{1/k}\) for \(u,v\ge0\), and the fact that
\(t\mapsto t^{k+1}\) is \((k+1)\)-Lipschitz on \([0,1]\),
\(\lvert\mu^{(k+1)/k}-\alpha^{k+1}\rvert\le(k+1)(\eta_k+k/p)^{1/k}\).

Finally, \(f^{(k)}=\binom a{k+1}+\sum_{j<k}\binom{a_j}{j+1}\). The at most \(k-1\) terms of the sum
are each at most \(p^k\), and \(\binom p{k+1}\ge(p/2)^{k+1}/(k+1)!\). So
\(\bigl\lvert f^{(k)}/\binom p{k+1}-\alpha^{k+1}\bigr\rvert
\le\eta_{k+1}+(k-1)(k+1)!\,2^{k+1}/p\).
#+end_proof

#+NAME: lem-rho
#+begin_lemma
\leanmark{\leanpartial}For \(2\le a\le b\) the region
\(R_{a,b}=\lbrace\mu\in\mathbb R^{\lbrace a,\dots,b\rbrace}:0\le\mu_a\le1,\ 0\le\mu_{k+1}\le g_k(\mu_k)
\ (a\le k<b)\rbrace\) lies in \(\lbrace1\ge\mu_a\ge\dots\ge\mu_b\ge0\rbrace\), and
\[
  \vol R_{a,b}=\rho(a,b)=\prod_{k=a}^{b-1}\frac{2k}{(b-k+1)(b+k)}
  =\frac{2^{b-a}\,(b-1)!\,(a+b-1)!}{(a-1)!\,(b-a+1)!\,(2b-1)!} .
\]
In particular \(R_n=R_{2,n}\) has \(\vol R_n=2^{n-2}(n+1)!/(2n-1)!\), and
\((n-1)!\vol R_n=(n+1)!/\bigl(2(2n-1)!!\bigr)\).
#+end_lemma

#+begin_proof
The inclusion holds because \(g_k(t)\le t\) on \([0,1]\). Put \(E_k=\sum_{j=k+1}^bj\). Let
\(F_b\equiv1\) and \(F_k(t)=\int_0^{g_k(t)}F_{k+1}(u)\,du\) for \(a\le k<b\). By downward induction,
\(F_k(t)=C_kt^{E_k/k}\) with \(C_b=1\) and \(C_k=C_{k+1}(k+1)/E_k\), because
\[
  \int_0^{t^{(k+1)/k}}C_{k+1}u^{E_{k+1}/(k+1)}\,du=C_{k+1}\frac{k+1}{E_{k+1}+k+1}\,t^{(E_{k+1}+k+1)/k}
\]
and \(E_{k+1}+k+1=E_k\). By Tonelli, \(\vol R_{a,b}=\int_0^1F_a=C_a\,a/(E_a+a)\), which is
\(\prod_{k=a}^bk/E_{k-1}\) with \(E_{a-1}=E_a+a\). Its last factor is \(b/E_{b-1}=1\), and
\(E_{k-1}=(b-k+1)(b+k)/2\). The closed forms follow from \(\prod_{k=a}^{b-1}(b-k+1)=(b-a+1)!\),
\(\prod_{k=a}^{b-1}(b+k)=(2b-1)!/(a+b-1)!\) and \((2n-1)!=2^{n-1}(n-1)!\,(2n-1)!!\).
#+end_proof

# 2026-11-14 (Jan, sweep item 9: "briefly explain your concept of classes and types"): what a class
# is, in the full/partial/zero terms of sec-lattice, and (after the definition of a type) what a
# chained type is, with its relation to staircase indices.
For \(\vek x\in\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\) call \((\tp(\vek x),\indeg(\vek x))\) its *class*. Since
\(x_1=p>0\) and \(\mu_1=1\), the classes are the pairs \((\tau,\iota)\) with \(1\le\tau\le n\) and
\(2\le\iota\le\tau+1\): the \(n(n+1)/2\) lattice points of a triangle. In the terms of the
discussion after Definition [[def-chained-points]], a point of class \((\tau,\iota)\) is full in its
own degrees below \(\iota\), partial in \(\iota,\dots,\tau\), and zero above \(\tau\). So the class is
the block of degrees in which the point is partial, and \([\iota-1,\tau]\) is its barycentric
support. For \(\iota=\tau+1\) the block is empty and the point is the vertex \(\tilde F_\tau\); the
class then records only the degree \(\iota\) at which the point drops from full to zero. Within a
class the partial coordinates \((\mu_\iota,\dots,\mu_\tau)\) range over a grid in a simplex, which is
what Lemma [[lem-anisotropic-count]] counts (proof of Lemma [[lem-kup-classes]]). The numbers of points of \(\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\) and of \(S_n^{(p)}\) in a
class, and their common scale, are
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:classcounts}
\begin{gathered}
  M^X_p(\tau,\iota)=\#\bigl\lbrace\vek x\in X_n^{(p)}:\bigl(\tp(\vek x),\indeg(\vek x)\bigr)=(\tau,\iota)\bigr\rbrace
  \qquad(X=\mathcal L,S),\\
  P_{\iota,\tau}(p)=\prod_{k=\iota}^\tau\binom pk ;
\end{gathered}
\end{equation}
#+end_export
and we put \(\rho(\tau+1,\tau)=1\).

#+NAME: lem-kup-classes
#+begin_lemma
\leanmark{\leannone}As \(p\to\infty\), \(M^{\mathcal L}_p(\tau,\iota)/P_{\iota,\tau}(p)\to1/(\tau-\iota+1)!\) and
\(M^S_p(\tau,\iota)/P_{\iota,\tau}(p)\to\rho(\iota,\tau)\).
#+end_lemma

#+begin_proof
For \(\iota=\tau+1\) the class is the vertex \(\tilde F_\tau\), an \(f\)-vector. Let
\(\iota\le\tau\). A point of the class has \(\mu_k=1\) for \(k<\iota\) and \(\mu_k=0\) for
\(k>\tau\), and is determined by its block \(\mu^\flat=(\mu_\iota,\dots,\mu_\tau)\). The block ranges
over \(\prod_{k=\iota}^\tau\binom pk^{-1}\mathbb Z\) intersected with
\(\Delta^\circ=\lbrace1>\mu_\iota\ge\dots\ge\mu_\tau>0\rbrace\), a set of volume
\(1/(\tau-\iota+1)!\) with null boundary. The first claim is therefore
Lemma [[lem-anisotropic-count]].

By the Kruskal--Katona theorem \eqref{eq:kk-cond}, and since \(\binom pk^{(k)}=\binom p{k+1}\), a
point of the class is an \(f\)-vector if and only if \(x_{k+1}\le x_k^{(k)}\) for \(\iota\le k<\tau\). For \(s>0\) let
\(R^{\pm s}=\lbrace\mu^\flat\in\Delta^\circ:\mu_{k+1}\le g_k(\mu_k)\pm s\ (\iota\le k<\tau)\rbrace\).
Once \(\varepsilon_k(p)\le s\) for these \(k\), Lemma [[lem-pseudo-power]] gives two inclusions:
- every \(f\)-vector of the class has \(\mu^\flat\in R^{+s}\);
- every lattice point of the class with \(\mu^\flat\in R^{-s}\) is an \(f\)-vector.

The boundaries of \(R^{\pm s}\) lie in \(\partial\Delta^\circ\) and in the graphs
\(\mu_{k+1}=g_k(\mu_k)\pm s\), which are null. So Lemma [[lem-anisotropic-count]] gives
\[
  \vol R^{-s}\le\liminf_{p\to\infty}\frac{M^S_p(\tau,\iota)}{P_{\iota,\tau}(p)}
  \le\limsup_{p\to\infty}\frac{M^S_p(\tau,\iota)}{P_{\iota,\tau}(p)}\le\vol R^{+s}.
\]
As \(s\downarrow0\), \(R^{+s}\) decreases to \(\Delta^\circ\cap R_{\iota,\tau}\). \(R^{-s}\)
increases to a set that differs from it by a subset of the graphs \(\mu_{k+1}=g_k(\mu_k)\). Both
volumes therefore tend to \(\rho(\iota,\tau)\) (Lemma [[lem-rho]]).
#+end_proof

#+ATTR_LATEX: :options [Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm-kupreyeva-limit}]
#+begin_proof
\(\lvert\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\rvert\) and \(\lvert S_n^{(p)}\rvert\) are the sums of
\(M^{\mathcal L}_p\) and \(M^S_p\) over the finitely many classes. For the class \((n,2)\),
Lemma [[lem-kup-classes]] gives \(P_{2,n}(p)\bigl(1/(n-1)!+o(1)\bigr)\) and
\(P_{2,n}(p)\bigl(\rho(2,n)+o(1)\bigr)\). Every other class has
\(\sum_{k=\iota}^\tau k<\sum_{k=2}^nk\), and at most \(\prod_{k=\iota}^\tau(\binom pk+1)\) points,
which is \(o(P_{2,n}(p))\). Lemma [[lem-rho]] gives \(\rho(2,n)=\vol R_n\) and the value of
\(q_n\).
#+end_proof

# 2026-11-10 (Jan, round 5): rem-kupreyeva-linusson moved here from Section sec-kupreyeva, next to the
# first proof of Theorem thm-kupreyeva-limit; sec-kupreyeva now points to it. Later the same evening
# (Jan: "use a 'second proof' environment", house style 93-CONVENTIONS/manuscript-style.md, "A second
# proof gets its own proof environment"): its first part is now this second proof, and the label
# rem-kupreyeva-linusson stays on the rate, which is what open problem 3 cites it for.
#+ATTR_LATEX: :options [Second proof of Theorem~\ref{thm-kupreyeva-limit}]
#+begin_proof
This proof takes over from the first only the count of lattice points,
\(\lvert\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\rvert\sim\prod_{k=2}^n\binom pk/(n-1)!\), and counts the \(f\)-vectors with
Linusson's formula instead of by volume. In his
notation \(\lvert S_n^{(p)}\rvert=F^p(n)-F^{p-1}(n)=\sum_{k=1}^nE^p(n,k)\), and \(E^p(n,k)\) is a
polynomial in \(p\) of degree \(M-\binom{k+1}2\), where \(M=\binom{n+1}2\). So
the term \(k=1\) dominates, and its leading coefficient is
#+begin_export latex
\begin{equation}\label{eq:linusson}
  \gamma_n=\frac1{(M-1)!}\prod_{i=1}^{n-2}\binom{M-\binom{i+1}2-1}{i}
\end{equation}
#+end_export
[cite:@Linusson1999 Theorem 3.3, and Proposition 2.8 with its proof]. Put
\(\alpha_i=M-\binom{i+1}2\). Then \(M-\binom{i+1}2-1-i=\alpha_{i+1}\), so the product telescopes to
\[
  \gamma_n=\frac{1}{(n-1)!\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\alpha_i\,\prod_{i=1}^{n-2}i!} .
\]
Since \(\prod_{i=1}^{n-1}\alpha_i=(n-1)!\,(2n)!/\bigl(2^{n-1}(n+1)!\bigr)\),
\[
  \gamma_n\,(n-1)!\prod_{k=2}^nk!=\frac{2^{n-1}\,n!\,(n+1)!}{(2n)!}=q_n .
\]
Since \(M-1=\sum_{k=2}^nk\) and \(\prod_{k=2}^n\binom pk\sim p^{M-1}/\prod_{k=2}^nk!\), this says
\(\lvert S_n^{(p)}\rvert\sim\gamma_n\prod_{k=2}^nk!\,\prod_{k=2}^n\binom pk\), with constant
\(\gamma_n\prod_{k=2}^nk!=q_n/(n-1)!=2^{n-2}(n+1)!/(2n-1)!\), the closed form in the theorem; and
\(q_n\) is the ratio of the leading coefficients of \(\lvert S_n^{(p)}\rvert\) and
\(\lvert\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\rvert\), which is \eqref{eq:qn}. That the constant is the volume of \(R_n\)
is Lemma [[lem-rho]], which this proof does not need.
#+end_proof

So Kupreyeva's observation was already implied by Linusson's leading coefficient, together with the
first-order count of lattice points. What the first proof, by volume, adds is the reason for the
value, and the extension to rank \(r\).

#+NAME: rem-kupreyeva-linusson
#+begin_remark
(/The rate./) Together, Linusson's polynomiality and the volume argument give the rate. \(\lvert S_n^{(p)}\rvert\)
is a polynomial in \(p\) [cite:@Linusson1999],
and \(\lvert\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\rvert\) is a quasi-polynomial in \(p\) for large \(p\), because the
vertices of \(\Koz^{(p)}(n)\) are polynomials in \(p\) [cite:@ChenLiSam2012 Theorem 1.4]. By
Theorem [[thm-kupreyeva-limit]] both have degree \(\sum_{k=2}^nk\) and constant leading coefficients in
ratio \(q_n\), so \(\lvert S_n^{(p)}\rvert-q_n\lvert\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\rvert\) has lower degree, and
\(\lvert S_n^{(p)}\rvert/\lvert\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\rvert=q_n+O(1/p)\).
#+end_remark

A *type* is a tuple \(((\tau_1,\iota_1),\dots,(\tau_r,\iota_r))\) of classes. It is *chained* if
\(\tau_{i-1}<\iota_i+\delta_i\) for \(2\le i\le r\), which is the condition \eqref{eq:chained} of
Definition [[def-chained-points]] read on classes.

In global degrees, summand \(i\) is partial in \(\iota_i+d_i,\dots,\tau_i+d_i\), and a type is
chained exactly when these blocks follow one another along the line of global degrees:
#+begin_export latex
\[
  \iota_1+d_1\;\le\;\tau_1+d_1+1\;\le\;\iota_2+d_2\;\le\;\tau_2+d_2+1\;\le\;\dots\;\le\;\iota_r+d_r\;\le\;\tau_r+d_r+1 ,
\]
#+end_export
the inequalities \(\iota_i\le\tau_i+1\) holding within a class and the others being the chaining
condition. So a chained type is a weakly increasing sequence of \(2r\) integers, the \(i\)-th pair in
\([d_i+2,d_i+n+1]\): disjoint blocks of partial degrees in the order of the summands, which is the
profile "zero, then at most one partial, then full" of the discussion after
Definition [[def-chained-points]], read degree by degree.
A staircase index \eqref{eq:staircase-index} is the case in which the blocks are contiguous and
cover \([2,N]\), with \(\iota_i+d_i=t_{i-1}+1\) and \(\tau_i+d_i=t_i\). When every gap is at most
\(n-1\), the types of largest weight in Lemma [[lem-kup-dominant]] below are of this kind.

#+NAME: lem-kup-truncation
#+begin_lemma
\leanmark{\leannone}Let \(p\ge n\).
1. Theorem [[thm-chained]] and Corollary [[cor-additivity]] hold for \(\Koz^{(p)}(n;\vek d)\).
2. A lattice point of \(\Koz^{(p)}(n;\vek d)\) lies in \(\FF^{(p)}(n;\vek d)\) if and only if every
   part of its canonical decomposition lies in \(S_n^{(p)}\). Padding the parts with zeros gives the
   canonical decomposition of the padded point in \(\Koz(p;\vek d)\).
3. Both \(\#\bigl(\Koz^{(p)}(n;\vek d)\cap\mathbb Z^{N+1}\bigr)\) and
   \(\lvert\FF^{(p)}(n;\vek d)\rvert\) are sums over the chained types, of
   \(\prod_iM^{\mathcal L}_p(\tau_i,\iota_i)\) and \(\prod_iM^S_p(\tau_i,\iota_i)\) respectively.
#+end_lemma

#+begin_proof
(1) The sequence \((\binom p2,\dots,\binom pn)\) is log-concave.

(2) "If" is trivial, and for \(n=p\) the statement is Corollary [[cor-membership]], so let \(n<p\).
Let \(\operatorname{pad}\) append zeros, from \(\mathbb R^{n+1}\) to \(\mathbb R^{p+1}\) and from
\(\mathbb R^{N+1}\) to \(\mathbb R^{p+d_r+1}\). It commutes with shifts and maps \(S_n^{(p)}\) into
\(S_p\). It maps a point of \(\Koz^{(p)}(n)\) to a point of \(\Koz(p)\) with the same \(\fl\) and
\(\tp\): the LYM chain is extended by zeros, and \(\mu_{n+1}=0<1\).

Let \(\vek x=\sum_i\shift^{d_i}\vek s_i\) with \(\vek s_i\in S_n^{(p)}\), and let
\((\vek p_1,\dots,\vek p_r)\) be its canonical decomposition. Then
\(\operatorname{pad}\vek x\in\FF(p;\vek d)\). The tuple \((\operatorname{pad}\vek p_i)_i\) is a
chained tuple of points of \(\Koz(p)\) with sum \(\operatorname{pad}\vek x\), so by
Theorem [[thm-chained]](2) it is the canonical decomposition of \(\operatorname{pad}\vek x\). By
Corollary [[cor-membership]], with \(p\) in place of \(n\), each \(\operatorname{pad}\vek p_i\) lies
in \(S_p\). It vanishes above degree \(n\), so \(\vek p_i\in S_n^{(p)}\).

(3) By (1), (2) and the uniqueness in Theorem [[thm-chained]], the two sets are in bijection with
the chained tuples of points of \(\mathcal L_n^{(p)}\) and of \(S_n^{(p)}\) respectively. Whether a
tuple is chained depends only on the classes of its parts. The second formula is also Part II's
[cite:@SnellmanArticle2 Theorem 60], obtained there from lex submodules.
#+end_proof

# 2026-11-13 (Jan, sweep item 2: rename the clashing letters): the weight of a type, D(t), is now
# omega(t), and D^* is omega^*; D_j, in the same statement, is the shift value of eq:aj.
#+NAME: lem-kup-dominant
#+begin_lemma
\leanmark{\leannone}For a type \(t\) put \(\omega(t)=\sum_i\sum_{k=\iota_i}^{\tau_i}k\). Among the chained types, \(\omega(t)\) is
largest exactly for the following ones. For each \(j\), the \(m_j\) summands with shift \(D_j\) \eqref{eq:aj}
have, in their order, \((\fl,\tp)=(s_{l-1},s_l)\) for \(l=1,\dots,m_j\), where
\(a_j-1=s_0\le s_1\le\dots\le s_{m_j}=n\). The multiset of the degrees \(k\) with
\(\iota_i\le k\le\tau_i\) is the same for all of them.
#+end_lemma

#+begin_proof
Let \(B_i=[\iota_i+d_i,\tau_i+d_i]\) be the global degrees in which summand \(i\) is partial, as
above. Chaining \eqref{eq:chained} gives \(\tau_{i-1}+d_{i-1}\le\iota_i-1+d_i\le\tau_i+d_i\), so the \(B_i\) are
disjoint and increasing. For \(g\in B_i\), \(2\le g-d_i\le n\). So \(g\) lies in
\(U=\bigcup_i[d_i+2,d_i+n]\), and \(d_i\ge d_*(g):=\min\lbrace d_l:d_l\ge g-n\rbrace\). Hence
\[
  \omega(t)=\sum_i\sum_{g\in B_i}(g-d_i)\le\sum_{g\in U}\bigl(g-d_*(g)\bigr),
\]
with equality if and only if every \(g\in U\) lies in a block \(B_i\) with \(d_i=d_*(g)\). The
\(g\in U\) with \(d_*(g)=D_j\) form the interval \([D_j+a_j,D_j+n]\). So equality means that the
own-degree blocks \((\fl,\tp]\) of the summands with shift \(D_j\) partition \((a_j-1,n]\).

Write \((f_l,t_l)\) for \((\fl,\tp)\) of the \(l\)-th summand with shift \(D_j\). Chaining at gap
\(0\) and \(\fl\le\tp\) give \(f_1\le t_1\le f_2\le\dots\le t_{m_j}\). The partition then forces
\(f_1\le a_j-1\) and \(t_{m_j}=n\). Conversely \(f_1\ge a_j-1\): \(f_1\ge1\) always, and for
\(j\ge2\), chaining with the last summand of shift \(D_{j-1}\), whose \(\tp\) is \(n\), gives
\(n+D_{j-1}\le f_1+D_j\). So \(f_1=a_j-1\), and the partition forces \(f_l=t_{l-1}\).

Every such type is chained, the inequality between groups being \(n<a_j+D_j-D_{j-1}\). The
multiset is \(\lbrace g-d_*(g):g\in U\rbrace\).
#+end_proof

#+ATTR_LATEX: :options [Proof of Theorem~\ref{thm-kupreyeva-rank-r}]
#+begin_proof
Let \(\omega^*\) be the largest \(\omega(t)\) over chained types, and \(T^*\) the set of types attaining it.
For \(p\ge2n\) the counts \eqref{eq:classcounts} satisfy
\(M^X_p(\tau,\iota)\le\prod_{k=\iota}^\tau(\binom pk+1)\le2^nP_{\iota,\tau}(p)\), and
\(P_{\iota,\tau}(p)/p^{\,\iota+\dots+\tau}\to w(\iota,\tau)=\prod_{k=\iota}^\tau1/k!\). By
Lemmas [[lem-kup-truncation]](3) and [[lem-kup-classes]], \(p^{-\omega^*}\) times the number of lattice
points tends to
\[
  \sum_{t\in T^*}\prod_iw(\iota_i,\tau_i)/(\tau_i-\iota_i+1)!
\]
and \(p^{-\omega^*}\lvert\FF^{(p)}(n;\vek d)\rvert\) tends to
\[
  \sum_{t\in T^*}\prod_iw(\iota_i,\tau_i)\,\rho(\iota_i,\tau_i);
\]
the types outside \(T^*\) contribute \(O(p^{\omega^*-1})\). By Lemma [[lem-kup-dominant]],
\(\prod_iw(\iota_i,\tau_i)\) is the same for all \(t\in T^*\), and \(T^*\) is a product over \(j\)
of the sets of sequences \(s_0\le\dots\le s_{m_j}\). So the limit of the fill rate is
\[
  \prod_j\frac{\sum_{\vek s}\prod_l\rho(s_{l-1}+1,s_l)}{\sum_{\vek s}\prod_l1/(s_l-s_{l-1})!} .
\]
The denominator is \(m_j^{\ell_j}/\ell_j!\) by the multinomial theorem.

(1) All \(m_j=1\), and \(\ell_j!\,\rho(n-\ell_j+1,n)=q(n,\ell_j)\) with \(\ell_j=\min(\delta_j,n-1)\)
and \(\ell_1=n-1\), by Lemma [[lem-rho]].

# 2026-11-13 (sweep, found by the inline-definition inventory): "s = 1" was a slip for g = 1, the
# number of distinct shifts in eq:aj.
(2) Here \(g=1\), \(a_1=2\) and \(\vol(r\Delta_n)=r^{n-1}/(n-1)!\). It remains to show
\(\vol(R_n+\dots+R_n)=\Phi_n(2;r)\). Let \(G_k(v)=\lfloor v\rfloor+g_k(v-\lfloor v\rfloor)\) for
\(0\le v<r\), \(G_k(r)=r\), and
\(Z=\lbrace\nu:0\le\nu_2\le r,\ 0\le\nu_{k+1}\le G_k(\nu_k)\ (2\le k<n)\rbrace\).
- If \(\nu=\sum_j\mu^{(j)}\) with \(\mu^{(j)}\in R_n\), then
  \(\nu_{k+1}\le\sum_jg_k(\mu^{(j)}_k)\le G_k(\nu_k)\). The last step holds because
  \(\sum_jg_k(t_j)\) is convex on \(\lbrace t\in[0,1]^r:\sum_jt_j=\nu_k\rbrace\), hence maximal at a
  vertex, where all \(t_j\) but at most one are \(0\) or \(1\).
- Conversely, for \(\nu\in Z\) the parts
  \(\mu^{(i)}_k=\min\bigl(1,\max(0,\nu_k-r+i)\bigr)\) of its canonical decomposition sum to \(\nu\)
  and lie in \(R_n\): when \(r-i<\nu_k<r-i+1\), the bound \(\nu_{k+1}\le G_k(\nu_k)\) says exactly
  \(\mu^{(i)}_{k+1}\le g_k(\mu^{(i)}_k)\); when \(\nu_k\ge r-i+1\), \(\mu^{(i)}_k=1\); and when
  \(\nu_k\le r-i\), \(\mu^{(i)}_{k+1}=0\), because \(\nu_{k+1}\le G_k(\nu_k)\le\nu_k\).
- So \(Z=R_n+\dots+R_n\). Now \(Z\subseteq r\Delta_n\), and on the interior of the cell
  \(C_{\vek s}\) of Theorem [[thm-staircase]] (all-ones legs, \(\vek d=0^r\)), \(Z\) is a translate
  of \(\prod_iR^\circ_{s_{i-1}+1,s_i}\). Inside a block the condition reads
  \(\mu_{k+1}\le g_k(\mu_k)\), and across blocks it holds automatically.
- Since the cells tile \(r\Delta_n\), \(\vol Z=\Phi_n(2;r)\). For \(n=3\),
  \(\Phi_3(2;r)=2r/5+\binom r2\).
#+end_proof

This paper owes its lattice-point question to Aliaksandra Kupreyeva's Bachelor's thesis
[cite:@Kupreyeva2019], written under the author's supervision at Linköping University, where the fraction of
lattice points of the Kozlov simplex that are \(f\)-vectors, its volume and its centroid were first
studied. Luca Amata and Marilena Crupi's Macaulay2 package [cite:@AmataCrupi2021] was used
throughout.

Claude (Anthropic) assisted substantially with this paper; what that amounted to is set out in the
disclosure section below.

The computations used SageMath [cite:@SageMath], Normaliz [cite:@BrunsIchim2010], Macaulay2
[cite:@Macaulay2] and the =barvinok= library [cite:@Verdoolaege2007]; the formal verification used
Lean 4 [cite:@deMouraUllrich2021] and Mathlib [cite:@Mathlib2020].

This manuscript was produced with substantial assistance from a large language model, Claude
(Anthropic), and the extent of it is set out here rather than left to be inferred. All of it was
done at the author's request and under his direction. Where a result is said below to have been
reviewed, the review was an adversarial one by a second, separately prompted instance of the model
(Section [[#sec-methods]]); none was by a human colleague.

- *Sections [[#sec-lattice]], [[#sec-volume]], [[#sec-ehrhart]].* The lecture hall identification, the
  canonical chained decomposition at positive gaps (Theorem [[thm-chained]]) and its corollaries, the
  staircase tiling (Theorem [[thm-staircase]]), the transfer-matrix Ehrhart formulas and the comparison
  with \(\lvert\FF\rvert\), were found and proved by the model, and all tables were computed by it;
  the literature on lecture hall polytopes was located by it. So were the lattice-path reading of
  staircase indices and its identification at equal shifts with the staircase triangulation
  (Example [[ex-staircase-paths]], Remark [[rem-staircase-cayley]], Figure \ref{fig:staircase-paths}),
  and the sources for the latter.
- *Sections [[#sec-centroid]] and [[#sec-moments]].* The involution \(\beta\), Theorems
  [[thm-beta-lattice]], [[thm-alexander-core]] and [[thm-n3-equal-shifts]], and all centroid and moment
  computations, were found and proved, or computed, by the model, and reviewed. So was the proof of
  Theorem [[thm-centroid-dilation]], in November 2026; it had been stated as a conjecture in earlier
  drafts. So were Remark [[rem-weak-convergence]] and the exact centroids of
  Proposition [[prop-dilates]], the latter stated as an observation in earlier drafts.
- *Corollaries [[cor-zero-shift]] and [[cor-deficiency]].* The statement about \(a(r)\) is Part II's; the
  lattice-point count, the comparison and the closed forms at \(n=4,5\) are new here, and were
  produced by the model.
- *Kupreyeva's limit (Section [[#sec-kupreyeva]], Appendix [[#sec-app-kupreyeva]]).* The
  identification of Kupreyeva's \(q_n\) as a volume ratio, the proof of her observation
  (Theorem [[thm-kupreyeva-limit]]), the second proof from Linusson's leading coefficient and the rate
  it gives (Appendix [[#sec-app-kupreyeva]], Remark [[rem-kupreyeva-linusson]]), and the limit at every rank
  (Theorem [[thm-kupreyeva-rank-r]]) with its proof, were found by the model in November 2026, and
  reviewed.
- *Theorem [[thm-codegree]], Corollary [[cor-gorenstein]] and Proposition [[prop-quadratic-coeff]].*
  Proved by the model in November 2026, from sketches in its own working notes; in the same check it
  found that the codegree criterion conjectured in an earlier draft fails when a gap of at least
  \(n-1\) follows an unrepeated value (Example [[ex-codegree-product]]). Reviewed. The direct proof of
  Lemma [[lem-interior-points]](2) was written by the model later, when the lemma was moved ahead of
  Proposition [[prop-hstar-props]], and has been checked by computation only.
- *Figures, code, formalisation.* All figures, all SageMath, Python and Normaliz code, and the Lean
  formalisation described in Section [[#sec-methods]] were written by the model.
- *Drafting.* This draft was written by the model from the working notes, with the author directing
  the scope and the organisation of the series.

Every numerical claim in the paper was checked by exact computation along a route independent of the
formula being tested, in most cases by two independently written programs, and the reference list has
been checked against primary sources, except for the paper of Savage and Schuster, whose theorem is
cited in the form quoted by two other sources. The author has verified the results to the best of his
ability, made the final edits, and assumes full responsibility for any errors, mistakes or gaps that
